RAY: I have an appointment. I come every spring. To see if it's come down.

ROSIE: Has it?

RAY: Not yet.

He eats the cracker, neatly, catching the crumbs in his palm. Then he tilts the packet towards her, the way anyone offers anyone a biscuit.

RAY: Want one? Don't knock it.

ROSIE: You're all right. Making weight.

RAY: So I hear.

He doesn't put the card away. Rosie looks at the card, and at him, and at the lard on the knife.

RECEPTIONIST: *(from the desk)* Mr Ambrose? Dr Osei will see you now.

RAY: In a minute.

Rosie goes.

### Scene 21. Harley Street, outside the clinic, Saturday, 10:25.

Bright, cold. Taxis. Rosie on the clinic steps, the leaflet in her pocket, saying it to herself as though saying it might make it smaller.

ROSIE: A hundred and eighty thousand. Seventy per cent.

Across the road, on the corner, a jeweller's: a narrow shopfront, one ring in the window on black velvet. Beside it, a bus shelter with a poster in it: a young soldier in fatigues, well fed and smiling, walking unmasked down a plague ward between the beds, nurses behind him in full hoods and visors. IMMUNE. FED. NEEDED. JOIN THE FEVER COMPANY.

At the kerb between the shop and the shelter stands a plain grey van with its back doors open. Two GUARDS come out of the jeweller's carrying a steel case between them and lock it into the van.

Rosie watches the case all the way in. She doesn't look at the poster. The camera holds all three in one frame: the girl on the steps, the van, the soldier on the ward.

Behind her, the clinic door opens. Ray, in his big coat, on the top step.

RAY: Tell your uncle it's seventy per cent.

She turns.

RAY: They don't tell you how.

He goes back in. The door closes on the brass plate.

Rosie looks down the street. The grey van's brake lights at the corner, turning left. She pulls up her collar and walks after it, quick, and her lips are moving. She is saying its number plate over to herself, the way Tess keeps a count.

**END OF EPISODE 2**

---

## Plants and payoffs

**Planted here, for later episodes**

- **Kieran's log (scenes 3, 13).** The Twitch ticket (04:17, 0.31 kg, empty plate), found because the radio's "next-door event" sends him to the night's tickets before school instead of after, and taped in under the poster his school printed ("found by weighing, never by looking"; "Sixty years we pointed dishes the wrong way"; "Miss says you can't look at them, you can only weigh them"). The test weight at 20.00 every night at nine, read off the ticket because the display is dark: "Same as every day. That's the point." In 4 it reads 19.78 and Peg calls it chipped; in 9 the log proves the April step. The Twitch itself stays a wonder.
- **Peg's faith in the little bridge:** "Bathroom scale's a liar. On the bridge." And her hatred of wasting roll ("that's my roll"; "forty tickets of a picture"), which becomes "Stop wasting roll" in 4. The bridge lies from 4 April (4, 9).
- **43.1, cheered, chalked and starred**, with a 43.10 ticket on the audit roll (the twist in 3; the rolls in 10).
- **Peg's copy theory and her home-made belief about cargo.** "Bank hung a copy the wrong way round... A picture doesn't [face home]. It's got nothing to face with. You hold it loose, you let it roll about." Liam asks the one question the scene needs — "Won't she come back with it? Whichever way it goes?" — and Peg waves it away with the family's oldest certainty: "Not if she faces home." The rule the family half knows (everything in you counts, breakfast too; the painting came back with Rosie in 1) is on the table for one beat, and the belief answers it. It is Peg's own idea, made up at the table because she needs a sale; the table laughs and nobody tests it. It keeps "copy or original" open until 3, where Peg concedes "That picture was never a copy" and Ray's "so did whoever carried it" breaks the belief. Tess shows she has learned in 4 ("Then you'd be eating it"); Kieran writes "Food turns too" in 9. *Continuity:* nobody in the family states the cargo rule before 3. Episode 1's "Means we came back straight" about the burned tissue is as close as they come, and nobody puts the two together.
- **Seven three-second hops, all failed.** Each gets "Straight?" "Straight." and exactly one glance from Rosie at the catalogue cutting by the stove (re-read in 3; run through the drift table in 6). Kieran asks "Straight?" once, man to man. At 1 in 5,000 a hop, seven failures are what the table predicts.
- **Tess's rule, "And a meal between every one."** It spaces the hops at mealtimes (that's why there are only seven), and she watches Rosie eat every forkful: dinner before hop two, toast before hops four and six. On re-watch the meals did nothing, like the glucose sweet ("Better. See?"), which sets up the gel that does nothing in 3.
- **The false alarm (hop four).** Headlights on the blind and the printer chattering mid-hop: nobody's due, and the whole room goes to the window, remembering that the printer announced Ray's car in 1. It is a real lorry the family can see, not an unseen visitor. Peg: "If it's ever that copper, it's a picture of a lady off Rayleigh boot fair." Peg asks "Straight?" without turning round, and nobody sees Rosie's glance. It puts the police in the room for a first-time viewer without giving Rosie away.
- **Rosie's hiding, kept to the plan's quiet tells:** the seven glances; the roll "joke" (Tess's alarm is about her certified roll and Pinnock, not about Rosie); the sweet; "Making weight" to Ray; "She likes it that way"; Sixpence growling after hop one ("Still jealous, is he?"). The call to Obverse is made openly, on the step, as the birthday joke promised in 1, and Terry's "Tell them I'm shy" pays off at the clinic's front desk. Peg's "Don't you sign nothing" lets the family's rules against clinics and paper sit over the joke without anyone stopping it (3: "We don't go to them").
- **Tess on the step:** "Nan tells it wrong. She says I was proud." / "I was hungry." Her wound, which Rosie is living; followed at once by the Ahern shed cheering a thin boy (6: "I gave you nine"; 10).
- **The doom's first link, as a boast:** "I know what slows a crosser down"; the insurers' thicker floors; the order book trebled; Peg's "Every job just got a second longer. Good for us both ways"; Tess's "I'll find it" (3, 7). Danny's new orders fill the yard, which is why Thursday has only two hops.
- **Danny's City vault — Mynors, named at the table in scene 6 (so Tess hears it), and told again unnamed at the bank in scene 5.** It is Aoife's bank. Maggie names it in 7; the Marlow vans the Aherns saw in 4 were this crew.
- **Liam:** "Thickest at the back. That's where they'd come in, off the lane" (needed in 3, where Marrable Street is thickest on the mews side); praised for his head, not his size; alone on the bathroom scale in the dark, wanting to know his number (the diet starts only when Rosie coaches him in 4, and so does the fry-up he slips to Sixpence); Peg's "Not with that breakfast in you, love," and he eats every bite, which rhymes with the fry-up he eats in 5; and his question at the table, the right one, answered away — the family's habit of not asking, shown in one exchange.
- **The Laundry:** Dilys's Tuesday van, "Your whites, Terry" / "Bless you, Dilys" as in 1 and 5, batch codes on the lids, the thin envelope counted out of the float tin (3: the float tin upended, empty), "He'll want it, Peg. He always does." / "He'll get it." The supplier behaves like a hard man who wants paying, which is Ray's cover (8: the money he never touched).
- **Con** on the phone: dripping, "a drop of gin", a faint old voice from somewhere with a river in it; Peg doesn't know who he is (4, 5).
- **The shed nobody can place:** the steelyard with a lad standing on its board, "Fifty-nine!", the girl with the saucepan, the woman with the tide book who doesn't cheer, a baby crying upstairs. Episode 4 names them: Joe, Declan, Siobhan, Maggie, Cian.
- **Immunity and the road not taken:** "Not had a cold since '21. Only perk"; the Fever Company poster in the bus shelter, in the same frame as the jeweller's van (8, epilogue). Rosie's surgeon videos, "Never. Not once. Four thousand of them", and "Marlows don't do paper" (6, epilogue).
- **Obverse:** "PATIENTS MUST NEVER TOUCH STAFF IN TRANSIT" and the receptionist's artificial left hand (4: "Girl at the clinic's is plastic"; 6: Danny's fingers). The 2009 menu at £800 and "OBVERSE TRIPLES ON ARMY DEAL" (the shares halve in the epilogue). Osei cured there; "That's what the fee pays for" (6). "A clinician looks in on him in transit, a second or two at a time": on re-watch in 6, Obverse keeps its own staff's crossings short because it knows the drift, and the sign guards against a grip. Rosie's "We feed our own" to Osei (8: "We never fed our own"). Osei's "The Register won't ask him anything... Everybody says that. Until they're hungry" (8).
- **The records room door (6):** Rosie asks for the toilet and passes a steel door marked RECORDS. STAFF ONLY, keypad beside the handle. Her recount in 6 — "I asked for the toilet" — is now shown first, and Danny's "Keypad doesn't matter" answers a door the audience has seen.
- **Ray:** the Register card shown without shame, lard for breakfast, "I come every spring", "So I hear" (3, 8, 10); "They don't tell you how" (6). Whether he followed Rosie is left open.
- **The Harley Street jeweller's van.** Rosie goes after it with its number plate in her head, the family's way of keeping a number (3: she follows it to Marrable Street).
- **The painting now lives in Peg's safe.** Danny and Rosie reach it through the door with a sent hand and post it back after every hop; nobody uses the key (5: the bullet on the painting, and Peg opening the safe with her key). Not new: the safe and the sent hand were first shown in 1. Here they become routine.
- **Also:** Peg's finger-prick and the meter among the fish knives (3); Tess at Frank's photo, and Peg's "There's nothing left when the black takes you. You know that" (9, 10). Texture only, owing nothing: the hand painted out under the woman's hand; Peg's man Stan; the tipper driver asleep on his wheel.

**Paid off here, from episode 1**

- Ray's "It's the original" and Peg's "Then the bank hung a copy" become Peg's plan, still on her copy theory, and a buyer who wants the painting to match its catalogue.
- "That's Terry's year at the Laundry" becomes the week's stakes.
- Rosie's "I'll price it for your birthday" and Terry's "Buy me two. One for Christmas" become the call on the step ("Get me the Christmas one and all") and her visit to the clinic.
- Ray's vodka and lard at his desk ("This is it"), and Terry's "Fat and spirits. It's all that sticks": Ray is turned.
- The TV surgeon's "Never turned" becomes Rosie's videos and Tess's three seconds ("That's a surgeon's hop").
- The BBC voice that reported the silent merger reports the Twitch.
- Kieran's nine o'clock weighing, and his habit of sorting the night's tickets, find the 04:17 ticket.
- The hatch-down, door-bolted breakfast of 1 is the family's routine; the printer that announced Ray in 1 sets off the false alarm.
- 43.6 at the birthday becomes 43.1, cheered and starred. Terry's "The black never gave me back" from 1 comes back, said to a pan, and Danny cuts it off ("It's a picture, Tel") because the family has heard it before. Sixpence still growls at Rosie, and Danny still calls it jealousy.
- Tess's "It was a different week" becomes "I was hungry".
- Ray's "I met what was left of him" is carried on: Tess asks Peg, and Peg shuts it down.

**Numbers used.** Rosie 43.1 on the bathroom scale and 43.10 on the little bridge. Shaun 8.4 seconds. The test weight 20.00. The Twitch at 04:17, 0.31 kg. Seven hops of 3 seconds each. "Fifty-nine!" for Joe. £180,000 and seventy per cent; 2009 and £800. Terry turned in '21; Danny's vault in '24. The surgeon's four thousand. No new canonical numbers ("a hundred goes" and "forty tickets" are only talk).

**Closed:** whether hops can fix the painting (not these ones); what Ray eats. **Opened:** what Restoration's method is; what the Twitch was (a wonder); what Ray knows; who the family across the river is.


# Episode 3: Making Weight

*Rosie's weight keeps falling, her family keeps cheering, and the detective starts adding seconds to her jobs.*

---

## Cold open

### Scene 1. The weighbridge office, Grays, Friday, a minute to midnight.

Job night. The lorries are parked up in a row outside, and the yard lights lay orange bars through the blinds. Inside it's warm and loud and smells of bacon.

TERRY (66) wraps bacon sandwiches in foil for the van crew, in his yellow gloves. DANNY (41) and LIAM (19) are in dark work clothes. SHAUN (26), who isn't on tonight, sits on the bottom stair with a mug of tea. KIERAN (12) has been allowed up for it, in pyjamas under his school coat. SIXPENCE, the old lurcher, lies in his basket by the stove. TESS (38) stands by the ticket printer with the pencil behind her ear. PEG (72) has her apron on over her nightie and a stick of chalk in her hand.

ROSIE (22) steps onto the little bridge in what she'll cross in: black jersey, black leggings, thick socks, and her rig, the padded belt, the collar with its brass disc, the wrist unit. Nothing else.

At night the displays are dark, so the printer does the telling. It chatters. Peg tears off the ticket and holds it under the lamp, glasses on the end of her nose.

PEG: Forty-one eight.

The room goes up. Danny bangs the table with the flat of his hand. Shaun whistles through his fingers. Kieran bounces on the settle. Liam claps his big hands. Rosie bows from the waist like a conductor.

Peg chalks it at the foot of Rosie's column on the board: 44.0, 43.6, 43.1, and now 41.8. Beside it she draws the biggest star that board has ever had.

PEG: That's a Marlow.

Rosie comes up out of her bow. For half a second her palm goes flat on the dresser top, like someone on a ferry when the deck shifts. Then she's grinning again, and bowing again, and the room is still clapping.

**MAIN TITLE: SECONDS**

---

## Act one: The longest job

### Scene 2. Harley Street, Saturday morning, two weeks earlier.

Caption: TWO WEEKS EARLIER.

Where we left her: Rosie walking fast down Harley Street after the jeweller's grey van, collar up, saying its number plate over under her breath. At the corner the lights change and the van gets away. She stands on the kerb and watches it go, her lips still moving on the number.

**Monday, 6 p.m.** Closing time. Rosie's own white van is parked across from the jeweller's with its engine off. The guards carry out the steel case. The grey van pulls away, and three cars back, so does Rosie.

Through the City. Into a narrow street of diamond dealers: shutters, buzzers, brass plates, men in black coats with cases chained to their wrists. A street sign: MARRABLE STREET. The guards carry the case down iron steps to a door under the dealers' exchange. Next door stands an empty shop, TO LET, with a grating over its basement window.

**Tuesday night.** The cobbled mews behind Marrable Street, one lamp. Rosie sits in her van with the engine off, watching the back of the exchange: a blind brick wall, and low down, just above the cobbles, a row of small barred windows. She drinks from a bottle of water. Her breath fogs the windscreen, and she wipes a hole in it with her sleeve.

Behind the barred windows a torch goes by, window to window, and stops at the last one. It stays there. After a while it goes out. Rosie's lips move, fixing it.

She takes a paper out of her coat pocket, folded small and gone soft at the creases, and opens it on the steering wheel: OBVERSE. RESTORATION. £180,000. She looks at it for a long moment. Then she folds it up along its creases, puts it back in her pocket, and sits on in the dark.

### Scene 3. The weighbridge office, Wednesday, 6 p.m.

Tea. Sausage and mash. The gate is shut and the hatch is down. Terry is at the stove in his gloves, with his blue enamel pot on the back ring. The whole firm is at the table: Peg, Danny, Tess, Liam, Shaun, Kieran with his homework.

Peg upends the float tin over the counter. Coins, a button, and a raffle ticket.

PEG: That's the float. Dilys comes Tuesday.

DANNY: Order book's full, Mum.

PEG: Order book's paper. Concrete money goes in the bank, where the taxman can see it. Dilys wants notes. *(She sweeps the coins back in.)* Concrete pays the bills. Concrete doesn't pay the Laundry.

The back door bangs. Rosie comes in on a gust of cold air, drops into her chair and steals a bit of fried onion off Liam's plate.

ROSIE: I've got one.

Forks stop. She tells it like a story at a bar, with her hands.

ROSIE: Jeweller's on Harley Street. Grey van, one steel case. I've had it two nights running. Every night the shop shuts, the stones go to bed in a diamond room. Weekends they sleep there Friday to Monday. One old boy on nights, sits by the door, light out by two. One tray. Two kilo, tray and all.

DANNY: In where?

ROSIE: Marrable Street.

Peg's knife stops on her plate. She looks up at Frank's photo on the wall, the big man laughing. The table goes quiet.

PEG: *(to Rosie)* Your grandad's street.

ROSIE: I know.

PEG: Last job he ever did. *(beat)* He'd have liked that.

KIERAN: Is that where the black took him?

PEG: Fifteen stone, he was. *(She taps the painted rule at the top of the board with her fork.)* That's why we make weight.

Tess looks down at her plate.

DANNY: Under the exchange? Iron steps, door with no handle?

ROSIE: That's the one.

A slow grin spreads over Danny's face.

DANNY: I poured that. Twenty-three. Diamond room, floor and back wall in one go. Best floor I ever did.

LIAM: Thickest at the back wall.

Everyone looks at him.

LIAM: I barrowed every bead. They wanted it thick on the mews side, 'cause that's where you'd come from.

DANNY: He did an' all. All-night pour.

Liam's ears go pink.

TESS: And the side?

LIAM: Empty shop next door. Party wall's old brick with one skin of Mix on it. They ran out of money.

Tess's lips move. The table waits; they all know this face.

TESS: From next door, then. Two trips, under three each. Half the parcels, home, the other half. The wall never feels her long enough to flood.

LIAM: That's what I said at Mayfair.

PEG: Never go back twice.

DANNY and ROSIE *(with her, the old chorus)*: Never go back twice.

PEG: Your dad's mate went in twice on the Old Street job. Second time, there was a copper stood in the room. Your dad never said his name again. *(to Tess)* One trip. The whole tray.

TESS: One trip, the wall floods and she's racing the beads. Mayfair was half a second.

PEG: And she came home with it.

ROSIE: I'll do it in one.

PEG: There.

Tess looks at Rosie properly: the shadows under her eyes, her wrists in Danny's old sleeves.

TESS: Then she makes weight.

ROSIE: I always make weight.

KIERAN: Lightest goes longest!

The table laughs. Tess has lost, and she knows it.

DANNY: Want it done before the Aherns smell it. They were working Mayfair at Christmas.

PEG: Declan Ahern learnt everything he knows at this table. Frank taught him.

SHAUN: And he never said ta.

ROSIE: *(through a mouthful of mash)* That tray's Terry's year. And his cure after, if you'd let him.

PEG: We don't go to them.

ROSIE: I priced it, for his birthday. Hundred and eighty thousand. Seventy per cent.

At the stove, Terry's spoon stops in his pot. He doesn't turn round.

PEG: Then it's his year. *(to Tess)* When?

TESS: Friday week. I want to walk it.

---

## Act two: The pursuer

### Scene 4. Marlow Aggregates, the batching plant cabin, the following Wednesday, 4 p.m.

A tin hut at the foot of the concrete plant, shaking gently as the drum turns overhead. The BATCHER, an old boy in a flat cap who has worked the controls since Frank's day, watches a thin man in an overcoat that is too big for him turn the pages of a lever-arch file on the desk. MARLOW MIX: POUR CONTRACTS.

DS RAY AMBROSE (54) stops at a page. MARRABLE STREET. VAULT FLOOR AND REAR WALL. HIGH-DENSITY BEAD. 2023. Signed with a big, looping D. MARLOW.

He copies it into a notebook.

BATCHER: Nothing to hide in concrete, Mr Marlow says. Proud of it.

RAY: He should be. *(He closes the file.)* Where would I find Miss Marlow? The older one.

BATCHER: Pit. With the boy.

### Scene 5. The flooded gravel pit, Wednesday, 4:30 p.m.

The water is cold pewter under a white sky. Kieran, in a wetsuit and swimming goggles painted black, drops off the edge of the pontoon and is gone. Tess kneels at the edge with her eyes on nothing and her lips moving.

Ray comes down the haul road and sits on an upturned drum at the end of the pontoon. Tess doesn't look round.

RAY: He kicks down.

TESS: Everyone kicks down.

Kieran breaks the surface, gasping, and pushes up his goggles.

TESS: Breathe out. Follow your bubbles. Again.

He goes under. Tess's lips start again.

RAY: The Mayfair wall kept a log. Every body in the black within ten metres, with its weight and its time. At twelve minutes past three it logged two.

TESS: Two what?

RAY: Bodies. Hers. And one more.

Tess's count falters for a second, then picks up.

TESS: Whose?

RAY: A wall gives you a weight, not a name.

TESS: What weight?

RAY: I'll keep that.

He takes a green card out of his inside pocket and lays it on the planks between them, face up, the way you'd lay down a winning hand. His photo. HANDEDNESS REGISTER. CLAIMANT. Tess looks at it for a long moment.

TESS: You're turned.

RAY: Since 2015. Seven seconds, with a kid under my arm. *(He takes out a packet of crackers and a pocket knife, and spreads lard on one.)* It's all that sticks.

Tess looks at the white fat on his knife, and for a moment says nothing.

TESS: Why show me?

RAY: *(eating, catching the crumbs in his palm)* There's a way to kill a crosser that takes six months and looks like a diet. How it's done, I don't know yet. *(He taps the card.)* What it looks like, I know.

TESS: You face home, you come back straight.

Ray doesn't argue. He eats his cracker. Kieran surfaces, gulps, "Again," and goes under.

TESS: Then look at her. You've got a rig.

RAY: I could. Two seconds beside her in the black and I'd see which side her heart's on. The law calls that a search. *(He brushes crumbs off his coat.)* We had a man once with a ring in his stomach. We could see it plain as day. Couldn't see why he'd swallowed it. Ten years of courts. *(beat)* So I wait till she comes to me hungry.

He stands. He looks out over the water at the conveyor gantry, black against the sky.

RAY: There's a car outside an empty shop on Marrable Street. It'll be there all week.

Tess goes very still.

TESS: Why would I care?

RAY: Had a call.

TESS: Who from?

RAY: Didn't leave a name. *(beat)* First job I ever caught was on Marrable Street.

TESS: I know what you caught.

She opens her mouth to ask the next thing. She doesn't ask it. Ray waits, and when nothing comes, he goes back up the haul road.

Kieran bursts up out of the water and tears off his goggles.

KIERAN: How long was I?

Tess looks at him. Her lips have stopped. There's no number on them.

At the top of the haul road Ray opens his car door, then turns.

RAY: Miss Marlow. *(She looks up.)* If the painting turned, so did whoever carried it.

He gets in and drives away. Across the yard, the big bridge prints the weight of his car.

KIERAN: Mum? How long?

TESS: *(too late)* Long. Good.

### Scene 6. The weighbridge office, Wednesday, 7 p.m.

Tea. Liver and onions, mash, gravy. Tess comes in late from the pit with Kieran's wet towel over her shoulder and takes the chair where she can see Rosie.

Rosie eats. She clears her plate, wipes the gravy up with bread, and holds the plate out to Peg for more. Peg piles it, delighted.

PEG: Look at her. Clears two plates and never puts a gram on.

DANNY: Burns it off in the black.

Rosie grins with her mouth full and eats the second plate too, every bite, steadily, the way you'd do a job.

Tess watches it go down. At the stove end Terry spoons from his white tub, as he always does, and never from theirs. Tess looks from Terry to Rosie's empty plate. Then she picks up her own fork.

### Scene 7. Marrable Street, Thursday, 11 p.m.

Tess sits in her own car at the dark end of the street with the engine off. The dealers' shutters are down. Across from the empty shop, TO LET, sits a grey saloon with a man in it and a coffee on the dashboard. He isn't hiding. He's there to be seen.

At the bus stop a thin lad of seventeen or so, in a hoodie, lets one bus go, then another.

Tess gets out and walks round the block into the mews, hands in her pockets, as if she lives there. She stops on the cobbles over the back wall of the exchange. She paces it to the wall and back, and down to where the diamond room must be beneath her feet. Her lips move the whole time.

Back in the car, she sits with both hands on the wheel.

TESS: *(to nobody)* Ten.

### Scene 8. The weighbridge office, Thursday night, late.

Peg waits up at the table in her dressing gown with a pot of tea. Danny is there, still in his work clothes. Tess comes in with the cold on her coat.

TESS: There's a car on the basement.

DANNY: Whose?

TESS: Old Bill's.

DANNY: How d'you know?

TESS: I went and looked.

Danny shrugs.

DANNY: Batcher says a copper was in the plant yesterday. Reading my pour contracts.

PEG: Which?

DANNY: All of 'em. *(a grin)* Best advert I've got.

Peg doesn't smile. Her cup stops halfway.

PEG: Who's talked?

DANNY: Nobody at this table.

Tess says nothing about the pit. She sits.

TESS: So we go the long way, from the street. The mews at the back, down through the road and in through the back wall. Ten seconds.

DANNY: Through Liam's beads.

PEG: She did nine four at Mayfair. Ten's near enough the same.

TESS: Near enough isn't.

PEG: *(evenly)* Stones sleep there till Monday, and Dilys comes Tuesday. Is it ten or isn't it?

TESS: It's ten.

PEG: Then it's the longest ever, and she'll be the one that did it.

Peg rinses her cup and goes up. Danny follows.

**Later.** The office is dark except for the printer's small green light. Tess sits alone at the table, walking the job in her head: down through the road, in through the wall, the tray, out. Her lips move in the dark.

A creak on the stairs. Rosie, in Danny's old jumper, stops on the bottom step when she sees her.

ROSIE: Thought everyone was in bed.

TESS: Walking it.

ROSIE: Course you are.

Rosie looks past her at the fridge. A beat. She crosses to the sink instead, runs the cold tap, drinks a glass of water standing up, and goes back upstairs.

At the top of the stairs a door stands an inch open. Terry's. Behind it, in the dark, a slice of his face.

### Scene 9. The weighbridge office, Friday, a minute to midnight.

The same minute as the cold open. This time the camera stands where Tess stands.

Rosie steps onto the plate in her rig and her socks. The printer chatters. Peg holds the ticket to the lamp.

PEG: Forty-one eight.

The cheer goes up, and now we see what we didn't see before. Tess's hands stay at her sides. Her eyes are on Rosie's wrists where the jersey sleeves have ridden up, and on the cords standing out in her neck. At the fridge, fetching milk for the Thermoses, Terry raises the lid of his blue pot a crack and looks inside: the level is where it was last night, and the spoon lying across it is clean. He lowers the lid slowly and shuts the fridge. Under the clapping, down in his basket, Sixpence growls at Rosie, low and steady, and nobody hears him.

Peg draws the star. "That's a Marlow." Rosie bows, and her palm finds the dresser.

Tess's lips move.

TESS: Forty-one eight. Two for the tray. *(beat, aloud, to Peg)* Eleven two. Job's ten.

PEG: Second and a bit to spare. Your dad never had a second to spare in his life.

Tess looks up at Frank's photo, the big laughing man, and back at the thinnest name on the board.

---

## Act three: Tests that pass

### Scene 10. The scullery behind the office, job night, five past twelve.

A narrow back room: the twin tub, coats on hooks, boots in a row, a new ticket roll on the shelf. Through the open back door Danny and Liam are loading the van in the yard. Rosie, rigged, sits on the edge of the drainer lacing her boots, her coat beside her.

Tess comes in and shuts the door to the office behind her.

ROSIE: What?

Tess puts one hand flat on her own chest. With the other she reaches for Rosie's, to feel for the beat against her own.

Rosie laughs and twists away, quick as a cat.

ROSIE: Get off. You'll want a kiss next.

TESS: Stand still.

ROSIE: What's got into you?

TESS: Nothing.

Tess looks around the scullery. Then she tears a blank length off the new ticket roll, takes the pencil from behind her ear, and holds both out.

TESS: Write your name.

ROSIE: What for?

TESS: Fast.

ROSIE: *(a grin)* Normal, or showing off?

TESS: Normal.

Rosie writes on the drainer, quick and careless: ROSIE MARLOW. Plain as a shop sign. Tess looks at it, and something in her shoulders lets go.

ROSIE: And showing off.

Under it, without a pause, she writes it again in mirror script, every letter running backwards and every one flowing.

TESS: Show-off.

ROSIE: Show-off, me.

She screws the ticket into a ball, opens the door to the office, leans through to the stove, opens its little door and posts the ball inside.

ROSIE: Paper talks.

She comes back, tucks the pencil behind Tess's ear without a word, shrugs on her coat and goes out to the yard, calling to Liam about the kit bag. Tess stands alone in the scullery. Through the doorway she watches the ball of paper catch in the stove and go. She breathes out, as if she's put something heavy down.

### Scene 11. The weighbridge office, job night, twenty past twelve.

Peg fills Thermoses at the counter. Terry is wrapping the last of the sandwiches. The back door opens and Kieran comes in from the yard with Rosie's woolly hat.

KIERAN: Nan said get your hat, it's bitter. *(He holds up a block of lard in torn greaseproof paper, a corner cut away.)* Why've you got lard in your van?

Tess, at the printer, turns her head.

ROSIE: For the pit. Cold-water swimming. Channel swimmers grease up.

KIERAN: Can I have some for the pit?

ROSIE: You'll smell like a chip shop.

She takes the lard off him and drops it on the dresser as if it's nothing. Tess is still looking at it.

Rosie meets her eyes and winks.

As Rosie crosses the room for her hat, Sixpence raises his head in his basket and growls at her, low.

PEG: He's still jealous.

ROSIE: *(ruffling the dog's ears as he backs away)* Everyone's jealous of me.

Tess turns back to the printer. Her lips move. The number hasn't changed. Eleven two.

### Scene 12. The yard behind the office, job night, half past twelve.

Terry sits on an upturned crate by the bins with the cordless phone. Beyond the fence the gravel heaps stand pale in the dark. He has taken off one yellow glove to hold the phone.

TERRY: Can't sleep, no. You?

CON *(on the phone, faint and old, a voice with a river in it)*: Never do now.

TERRY: They've been clapping again.

CON: What for?

TERRY: Weight.

Con says nothing. A lorry goes by on the A13.

TERRY: Pot's going down, Con. Every night since the boy's birthday. Two fingers. I top it up.

CON: Mice.

TERRY: Big mice. *(beat)* Last night it never moved. Nor today.

CON: That's good, then, isn't it?

TERRY: Is it?

Silence on the line.

CON: Boat's late again.

TERRY: Come here, I'll feed you.

CON: You don't know where here is, Tel.

TERRY: *(a small laugh)* No. I don't.

He rings off, pulls his glove back on and goes in. He takes his blue pot out of the fridge and sets it on the side by the stove. He takes the lid off, stands a clean spoon upright in it, and leaves it there, where anyone could reach it.

### Scene 13. The yard, job night, ten to one.

The van's back doors are open. Liam stows the kit bag, the sandwiches and the Thermoses, everything in its place. Rosie, rigged under her coat, sits on the tailgate with her hat on and her boots unlaced.

LIAM: How d'you keep it off?

ROSIE: Willpower, me.

LIAM: I've got none.

ROSIE: You've got loads. You just spend it all on breakfast.

He laughs. She laughs.

LIAM: What's ten like?

ROSIE: *(a beat)* Long.

She hops down and goes back inside for her gloves. The office is empty. She passes the stove, and the blue pot with the spoon standing up in it. She stops. Her hand moves.

The back door opens behind her. Tess, come to fetch her.

Rosie's hand goes past the pot and picks up the last foil sandwich instead.

ROSIE: For Liam.

She takes her gloves off the dresser and walks out. The spoon stands in the pot.

Peg stands at the office door with a coat over her nightie as the van doors close.

PEG: Face home.

ROSIE: Always.

Tess gets in last. As the van rolls across the big bridge and the printer chatters behind them, her lips are moving.

TESS: Eleven two.

---

## Act four: The diamond room

### Scene 14. The mews behind Marrable Street, Saturday, 2:58 a.m.

The van sits on the cobbles over the back wall of the exchange with its lights off. Danny at the wheel with the stopwatch on his phone. Liam in the passenger seat. In the back, Tess kneels facing Rosie, and holds out her hand.

TESS: Coat. Hat. Gloves. Boots.

Rosie peels them off one at a time and passes them forward. Liam takes them onto his lap.

TESS: What the plate saw. *(beat)* Forty-one eight, two for the tray. Eleven two. Job's ten. Down through the road, in at the corner, drawers on your left. Wall's at three.

ROSIE: Don't be pretty about it.

TESS: Don't be pretty about it.

Rosie flexes her fingers once, the way every Marlow does, and steps off the world.

**The black.** Sound drops to her heartbeat and her breath. Her glow reaches an arm's length round her, grey-gold, and past it there is nothing at all. Her ears scream that she's falling. She moves the way the pit taught her: small pulls at nothing, down by count.

The wrist unit counts tenths. 0.4. In the glow, the van's floor from inside, then the cobbles from underneath, packed sand, a gas main like a sleeping snake. 1.1. Clay. An old brick sewer, and inside it black water moving. 1.9. Then the concrete.

It is Danny's concrete, and inside it are the beads, packed grey through the grey like frogspawn in jelly, the whole thickness of the wall. She pulls herself through, slow. 2.6. On a seam between two pours, deep inside the wall, her glow slides over a handprint the size of a shovel blade, and beside it, drawn with a finger in the wet mix three years ago, the letters LM.

She goes on past it.

**The van.** Tess counts under her breath.

TESS: ...two... three. There's the wall.

Somewhere under the cobbles comes a rumble, like a train going by a long way down. Liam feels it through the seat.

LIAM: She can't hear you.

TESS: I know.

LIAM: Then why d'you do it?

TESS: So someone's counting.

**The black.** 3.8. She is out of the concrete and into the room. The glow shows steel drawers from the inside, their locks, their runners. The drawers stand hard by the room's steel door, and on the far side of it, tipped back against the steel in his chair, close enough to touch, a guard sleeps. The glow shows her the inside of him: ribs rising and falling, the slow fist of his heart. For one beat she watches it, the way she watches the surgeons on her phone.

The first beads fall through the top of her glow, silent, like black hail. She reaches into a drawer through its steel face and draws out the tray: velvet, and folded papers, and in the papers a hard glitter. The beads climb the drawers. 4.9. The tray is flat against her chest, and the beads close over the drawer where it lay.

She turns and goes back the way she came.

**The van.** At the mouth of the mews, a thin lad on a bike has stopped under the lamp with one foot on the kerb. His hood is up. He's watching the van.

DANNY: Kid.

LIAM: Just a kid.

Danny's hand goes to the door.

TESS: *(not looking up, still counting)* Nobody moves.

Nobody moves. The lad watches a moment longer, then pedals off into the dark.

**The black.** 7.2. The concrete again, the beads again. The handprint slides past. 8.6. The sewer. 9.0. Clay.

9.2. Close on her fingertips as the tingle starts, a fizz under the nails, while she is still in the clay under the road, where stepping out means dying inside the ground. She doesn't hurry, because hurrying wastes pulls. She finds the tug of home, faces it, and goes.

**The van.** Danny, eyes on the stopwatch:

DANNY: Nine four.

Nobody breathes. It's the longest a Marlow has ever been gone, and the count goes on past it.

Rosie lands on the floor of the load bay with a gasp, the tray against her chest. The van smells of bacon and diesel.

TESS: Ten dead.

Danny stops the watch. He turns round in his seat.

DANNY: Straight?

ROSIE: Straight.

She's shaking. Liam puts her own coat round her, and she laughs up at him with her teeth chattering.

ROSIE: Longest ever.

DANNY: Longest ever.

Liam holds out the foil sandwich she brought him. She pushes it back.

ROSIE: Big lad needs it.

Danny pulls out of the mews. At its mouth, under the lamp, there's nobody.

### Scene 15. The weighbridge office, Saturday, ten past four in the morning.

Peg has waited up with the kettle on, and Shaun with her. Terry sits at the stove end in his dressing gown; beside him on the side stands the blue pot, the spoon still upright in it. Kieran is asleep sideways in the armchair under his coat. The van rolls onto the big bridge, and the printer chatters.

They come in low and happy, so as not to wake the boy. Peg kisses Rosie on both cheeks, takes the coat off her shoulders and hangs it on the hook by the door, and goes straight to the board with her chalk. Beside the big star she starts to write the job, the way the family writes its best nights in chalk and wipes them off later: 10.0.

Danny buckles on his rig's belt, rolls up his right sleeve and goes to the green safe at the end of the little bridge, ready to post the tray through the door. Rosie carries the tray across to him. She steps onto the plate.

Peg's chalk is on the second nought when Rosie's knees go.

She folds onto the steel the way a coat slides off a hook. The tray skids across the plate and stops against the safe. Under the counter the printer chatters and prints a ticket nobody tears off.

LIAM: Rosie?

He is there first. He gathers his sister up off the plate the way he carried the canvas in from the van that first morning, like a tray of glass, and carries her to the settle by the stove.

---

## Act five: The meter

### Scene 16. The weighbridge office, continuous.

Rosie lies on the settle, grey, her skin wet and cold, shaking in long shudders. Her eyes roll and come back and roll again. She's murmuring about home. Danny kneels at the end of the settle and holds her stockinged feet in his big hands to warm them.

Peg puts two fingers to Rosie's neck.

PEG: Going like a bird. When did she last eat?

DANNY: She had a fry-up yesterday. I watched her.

Tess yanks open the cutlery drawer and digs Peg's glucose meter out from among the spoons. She pricks Rosie's finger, squeezes a bead of blood onto the strip and waits for the beep.

The screen reads **LO**.

TESS: She's hypo.

Peg takes over at once. She has done this for herself in lay-bys and car parks for years.

PEG: Gel. Top drawer of the dresser. In her cheek, rub it on the gum.

Tess squeezes a tube of glucose gel into Rosie's cheek and rubs it along her gum with a finger. They wait. Peg watches the clock. Tess tests again.

**LO.**

PEG: Another.

A second tube. They wait. Kieran is awake now, standing on the armchair cushion to see over the adults' shoulders.

**LO.**

PEG: *(quieter)* She should be coming up.

Danny has his phone out. His thumb is on the nine.

DANNY: I'm ringing someone.

PEG: With that on the floor?

The tray lies against the safe where it skidded, glittering. Danny looks at it. He looks at his daughter.

PEG: Nobody's ringing anybody. We look after our own. *(to Shaun)* Tea. Four sugars.

Shaun makes it with shaking hands. Tess holds the cup to Rosie's mouth. The tea runs out of the corners and down her neck. She can't swallow.

Terry is standing at the stove with his blue enamel pot in his hands. He picked it up off the side at the first LO, and he has been holding it ever since.

TERRY: Shift.

Nobody moves.

TERRY: Shift, Tess.

He kneels by the settle, a big soft man in yellow gloves, and puts a spoonful from the pot between Rosie's lips.

A beat. She swallows. Her eyes open, and they find Terry, and stay on him. Another spoonful. She swallows again. The shaking slows.

Colour creeps back into her face as if someone were filling her up from the bottom.

Terry puts the spoon back in the pot.

TERRY: She's been at my pot since the party.

Nobody speaks. The fridge hums.

PEG: Don't be soft, Terry.

TERRY: Every night, Peg. Two fingers. I've been topping it up.

Tess looks down at the meter in her hand. LO. On Peg, in lay-bys, it has always meant one thing: sugar, now.

TESS: It's not low. It can't read her.

Her eyes go to the fridge. On its door, side by side, hang the two birthday cards: the old one in careful, wobbly mirror script, and the new one, where every backwards letter runs as easily as breathing.

TESS: *(flat, like a number)* She's turned, Mum.

Peg flinches at the word as if Tess had thrown something.

Kieran climbs down off the armchair. He goes to the printer, the way he does every morning, tears off the ticket it printed when Rosie fell, and holds it in both hands. Nobody sees him do it.

Tess crosses to the hook by the door and takes down Rosie's coat. She goes through its pockets slowly, one after another, turning each one inside out. A hair tie. A biro, chewed. And a paper folded small and gone soft at the creases. She unfolds it. OBVERSE. RESTORATION. £180,000.

She turns it over. The back is covered in writing: ROSIE MARLOW, over and over, down the page. The first ones are pressed hard and slow, like a child copying from the board. Each one after is quicker and looser, until the last few look dashed off without a thought.

Tess looks at it for a long moment.

TESS: The job. You brought us the job.

ROSIE: *(barely a voice)* It was for Terry.

TERRY: *(gently)* No it wasn't, love.

Tess folds the leaflet along its soft creases and puts it back in the pocket.

Peg looks across at the green safe, where the painting lies. For a moment she looks very old.

PEG: That picture was never a copy.

### Scene 17. The weighbridge office, Saturday, half past five in the morning.

Still dark outside; the yard lights lay their orange bars through the blinds. The whole family is at the long table where they cheered her, under the board. Rosie sits wrapped in a blanket with Terry's pot in front of her and eats from it slowly, openly, for the first time, and cannot stop. The tray of stones sits beside the pot, and nobody looks at it. Peg's meter lies among the mugs where Tess put it down.

PEG: When?

ROSIE: Mayfair.

DANNY: Mayfair.

He puts his face in his hands.

PEG: You've sat at this table every morning.

ROSIE: Every morning.

PEG: And we clapped.

Nobody has anything to say to that.

PEG: You'll go again. Short ones. It'll come up.

TERRY: *(very quietly)* Eleven times, Peg.

Peg doesn't look at him.

PEG: *(to Rosie)* You faced home.

It isn't quite a question. Rosie looks round the table: her dad, her brother, Tess, the stars on the board, Terry.

ROSIE: Something took hold of me in the black.

Silence.

ROSIE: At Mayfair, after the wall. Something heavy, right up by me. Like a lorry going by too close on the A13. It pulled me off home, Dad. I lost home. And when I found it again...

She doesn't finish. She doesn't need to.

Terry looks at her across the table. Then he looks down at his gloves. He gets up and fills the kettle.

DANNY: *(low)* Pulled you off home.

TESS: The wall logged two.

Every head turns to her.

TESS: At Mayfair. Two bodies in the black at twelve minutes past three. Hers, and one more.

PEG: Who told you that?

TESS: The copper. Wednesday.

DANNY: Wednesday. *(beat)* And you tested her. What with?

TESS: Her name. Write it, fast.

ROSIE: I passed.

Nobody speaks. Peg's eyes go to her meter, lying among the mugs. So do Tess's.

Danny pushes his chair back.

DANNY: Aherns work Mayfair.

SHAUN: All winter.

Danny is already reaching for his coat on the back of the door.

Peg looks up at Frank's photo. Then she looks at her son.

PEG: Blood pays blood.

**Cut to black.**

**END OF EPISODE 3**

---

## Plants and payoffs

**Paid off here, from episodes 1 and 2**
- **Rosie turned since Mayfair (twist 3, the first major reversal).** The camera points at one clue only after the meter, the two birthday cards on the fridge, and leaves the rest for the audience to re-read for itself: the crisp packet (1); Sixpence growling, "jealous" (1, 2 and 3); the jar (1); Terry's "Goes through me like water" (1); 43.6 and 43.1 cheered, now 41.8 (1-3); the seven glances at the catalogue cutting (2); the roll "joke" (2); "I'll price it for your birthday" (1), the clinic (2), the leaflet on the steering wheel (3) and "No it wasn't, love"; Terry's pot dropping (1) and topped up (1, 3); the two plates (3); the lard (3); the dodge (3); the plain signature, and the mirror one that flows (3); the job she pitched (3).
- **Ray's "It's the original" (1):** Peg concedes at the meter, "That picture was never a copy." Ray's line at the car, "If the painting turned, so did whoever carried it", is heard only by Tess, who keeps it to herself. The family's belief from 2, Peg's idea that a picture can roll about and turn on its own, breaks at the meter, not at the car.
- **Ray's 03:12 notebook and Rosie's flinch (1):** "two bodies in the black at twelve minutes past three." In 1 she felt a real pull, "the way a lorry passing too close on the A13 tugs at a car"; she builds her lie on it in the same words.
- **Tess's glucose sweet and "Better. See?" (2):** the gel does nothing. **Peg's meter in the cutlery drawer (1, 2):** it reads LO.
- **Ray turned (2):** Tess sees the Register card and hears "It's all that sticks", Terry's own words from 1.
- **"What was left of him" (1), and Tess asking Peg about it (2):** "First job I ever caught was on Marrable Street." Tess starts to ask and doesn't.
- **"She can't hear you, Tess." / "I know." (1):** Liam, who was in the van that night, says it now, and asks the question nobody asked: "Then why d'you do it?" "So someone's counting."
- **"Everything in you counts, breakfast too" (1):** on job night Rosie is weighed in exactly what she crosses in, and in the van Tess strips her back to "what the plate saw".
- **Liam's "Could've gone in twice" (1) and "Thickest at the back" (2):** now the Marrable Street floor.
- **The Harley Street van and its number plate on her lips (2)** become the job. **The Aherns**, first named here, work Mayfair.

**Planted here, for later**
- **"Something took hold of me in the black... It pulled me off home" (7:** "Nothing took hold of me. I thought I'd faced wrong, and I was ashamed"). It is a pull, not a grip, so it breaks no rule, and it rests on the family's own belief that facing home keeps you straight. Nothing on screen says it's a lie: Terry looks at his gloves and fills the kettle, which can be read either way. The audience felt the real pull in 1 and can half believe her until 6.
- **Two bodies at 3:12; Ray keeps the second weight (5, 7:** Joe, 59.0, six metres away throughout). At the table Tess passes on only the two bodies. Ray's diet line she keeps to herself, so in 4 she can be the one to pass it on.
- **"Had a call"; the car on the basement; the thin lad at the bus stop, and on his bike at the mouth of the mews at about 3 a.m. (4:** Maggie's tip, and Joe's "All week. They went in the long way"; 7: the family learns).
- **"Blood pays blood"; Danny reaching for his coat at half past five on Saturday 4 April (4:** the war. The April dig is at 05:40, ten minutes later).
- **Ten seconds at Marrable Street (6:** a seven-in-ten chance of curing Rosie that came up wrong). **Tess's two short trips, overruled (6:** "I gave you nine").
- **Peg: "Your dad never had a second to spare in his life" (10:** a tenth). **"Your grandad's street", Frank's last job, and "Fifteen stone, he was" under the painted rule, beside a ten-second job (9, 10:** 95 kilos buys one second, a sum any viewer can do; 64.3 kg on the Marrable Street wall; Frank's tickets).
- **Peg: "Clears two plates and never puts a gram on"** (a re-read the audience makes at once, and again in 10 with Frank's curve). **Peg chalks the job's 10.0 beside the star, a record kept in chalk (10:** she wipes the board before the investigators come).
- **Ray: "How it's done, I don't know yet... What it looks like, I know"** (4: the Aherns' spinner theory; 7: nobody spun anybody). **"So I wait till she comes to me hungry" (8:** the Laundry). **The ring in the stomach:** Ray can't look, and Tess, who can reach, is dodged.
- **Danny: "Best advert I've got"** about a copper reading his pour contracts (7: his floor under Aoife).
- **The writing test.** Rosie's plain hand is practised down the back of the Obverse leaflet; her mirror hand flows (10: Ray's mirror signature, the same trick the other way round). Tess's pencil comes down, but Rosie writes with it and Tess writes nothing (7: the ferry timetable is the first thing Tess writes). The ticket goes in the stove; the leaflet goes back in Rosie's coat pocket (4: she still has it).
- **"Tested her. What with?" "Her name."** Tess's first real search (the coat), and both women looking at the meter among the mugs: a check against something that never crossed was in the drawer all along (7, 9, 10).
- **"Near enough isn't" against Peg's "Ten's near enough the same" (5, 7:** Peg plans "near enough").
- **"What the plate saw" (5:** Liam's fry-up, eaten after the plate has seen him).
- **Con:** "Boat's late again"; "You don't know where here is, Tel" (4: Con is an Ahern, fed off the Rotterdam boats).
- **Liam:** his handprint and "LM" inside the Marrable Street wall; "How d'you keep it off?", "You spend it all on breakfast" (4: Rosie coaches him; 5: the fry-up and the bead floor).
- **Peg: "Nobody's ringing anybody. We look after our own" (8:** the Register ward, "Police don't get the list").
- **Peg: "You'll go again. Short ones." Terry: "Eleven times, Peg" (6:** why hops fail; 8: the fourteen hops).
- **Tess says "turned"; Peg flinches (10:** "Terry. Turned. 2021").
- **The leaflet, £180,000 (4:** Lisbon and the passport; 5: Liam's job, "cash for Rosie's cure").
- **The ticket printed when Rosie folds on the plate.** Kieran tears it off and keeps it; the audit roll keeps its own copy (10: the rolls).
- **Terry's pot**, out of the fridge and on the side with a spoon standing in it, now feeding two turned mouths (4 opens on it; 8: the Laundry's bill).
- **The tingle** comes at 9.2, about two seconds before her time runs out, as it did at Mayfair (9.0, with the painting counted). Episodes 5 and 6 can bring theirs "early" by the same measure.

**Continuity for episode 4.** This episode ends at half past five on Saturday 4 April, with Danny reaching for his coat and the tray of stones on the table. Rosie's writing test is paid off here, on the back of the leaflet, so the episode 4 draft's letters in the windscreen fog would show it a second time. The leaflet is back in Rosie's coat pocket. At the table Tess told the family about the two bodies and nothing else Ray said.

**Numbers used:** Rosie 41.8, weighed rigged in her crossing clothes (11.2 seconds with the 2 kg tray, for a 10.0-second job); 44.0, 43.6, 43.1 on the board; Mayfair 9.4 and 3:12; the Marrable Street floor 2023; Ray turned in 2015, seven seconds; Terry's eleven hops; £180,000 and seventy per cent; "six months". The wrist-unit tenths in the black are Rosie's route, and nothing turns on them: out of the concrete at 3.8, the tray at 4.9, the tingle at 9.2, home at 10.0. No new canonical numbers.

**Names introduced:** the batcher; the dealers' exchange on Marrable Street; the Old Street job, Frank's mate's second visit.


# Episode 4: The Other Bank

*The war begins, and across the river the enemy is living the same nightmare.*

---

## Cold open

### Scene 1. The weighbridge office, Grays, Saturday 4 April, 05:40.

Ten minutes after "Blood pays blood". Grey light at the blinds. Nobody has been to bed.

The family is still round the long table where we left them. ROSIE (22) sits wrapped in a blanket with Terry's blue enamel pot in front of her, eating from it slowly, a spoonful at a time. The tray of stones from Marrable Street lies beside the pot, glittering on its velvet, and still nobody looks at it. TESS (38) holds a mug of tea gone cold. PEG (72), in her dressing gown, sits at the head under the chalk board, where Rosie's column ends in 41.8, the biggest star the board has ever had, and a 10.0 with its last nought half drawn. SHAUN (26) and LIAM (19) slump at the far end. KIERAN (12) is asleep sideways in the armchair under his school coat. TERRY (66), in his yellow gloves, pours the kettle into the teapot. SIXPENCE, the old lurcher, sleeps in his basket by the stove.

The back door opens. DANNY (41) comes in from the yard in his shirtsleeves with his coat over his arm and his rig still buckled round his waist, rubbing the cold out of his arms. Everything counts, so he hopped without the coat.

PEG: Where've you been?

DANNY: Pit.

PEG: At this hour.

DANNY: Seeing if I've still got it.

TERRY: And have you?

DANNY: Two seconds' worth.

He drops into his chair. Terry puts a mug in front of him.

The floor starts to hum.

It sounds like a loaded lorry rolling onto the big bridge outside, but there's no lorry. The hum turns into a grinding that comes up through the lino, through the steel plate of the little bridge, through the legs of the table. The cups on the dresser hooks begin to swing, and then to walk, one after another, off their hooks and onto the floor. Peg's brass weights chatter in their wooden box. The stones shiver on the velvet. Frank's photo swings on its nail. Rosie puts both hands round Terry's pot and holds it down. Sixpence is up, barking. Kieran jerks awake.

It stops.

A car alarm goes off somewhere on the estate. Outside, every gull on the gravel heaps goes up at once, screaming.

Terry turns up the radio on the counter. It's the same BBC voice that reported the silent merger in March.

RADIO: ...a tremor felt across Thurrock and Gravesham at twenty to six this morning. The British Geological Survey says there is no fault beneath the estuary that could account for it. In London, nobody felt a thing. Scientists are calling it a next-door event.

Danny looks down at the floor, then out through the blinds, towards the marsh.

DANNY: The Hill's moved.

Peg gets up and straightens Frank's photo on its nail.

PEG: Hills don't move.

Kieran is already off the armchair. He hauls the cast-iron test weight out from under the counter with both hands — his arms are shaking, from the quake or the hour — and sets it on the counter's edge while he digs his exercise book out of his bag. The weight tips, goes off the edge and lands on the lino with a clang that puts everybody's heart in their mouths. Sixpence barks at it.

PEG: Mind my floor!

Kieran apologises with a look, hauls the weight onto the middle of the little bridge, and stands back. The display is dark; the printer chatters. He tears off the ticket, reads it, and frowns. He takes the weight off, lets the plate settle, and sets it down again. The printer chatters. The same.

He opens his exercise book. At the top of a column of 20.00s going back to February is last night's: 20.00. He looks at the ticket in his hand. He looks at the weight, sitting where it always sits.

He writes: 19.78.

Nobody asks him what it says. Peg bends and picks a cup up off the lino. Its handle stays on the floor.

**MAIN TITLE: SECONDS**

---

## Act one: Blood pays blood

### Scene 2. The weighbridge office, Saturday, 06:30.

The first lorries are queuing at the gate with their engines running. The hatch stays down and the door stays bolted. Terry passes bacon rolls out of the side window to the cabs, wrapped in foil, and comes back to the stove.

PEG: *(to Rosie)* Bed.

ROSIE: Nan —

PEG: Bed.

Rosie takes her pot and goes up the stairs. Halfway up, where the stairs turn and you can see the room through the banisters, she sits down.

Peg wets a dishcloth under the tap and goes to the chalk board. She wipes Rosie's column: 44.0, 43.6, 43.1, 41.8, the star, the unfinished 10.0. Three strokes, and it's a grey smear. Low down at the board's edge, where years of wiping have thinned the paint, an older name shows through the chalk dust: TERRY. Nobody at the table says anything. Through the banisters, Rosie watches her own numbers go, and then sees the other name. She looks at Terry, pouring tea at the stove with his back to the room. He doesn't turn round.

Peg rinses the cloth, sits down and pours the tea. Danny sweeps the stones off the table into their canvas bag without counting them.

PEG: Go on, then.

Danny puts both fists on the table.

DANNY: Something took hold of my girl in the black. Two bodies in the wall's log, and the Aherns have worked Mayfair all winter.

On the stairs, Rosie's spoon stops.

TESS: *(quiet)* The copper said there's a way to kill a crosser without leaving a mark. Sit heavy by her in the black till she loses home. She faces wrong, she comes home wrong, and six months later it looks like a diet.

DANNY: When did he tell you that?

TESS: Wednesday.

DANNY: And you're telling us now.

TESS: Now I believe it.

DANNY: Then they meant her dead.

SHAUN: Blood pays blood.

DANNY: They've got turned over there. Everyone on the river knows. They buy their tubs off the Rotterdam boat and keep them in a reefer on their wharf. *(He taps the table: one, two.)* We take their tubs through the black and bring them back the wrong way round. Like the picture. Then their turned can starve at their table, like mine did at this one.

Tess doesn't answer. She nods at the wall by the stove, where the catalogue cutting is still pinned up: the woman, the balance, the window on the left.

TESS: Then you'd be eating it.

DANNY: What?

Tess keeps her eyes on the cutting. Danny follows them: the window on the left. In the safe at the end of the plate lies a painting with its window on the right. Then his eyes go to the stove end, where Terry has taken a white tub out of his fridge and is eating from it with a spoon, standing up, in his gloves.

Danny sits back.

DANNY: Then I'll turn one of them. Get hold of one in the black and wrench him round. I've got two seconds.

TESS: Grab someone in there and one of you turns. Coin says which.

DANNY: I'll have him by the collar.

TESS: Collar does nothing. It's skin, or it's nothing. And don't think you'll do it with a posted hand. A hand still stood on the world can't hold anything that's off it. Slides off him like smoke.

ROSIE *(from the stairs)*: And whoever turns leaves the hand in there.

Every head comes round.

ROSIE: Girl at the clinic's is plastic. Left one. Pours your water with the other.

KIERAN: Is that true?

SHAUN: You know the fella off the Tilbury ferry? Sleeve pinned up?

KIERAN: He said a dog had it.

SHAUN: Shook on it.

Kieran looks down at his own hand, opens it, and shuts it.

On the stairs, Rosie turns her own hand over and looks at it — both sides, the way you'd look at a tool you can't afford to lose. Then she looks at Terry's yellow gloves at the stove. Terry, without turning round, moves the kettle back over the flame. Nobody says anything about any of it.

Peg puts the teapot down.

PEG: Nobody's wrenching anybody. *(to Danny)* You want to hurt them? Have the lot. Every tub. Let them find out what hungry is.

DANNY: Take it?

PEG: It's two mouths here now. *(She doesn't look at the stairs.)* Stones go to the fence Friday. A third, if he's kind.

TESS: That's Terry's year. Not two.

PEG: Then theirs'll do for this month.

TESS: A reefer's a tin box with a thin skin. You don't go in. *(to Danny)* You put your hand in. Same as the safe.

DANNY: Blind.

TESS: You've done the safe blind for years.

Danny flexes his right hand, and for the first time since the van came home, he nearly smiles.

TESS: Their tow goes out at half one, comes past ours just after two, and it's back for four. Declan takes her up. That leaves the women, the kids and an old fella.

SHAUN: Boat's Tuesdays.

PEG: Wednesday night, then. When it's full.

LIAM: I'll carry.

DANNY: You'll carry.

Liam goes pink to the ears.

KIERAN: I could carry.

PEG: You could eat your breakfast.

ROSIE: I'll go.

PEG: You'll stop in.

ROSIE: I'm lightest.

PEG: *(sliding an egg onto Shaun's plate)* You're poorly.

ROSIE: I'm turned, Nan.

The fish slice stops over Shaun's plate. Then it goes on. Peg doesn't turn round.

PEG: Shaun drives.

Rosie looks at the grey smear on the board, and at the older name showing through beneath it. She takes her pot, and goes on up the stairs.

### Scene 3. Grays Post Office, Tuesday, 10 a.m.

A queue of pensioners and parcels. On the glass, a laminated sign: PASSPORTS. CHECK AND SEND.

Rosie waits with a passport form on a clipboard.

At the counter. The CLERK (60s, glasses on a chain) runs down the form with the end of her pen. Name. Date of birth. Place of birth. At the bottom, Rosie has signed it: ROSIE MARLOW, plain as a shop sign, quick and flowing. The pen stops at a box: HANDEDNESS: C (COMMON) / T (TURNED). Rosie has ticked C.

CLERK: Going anywhere nice?

ROSIE: Lisbon.

CLERK: Lovely. *(Her eyes go, just for a second, to Rosie's wrists, bone-thin in her sleeve.)* Only you'll want to be sure of this box, love. They strip-test you at the gate now.

ROSIE: Strip?

CLERK: Sugar strip on your finger, like my Alan does for his diabetes. Your photo can't tell them which hand you are. The strip can.

ROSIE: And if it comes up T?

CLERK: Then you don't fly. Portugal won't have a T. Most places won't. *(lower)* They're frightened what you'd bring in with you.

ROSIE: And if I'd ticked C?

CLERK: Then you've told the Passport Office a lie, and you still don't fly. *(beat)* Had a lad in before Christmas, going out for one of them cut-price jobs. They sent him home from Stansted.

Rosie takes a paper gone soft at its creases out of her coat pocket and opens it: the Obverse leaflet. RESTORATION. £180,000. Under the printed price, in biro, in her handwriting: LISBON. HALF. She folds it back along its creases and puts it away.

ROSIE: Southend, then.

The clerk laughs. Rosie laughs louder: the laugh the family knows, the one she does when she's lying.

CLERK: Next!

Outside, in the Post Office window, the poster from the bus shelter: IMMUNE. FED. NEEDED. Join the Fever Company. Rosie looks at it the way you look at a bus you're not getting on. Then she turns her collar up and walks home along the fog line of the river.

### Scene 4. The flooded gravel pit, Tuesday, dusk.

Liam stands on the pontoon in a wetsuit that won't zip the last hand's width up his back, with the black-painted goggles pushed up on his forehead. Rosie sits on the upturned drum at the end, in Danny's old jumper under her coat, with the stopwatch open on her phone.

LIAM: So?

ROSIE: T doesn't fly. *(beat)* So it's a hundred and eighty thousand. And it's here.

LIAM: Then I'll get it you.

ROSIE: Carrying tubs?

LIAM: Crossing. A proper job. *(He slaps his thigh; it wobbles.)* Not like this. I'll get under.

ROSIE: You're the big lad.

LIAM: Not for ever.

Rosie looks at him: the broad white shoulders, the wetsuit gaping at his back.

ROSIE: Goggles.

He pulls them down and drops off the pontoon. She starts the watch. Her lips move as she counts, exactly as Tess's do.

He comes up gasping and shoves the goggles back.

LIAM: How long?

ROSIE: You kicked down.

LIAM: Everyone kicks down.

ROSIE: Breathe out a bit. Follow your bubbles.

LIAM: *(grinning)* That's what Tess says to Kieran.

He hangs on the pontoon edge, dripping.

LIAM: How d'you keep it off, Rosie? Really.

ROSIE: Told you. Willpower.

LIAM: Really.

She looks at the water, at the flat grey of it, for a moment too long.

ROSIE: Skip the fry-up. Nan never looks under the table, and the dog'll have it. When it grabs you, drink a pint of water, and it goes off. *(beat)* And sleep in your coat.

LIAM: What for?

ROSIE: You get cold. You're cold all the time, making weight. *(beat)* After a bit you stop noticing.

LIAM: *(delighted)* Every morning, then. I'll do it every morning.

ROSIE: They'll clap you.

LIAM: They never clap me.

ROSIE: They will.

She hears it as it comes out. Her eyes drop to her own wrists, all bone in Danny's sleeves. When she looks up he's on the edge again with his goggles down, waiting for her, happy.

ROSIE: Again.

He drops in. She starts the watch. Her lips move.

---

## Act two: The hand

### Scene 5. An alley behind the Ahern yard, Gravesend, Thursday, 02:10.

Over the river by the QEII bridge, lit up like a fairground, and down into Gravesend.

The alley runs along the back of a tug yard on the south bank. Wet cobbles, one broken lamp, chain-link with a strand of barbed wire along the top. Hard against the wire on the inside, filling the frame, stands a twenty-foot refrigerated container, white, streaked with rust, with ROTTERDAM stencilled along its side. One rack of shelves runs down its whole fence side. A fat cable runs from it to the tug shed. It hums like a fridge in a quiet kitchen.

Beyond it lie the slipway and an empty berth. The mooring lines lie looped round the bollards on the quay, waiting for the tow to come back, their big knots furred green with weed. Above the shed are the windows of the Ahern rooms, all dark.

The van waits at the end of the alley with its lights off. Shaun stands at the corner, keeping watch. His eyes keep going back to the bollards and the knots.

Liam stands by an empty crate in his coat, shivering, though the night isn't that cold.

TESS: No wall on it. Nobody weighs a pantry.

Danny takes off his jacket and hands it to Liam. He rolls his right sleeve up past the elbow, buckles his rig on over his shirt and thumbs the wrist unit. He lays his palm flat on the chain-link, where the container's steel side presses against it, and pushes.

His arm goes into the wire and the steel together, like a hand going into water, up to the elbow, and stops there in a clean edge.

LIAM: *(a whisper)* You do that to the safe.

DANNY: Same thing, son.

Liam swallows. Tess counts each reach under her breath.

DANNY: Shelf. *(beat)* Something. *(His breath hisses through his teeth as his fingers close.)* Tub. Frozen solid.

He draws his arm back out of the steel. In his fingers is a blue plastic tub, white with frost, with a label in Dutch. There's frost on his fingertips and across his palm. Liam takes the tub in both hands as if it were hot, and lays it in the crate.

DANNY: One.

They work along the wire, a pace at a time, Liam sliding the crate after them. Danny goes in again and again, and says what his fingers find: tub; two stacked; a loose lid; tub.

LIAM: *(a whisper)* Nan says never go back twice.

DANNY: *(eyes shut)* It's only my hand.

At the corner, Shaun snorts.

By the ninth tub Danny's fingers come out white and stiff, and he fumbles the handover. Liam catches the tub an inch off the cobbles.

TESS: Can you feel your tingle?

DANNY: Can't feel anything. It's like grabbing ice.

TESS: Then I'll count. Nineteen for the arm, Danny. I say out at fifteen.

From then on she counts aloud, low and even, and when she says "Out" he comes out, whether he's found anything or not.

Above the shed, a window lights up.

Everyone stops. Danny's arm is inside the container up to the elbow.

A WOMAN comes to the window with a baby on her shoulder, walking it: up to the glass, away, up to the glass. She stops and looks out over the yard, at the river and the empty berth, the way you look for a boat that isn't due yet.

TESS: *(not raising her eyes)* ...seven... eight...

DANNY: *(barely a breath)* Tess.

TESS: Nobody moves. ...nine... ten...

The woman turns away from the glass, still walking the baby. The window stays lit.

TESS: Out.

He comes out with a tub.

In again, at the far end of the rack. Danny's face changes.

DANNY: Something little.

TESS: ...four... five...

DANNY: Box. Card. It's light. *(beat)* It rattles.

TESS: Leave it.

DANNY: Mum said the lot.

He leans his cheek harder into the wire, reaching.

TESS: ...eleven... twelve...

DANNY: It's gone down the back. I've got a corner —

TESS: ...sixteen... Danny.

DANNY: Nearly —

TESS: Seventeen. Out.

He pulls. His hand comes out of the steel, and pinched between two numb fingers is a small white carton with a chemist's label in Dutch. He drops it in the crate among the tubs, shakes out his hand and blows on it.

DANNY: Never felt it come.

TESS: It came.

Two more reaches at the end wall. Bare shelf. Bare shelf. His hand sweeps the frost on the floor under the last shelf and finds nothing.

DANNY: That's it.

SHAUN *(from the corner)*: That's all? For a whole family?

Danny opens his eyes and draws his arm out, empty. Two crates, and the second isn't full.

DANNY: They're shorter than us.

Tess looks up at the lit window.

TESS: Then that's all of it.

They carry the crates to the van. Behind them the window stays lit.

### Scene 6. The weighbridge office, Thursday, 04:30.

The van rolls onto the big bridge and the printer chatters. Peg has waited up with the kettle on. Terry sits at the stove end in his dressing gown. Rosie sits on the stairs.

Liam carries the crates in. Sixpence gets up in his basket and growls at them, low and steady: the growl he keeps for Terry.

PEG: Hark at him. Smells the Laundry on them.

She takes a table knife and starts on the first tub.

PEG: Labels. Paper talks.

Tess takes the knife from her, and Peg lets her, because Tess is quicker. Tess scrapes, Terry stacks, and Liam flattens the cardboard sleeves the tubs were packed in. Tess feeds labels and cardboard into the stove a handful at a time. The little white carton goes in folded inside a sleeve. For a second, in the stove's mouth, a strip of foil blisters and a line of Dutch print curls up in the flame. Then Tess shuts the door on it. Nobody saw it.

Terry stacks the blue tubs on the bottom shelf of his fridge, under the white ones from the Laundry. Rosie comes down the stairs and counts them without meaning to.

PEG: Two mouths, the best part of a fortnight, and it never cost us a penny.

TERRY: Cost someone.

PEG: Them.

Terry shuts the fridge.

At the sink, Danny runs the hot tap over his right hand. The fingers are red and white and he can't close them. Rosie comes and takes the hand in both of hers and rubs it between her palms, the way he held her feet on the settle on Saturday. He lets her. Neither of them says anything.

Across the room, Tess wipes the soot off her hands onto her jeans.

---

## Act three: Across the river

### Scene 7. The Ahern yard, Gravesend, Thursday, 7 a.m.

Daylight on the south bank. The tow is back at its berth. JOE AHERN (17), the skinny lad from the steelyard, in the hoodie we last saw on a bike at the mouth of the mews behind Marrable Street, snips the numbered seal on the container's doors and hauls them open. Cold smoke rolls out over his boots.

Every shelf inside is furred with frost, and in the frost are clean round rings where the tubs stood. Along the floor, an arm's length in from the wall, the frost is scored with long raked tracks, running the length of the rack. There isn't a bootprint anywhere.

DECLAN AHERN (70, cap), the old man who called "Fifty-nine!", stands at the doors. Behind him is MAGGIE AHERN (44), the woman who didn't cheer, with her tide book under her arm.

DECLAN: Seal was on.

JOE: Padlock and all.

Maggie's lips move over the rings, one after another.

MAGGIE: A dozen tubs, one at a time. Nobody hops a dozen times.

She crouches at the tracks and passes two fingers above them, not touching.

MAGGIE: Raked, an arm's length in. All the way down. No boots. *(beat)* That's a hand.

DECLAN: One man on this river sends a hand like that. Frank Marlow's boy.

Maggie stands and looks past him, at the empty berth and the looped lines on the bollards.

MAGGIE: Tow was out. They knew.

She looks up. At a window above the shed an old man in a cardigan, thin as a coat hanger, is looking down at the open doors: CON.

### Scene 8. The Ahern rooms over the shed, Thursday, 6 p.m.

Up a wooden stair from the shed. Through the floorboards you can hear the tide working at the slip. A long kitchen with one window over the yard, a stove, and waterman's tide books in a row along the dresser. Through the door at the head of the stair you can see down into the shed, where the old salvage steelyard hangs from its beam.

By the door hangs a chalk board of names, each with a star beside it. One column, headed AOIFE, has nothing under it at all.

On the fridge is a photo of a young woman laughing at the wheel of a tug with a baby in a sling on her chest. Beside it, taped at the top and curling at the bottom, hangs a yard of printer paper from a weigh-wall: the same line printed again and again down the strip, a date, a time and a weight, and the weight a little smaller every line. Written across the top in biro: *Aoife.*

SIOBHAN AHERN (13), the girl with the saucepan, is laying the table. CIAN, nine months old, bangs a spoon on the tray of his high chair. Declan sits at the head. At the foot sits CON AHERN (70s) in the cardigan, with a jar of dripping, a bottle of gin and a spoon, and nothing else in front of him. Joe sits by his grandad.

Maggie has the day's post and a butter knife, and she opens every envelope and reads every one: harbour dues; the Port's new tide tables; a catalogue. Then she takes a form off the dresser, filled in in her square capitals, and lays it beside Con's jar with a pen on top.

MAGGIE: Register. They'll feed you from Monday. They don't ask how you turned, or who you work for. I've done it all. You sign.

Con looks up the table at his brother.

Declan reaches down the table, picks up the form, opens the stove door and posts it in.

DECLAN: We feed our own.

MAGGIE: With what, Dad? The boat's been short since Aoife stopped doing the Rotterdam run. And now there's none.

DECLAN: Then we'll have it back off them.

SIOBHAN: Why'd they take it?

DECLAN: 'Cause they're Marlows. First they spin our Aoife. Now they starve our Con.

SIOBHAN: Did they, though? Spin her?

DECLAN: Aherns face home. Nine seconds she was in that bank, and she'd done nine once before and come back right. Their vans were outside it all week, MARLOW on the side. And she comes home to us the wrong way round. *(He looks at the fridge.)* Somebody had hold of her in there.

Nobody answers. Cian bangs his spoon.

SIOBHAN: Grandad. Do me.

She fetches a bathroom scale from the landing, sets it on the lino and steps on it in her socks, hands up like a diver. Declan leans over the dial with his back to us, squinting, for a long moment. Then he straightens up, goes to the board, and chalks her name with a star beside it.

DECLAN: Light as Aoife was, at your age.

Siobhan glows. Joe claps. Con raps his spoon on the dripping jar.

Maggie gets up, cuts two more slices off the loaf, butters them thick, and puts them on Siobhan's plate. Declan doesn't notice.

### Scene 9. The Ahern kitchen, Friday, 3 a.m.

One lamp. Maggie is at the stove making up Cian's bottle: water just off the boil, a scoop of powder levelled with a knife, another, another. Her tide book lies open on the table at the Nobody columns, a pencil in the spine. Overhead, the baby grizzles.

Con sits at the table in his cardigan, wide awake, with his gin, his dripping and his spoon.

CON: You spoil him.

MAGGIE: Her milk was wrong. He screamed three days before we understood. *(She screws the teat on.)* He can have it warm.

Con nods at the fridge, at the curl of paper.

CON: A month, the wall read her. Less every day. We stood our minute on our own quay, with the tide going out under us.

MAGGIE: I know, Con.

CON: She went again for him.

MAGGIE: So she could feed him. *(She shakes the bottle.)*

Con rubs his chest under the cardigan, slow circles with the flat of his hand.

MAGGIE: Where's your tablets?

CON: Few in my coat.

MAGGIE: And the rest?

CON: In the cold. With the rest.

Maggie stops shaking the bottle.

MAGGIE: How many's a few?

Con doesn't answer. He watches her hands on the bottle.

MAGGIE: That chemist in Rotterdam took all winter making them. All winter. Four hours each way on that boat, in January, you went.

CON: *(beat)* They worked, though. Slept right through.

He pushes himself up and takes the cordless phone off its hook on the wall.

CON: Can't sleep. I'll ring my pal.

He goes down the stair to the yard with it.

A creak on the other stair. Joe, in the hoodie, woken by the baby. He runs the tap and drinks from his cupped hands. He looks at the fridge, at the curl of paper, at the weights going down.

JOE: *(low)* All winter you've had me sat outside their jobs. *(beat)* Was it worth it, Mum? Your phone call. They still came out with the stones.

MAGGIE: Did the police sit on their basement?

JOE: All week. They went in the long way.

MAGGIE: Good.

She tests the bottle on the inside of her wrist.

MAGGIE: Ten seconds under a road, Joe. Let them count that. *(beat)* One of these nights it'll be more than they've got.

Joe looks at the paper on the fridge: the weights, smaller every line.

MAGGIE: Not a word to your grandad.

JOE: He'd kill you.

MAGGIE: He'd put the phone in the stove.

She goes up with the bottle. Overhead, the grizzling stops.

### Scene 10. Two yards, either side of the river, Friday, 3:10 a.m.

**Gravesend.** Con sits on a bollard at the edge of the empty berth, beside the humming container, with the phone to his ear. Across the black water are the lights of Tilbury and, further up, the orange glow over Grays.

**Grays.** Terry sits on his upturned crate by the bins with one glove off and the phone to his ear. Beyond the fence the gravel heaps stand pale in the dark.

TERRY: Con. You're late tonight.

CON: Your lot took my dinner, Tel.

Silence. A lorry goes by on the A13.

TERRY: My lot.

CON: Every tub, and never broke the seal. Dec says there's only one arm on this river goes through a tin like that.

Terry turns his head and looks through the lit office window, at his fridge.

TERRY: Where are you, Con?

CON: Other bank. Look across.

Terry looks out past the gravel heaps, at the river, and at the lights on the far side.

CON: Con Ahern. Declan's my brother.

TERRY: You knew who I was.

CON: Everyone knows a Marlow. *(beat)* The gravy was nice, though.

TERRY: I'll bring it back. Every tub.

CON: You won't. They wouldn't let you. Mine wouldn't. *(beat)* I wouldn't have, either.

Neither of them rings off. Two old men on two banks, with the tide running out between them, each holding a phone to his ear and saying nothing.

CON: Can't sleep, Tel.

TERRY: No. Me neither.

**Later.** The office, dark but for the printer's small green light. Terry opens his fridge. White tubs on the top shelf. Blue on the bottom, with their labels scraped off. He looks at the blue ones for a long time. Then he shuts the door.

---

## Act four: Light

### Scene 11. The weighbridge office, Friday, 06:30.
