The hatch is up and the day has begun. Lorries roll onto the big bridge and the printer chatters. Terry, who hasn't slept, turns bacon in his gloves and says nothing. Peg works the hatch.

MICK (50s, hi-vis) leans in for his ticket and a bacon roll. He turns the ticket over and frowns at it.

MICK: Your bridge has had me two hundred light, Peg.

PEG: Then you've had a good day.

Mick shrugs, takes his roll, and goes. The printer chatters behind him.

At the end of the counter Kieran has his exercise book and the night's tickets. His graph is taped to the wall above him: two months of flat line, and now a second flat line running along a step lower.

KIERAN: My weight's gone light too, Nan. Since Saturday.

PEG: You dropped that thing on my floor Saturday morning, hard enough to wake the gulls. It's chipped. Tip it up and look.

Kieran gets down on his knees and tips the test weight over on its side under the counter. On the rim of its base there's a chip: grey paint gone, bright iron under it. He runs his thumb over it. He nods.

KIERAN: That'll be it, then.

PEG: There's a boy. Stop wasting roll.

He sets it back. The post comes through the door. Peg sorts it on the counter: a seed catalogue, a feed bill, and a brown window envelope with the council's crest in the window. She opens the bill. The brown envelope she drops into the dresser drawer, on top of years of others, unopened, and pushes the drawer shut.

### Scene 12. The weighbridge office, Friday, 07:30.

Breakfast, with the hatch down for twenty minutes. The family at the long table: Peg, Danny (his right hand wrapped round his mug, still stiff), Tess, Shaun, Liam, Kieran, and Rosie, down for breakfast for the first time since Saturday. Terry at the stove. On the board, the grey smear where Rosie's column was, and the older name showing through beneath it.

Peg puts a full fry-up in front of Liam: two eggs, bacon, sausage, beans and fried bread.

PEG: Get that down you. You're carrying for your dad now.

LIAM: Ta, Nan.

Rosie gets up, goes to Terry's fridge and takes a blue tub off the bottom shelf without looking. At the stove, Terry watches her peel the lid off. He says nothing.

Tess watches Rosie eat, every spoonful, as she has since Saturday.

Under the table, Liam's hand comes down with a rasher. Sixpence's nose finds it. The rasher's gone. Then a sausage. Across the table, Rosie sees the dog's tail thump against the table leg. She looks at Liam. He looks back and waits for her to say something.

She doesn't. Peg turns from the stove with the teapot, heading for Liam's end of the table.

ROSIE: *(loud)* Nan, who's had the brown sauce?

Peg turns to look for it. Under the table, the second rasher goes.

At the end of the table, Kieran has seen the whole thing. He looks at Liam's plate, and at the dog, and at his nan and his mum, who saw none of it.

Peg comes back and pours.

PEG: Clean plate, Li. That's a Marlow.

Liam looks from his empty plate to the chalk board, at his own 72.0, which has never had a star. He sits up straight and eats the last of his fried bread with his fork proper, like his dad.

Rosie looks down into her tub. Tess is still watching Rosie.

---

## Act five: 02:04

### Scene 13. The weighbridge office, Saturday, 08:00.

Kieran is at the counter sorting the night's tickets for his log. There are the lorries that weighed after hours on the big bridge, and his own, 21:00, the little bridge, 19.78. He copies it in under the others.

And there's one more. 02:04. LITTLE BRIDGE. −59.0 KG.

He reads it twice. He looks at the plate: a square of steel set in the lino, empty, as it was all night. He looks at the bolted public door, the stove, the settle, the stairs. At four minutes past two, everyone in this house was in bed.

He turns to call his nan. She's at the hatch with a driver. He remembers "Stop wasting roll", and shuts his mouth.

A scale can't weigh less than nothing. He tapes the ticket into his book under the Twitch ticket from March, and writes beside it in pencil: GLITCH. He looks at it. Then he adds a question mark.

He squares the two tickets together — 0.31 on an empty plate in March, −59.0 on an empty plate last night — and closes the book on them both.

### Scene 14. The weighbridge office, continuous.

The phone rings on the wall by the dresser. Peg is at the hatch. Terry is at the stove. Tess, coming down the stairs, picks it up.

TESS: Marlow Aggregates.

MAGGIE *(on the phone)*: Is that the weigher?

TESS: Who wants her?

MAGGIE: The other one.

Tess goes still.

MAGGIE: Maggie Ahern.

Tess turns her back to the room.

TESS: *(flat, not much louder than the printer)* Ahern.

At the stove, Terry's spoon stops. He doesn't turn round.

MAGGIE: Little white box. Chemist's label, in Dutch. It was in with the tubs.

Tess's eyes go to Terry's fridge, and the blue tubs behind its door.

MAGGIE: Con's heart pills were in them tubs. Made to his hand. There's no more.

A beat, the length of a breath.

MAGGIE: Keep the rest. I'm not asking for the rest. The box I'll buy back. Name a price and I'll row it over tonight.

Tess looks at the stove. Behind its little window the fire is going, the same fire she fed on Thursday, a handful at a time.

Tess doesn't answer. The line clicks and goes to the dial tone. She stands with the phone against her chest.

At the stove, Terry has turned round at last. He looks at her, and past her, at the stove he cooks at. He doesn't ask.

The stove ticks.

**Cut to black.**

**END OF EPISODE 4**

---

## Plants and payoffs

**Paid off here, from episodes 1 to 3**
- **"Blood pays blood" and Danny reaching for his coat (3).** The cold open is ten minutes later; Danny has been to the pit to see if he's "still got it", and the war council follows at 06:30.
- **"Something took hold of me" and the two bodies at 3:12 (3).** Danny makes them a war. Tess at last passes on Ray's "six months, and it looks like a diet", and Danny's "When did he tell you that?" / "Wednesday" shows she sat on it for days; the turning itself is her own sum, for Ray told her he didn't know the how. Across the river Declan says the same of Aoife: "Somebody had hold of her in there."
- **The cargo rule (Ray's line at the car in 3).** Tess has learned it: a nod at the catalogue cutting, "Then you'd be eating it", and Terry eating his tub finishes the thought.
- **The clinic sign and the receptionist's hand (2); the one-handed man on the ferry (1).** "Girl at the clinic's is plastic." Kieran learns the grip rule, and that the ferry man told him a dog had his hand.
- **The writing test (3).** Rosie signs the passport form in her practised plain hand, quick and flowing, while the clerk's eyes go to her wrists anyway. The Obverse leaflet is back in Rosie's coat, where episode 3 left it; her biro "LISBON. HALF." shows only at the counter, after the clerk's "cut-price jobs" line, so the audience hears such jobs exist before it sees she has already priced one.
- **"Willpower, me" (3)** becomes "Told you. Willpower." / "Really." — and then the true answer, and her guilt.
- **The leaflet (3).** Lisbon is closed by the strip test and HAND: T, so it has to be £180,000, here. That is why Liam wants to cross.
- **The shed nobody could place (2)** is named: Declan, Joe, Siobhan, Maggie, and Cian crying overhead. **The thin lad at the bus stop and on the bike (3)** is Joe, in the same hoodie. **"Had a call" (3)** was Maggie, and Declan doesn't know. Closed.
- **Con (2, 3).** "Boat's late again" becomes "the boat's been short since Aoife stopped doing the Rotterdam run"; "You don't know where here is, Tel" becomes "Other bank. Look across." Closed for the audience and for Terry.
- **Danny's vault in '24 and "the floor underneath" last autumn (2).** Declan: "Their vans were outside it all week, MARLOW on the side." Planted, not pressed; Maggie names the bank in 7.
- **Liam's "How d'you keep it off?" (3)** gets the true answer, and Peg's "That's a Marlow" (3) goes to Liam, for a plate he fed to the dog.
- **Kieran's nightly test weight and his sorting of the tickets (1, 2)** catch the 4 April step and the 02:04 ticket, taped under the Twitch ticket. **The drawer (1)** takes the April envelope. **The tingle (1, 3)** bites on numbers: Danny's fingers are too cold to feel his, so Tess counts — and it came anyway.

**Planted here, for later**
- **The quake, "The Hill's moved", "Hills don't move", "In London, nobody felt a thing" (8, 9).** The first stroke of the message; Danny's guess is wrong and Peg, for once, is right. The London line is the one hint that the pull comes from under the estuary.
- **19.78, the fall, the chip, Mick's two hundred light, the council envelope (9).** The world's change came with its own cover story: the quake knocked the weight out of the boy's hands, so the chip is real, dates from that morning, and satisfies the one person in the family who checks. His graph has grown a step. He believes it because he dropped the thing himself.
- **02:04, −59.0, "GLITCH?" (5, 6, 7).** Episode 2's "Fifty-nine!" is now the only plant, for sharp viewers; the Ahern board carries names and stars only, and Siobhan's number is chalked with Declan's back to us. The scene ends on Kieran's book, not the safe.
- **The tow: "out at half one, comes past ours just after two, and it's back for four" (6: Danny's sum), and Maggie's "Tow was out. They knew."** No one puts Joe on the tow; on Friday at 3 a.m. he is at home, woken by the baby.
- **Con's pills (5).** "A few in my coat"; the Rotterdam chemist, all winter, four hours each way by boat in January; no resupply possible. Maggie's call is a plea to buy the box back, so Tess's slow look from the fridge to the stove is the answer, and the audience has it before she says it in 5.
- **Who knows what.** Terry knows Con is an Ahern (Con's call); Tess knows it too (Maggie's call, and Terry hears her say the name). Terry does not know about the pills. Tess does not know Con was Terry's friend.
- **Danny's hand.** Into the reefer to the elbow and back whole, too numb to feel the tingle: "Never felt it come." / "It came." Tess prices the arm at nineteen seconds and calls out at fifteen; the pill carton runs him to seventeen, two over her call. (6: the same hand loses its fingers.) Rosie warms it at the sink.
- **Shaun's long look at the Ahern mooring lines and their weed-furred knots (5).**
- **Liam (5).** "Skip the fry-up... a pint of water... sleep in your coat"; shivering in the alley; the rasher and the sausage; Rosie covering with "Who's had the brown sauce?"; from here she times his blindfold runs. Her guilt starts here.
- **Kieran sees Liam's trick, and no adult does (5–9).** Tess, still in the house, watches Rosie's spoon and never Liam's plate.
- **The wiped column and the ghost of TERRY under it (9, 10).** The family has wiped a turned name off that board before. Terry never had it wiped by anyone but time — and Rosie sees it, and he doesn't look.
- **Rosie's hands at the council (8, 10).** After "Shook on it" she turns her own hand over — the surgeon's hands — then looks at Terry's yellow gloves, and Terry puts the kettle back on the heat. Nobody speaks. Plants "We could hold on, in there" and "I cook with these".
- **"You're poorly." / "I'm turned, Nan." (10: "Terry. Turned. 2021").**
- **The Aherns (7, 10, epilogue).** Maggie opens every envelope; Peg's drawer won't. The tide books with the Nobody columns. Aoife's empty column. Declan chalks Siobhan "light as Aoife was"; Maggie answers in bread, not words, so the epilogue's "does not cheer" is a return, not a repeat.
- **Aoife (7).** Nine seconds, "she'd done nine once before and come back right"; "she went again for him"; a month on the wall, less every day; the minute on their own quay. The bank is not named.
- **Maggie's slow war:** "Ten seconds under a road. Let them count that" (7: she tells Tess herself). **"The other one"**: the two weighers speak for the first time (7: the ferry).
- **Money (5, 8).** Two mouths on the pantry; the stones to the fence Friday, "a third, if he's kind".

**Numbers used.** 4 April, 05:40; the test weight 20.00, then 19.78, chipped on the morning of the quake; Mick two hundred light; 02:04 and −59.0; Aoife's nine seconds; Danny's "two seconds' worth"; £180,000, Lisbon at half; "six months"; Cian's three days; a month on the wall; nineteen for the arm, out at fifteen; the count to seventeen on the long reach. No new canonical numbers ("a dozen", "the best part of a fortnight", "a third" and Cian at nine months are talk only, and nothing turns on them).

**Names and places introduced.** The Ahern rooms over the shed; the twenty-foot reefer, ROTTERDAM on its side, with a numbered seal and one rack down the fence side; the blue Ahern tubs with Dutch labels (the Laundry's are white); the Post Office clerk and "my Alan"; the chemist in Rotterdam.

**Continuity for episode 5.** This episode runs Saturday 4 April to Saturday 11 April 2026, and ends on Maggie's plea on the Saturday morning. Con's pills are a white carton, chemist's label in Dutch, foil blisters inside; "a few in my coat"; nothing can be made before winter. Con's phone is the yard cordless by the shed stair; if episode 5 wants Terry to ring a mobile, plant it there, or let Siobhan answer the yard phone. Tess burned the carton without knowing (a declared change from the plan, which had the pills simply go with the stolen tubs); Maggie's plea is the moment Tess works it out. The stones went to the fence on the Friday.

**Gaps.** *Opens:* the 02:04 ticket; why the scales and the test weight read light, and what the Marlow vans were doing at Aoife's bank (planted, not pressed); what the quake was; what Tess will do about the stove. *Closes:* why the Aherns hate them; who Con is; who made Ray's "call".


# Episode 5: Delivered

*The war turns lethal, and the family's heaviest boy starves himself to join it.*

---

## Cold open

### Scene 1. The weighbridge office, Grays, Tuesday, ten to seven in the morning.

Late April. Rain on the yard. Two lorries idle at the gate with their lights on, waiting for seven. Inside, the hatch is down and the public door is bolted: family only.

TERRY (66) turns the bacon in his yellow gloves. Sixpence, the old lurcher, gets up stiffly in his basket, growls at him — the low, steady note he has given Terry for five years — and lies back down. Nobody looks round. They all know what it means now.

TERRY: Yes, all right.

KIERAN (12), back in his school jumper, copies last night's ticket into his exercise book: 21:00 · LITTLE BRIDGE · 19.78. ROSIE (22) sits at the table with a blue-lidded tub and a spoon, and there is colour in her face. TESS (38) holds a mug and looks at nothing, her lips moving. PEG (72), apron over her cardigan, butters toast for a boy who is already late.

A horn at the back. DILYS (60s, launderette tabard under her coat) bangs in at the back door with the crate of white tubs.

DILYS: Your whites, Terry.

TERRY: Bless you, Dilys.

PEG: *(wiping her hands)* Where's Danny? He's to put his hand in the safe for the money.

TESS: Pouring. Moorgate. Since five. Liam and Shaun with him.

Rosie is already up.

ROSIE: My rig's in the van. I'll post my hand in.

PEG: You'll sit.

ROSIE: It's a hand, Nan. Hands don't turn.

PEG: You'll not put a finger in the black till you're right.

Rosie sits. Dilys waits in the doorway, the rain behind her, tapping the crate with one fingernail. Peg looks at the green safe standing at the end of the little bridge, the steel plate set flush in the lino.

PEG: I've a key, haven't I.

KIERAN: Since when?

PEG: Since before you.

She pulls at the dresser drawer. It sticks, as it always sticks. It's packed with brown envelopes, years of them, unopened: crowns, window envelopes, WEIGHTS AND MEASURES on top. She pushes her whole forearm in under them and digs. A corner of pale green paper with a printed hand on it slides past her wrist and back under. At the very bottom her fingers close on a key ring.

Two keys. A long brass safe key, dark with age. And a small padlock key on a loop of orange baler twine gone pale. Her thumb rests on the small one for a moment. Then she takes the ring to the safe.

She gets down on her knees on the steel plate, slowly, a hand on the safe for balance. Under the counter the printer chatters and prints her weight. Nobody tears it off.

PEG: Not since Blair.

The key goes in stiff. It won't turn. She puts both hands to it. Tess comes and takes the handle, and together they lean, and the lock gives with a clack like a knuckle. The door swings open on a groan.

Inside, on the shelf: a brown envelope of notes. A cash box. And lying flat on its tea towel, face up, *The Assayer's Wife*: the woman at her window, holding her little brass balance up to the light.

On one of the two painted pans of the balance, standing upright on its base like a small brass man, is a bullet.

Peg looks at it. She looks at the key in her hand, and at the lock she has just forced. Behind her Tess has gone very still. Rosie is on her feet. Kieran has stopped writing.

Peg reaches past the bullet without touching the painting, takes out the envelope, gets up off her knees, and counts notes into Dilys's hand at the back door with no tremble in her fingers at all.

DILYS: You all right, Peg? You've gone a colour.

PEG: Same time next week, Dilys.

The back door shuts. Peg turns round to her family.

PEG: It's been delivered.

**MAIN TITLE: SECONDS**

---

## Act one: The radius

### Scene 2. The weighbridge office, seven o'clock.

The wall clock clicks to seven. Peg does what she does at seven: she slides up the hatch and unbolts the public door.

TESS: Mum.

PEG: Lorries don't know.

MICK (50s, hi-vis) leans in at the hatch for his ticket, rain dripping off his hood. At the same moment the public door opens, and in comes a thin man in an overcoat too big for him.

MICK: Your copper's back, Peg.

PEG: He's not mine.

DS RAY AMBROSE (54) puts a coin on the counter.

RAY: Hot water, Mrs Marlow, when the kettle's on. *(pleasantly)* Heard someone emptied a cold room in Gravesend without opening the door. Thought I'd come and not ask you about it.

Then he looks past her. At the end of the room the green safe stands open, and the family stands round it like people round a hole in the road.

Peg reaches across to swing the safe door shut.

RAY: Leave it.

Her hand stops.

PEG: It's a copy. You'll have heard.

RAY: I'm not here about the picture.

Peg holds his eye for a long moment. Then she slaps the hatch down in Mick's face.

MICK: *(through the hatch)* Peg!

PEG: Bridge's shut ten minutes.

MICK: What's up with it?

PEG: Scale's playing up.

Out in the yard Mick's lorry sits on the big bridge with its engine running. Peg bolts the public door so no one else comes in. Ray raises the counter flap and walks through into the family's side of the room, where he has never been. He crouches by the safe with his hands in his coat pockets and looks without touching.

RAY: Never been fired. Doesn't need to be. *(beat)* Stood it up on her little pan. You don't do that blind.

TESS: Hand through a door's blind.

RAY: So it wasn't a hand. *(He looks at the bullet a while longer.)* Last one I saw like that was in a man's head. Not a mark on him. No way in, no way out. Sat in the middle of his brain like a stone in a plum. Coroner didn't know what to put. Wrote "delivered".

Nobody says anything. Terry turns off the gas under the bacon.

RAY: To put that there, you've to see it. To see inside a safe you've to be in the black beside it, arm's length. Whoever did this stood beside your safe.

PEG: Nobody stood there. Door bolted, dog in. Nobody's been in this room but family and drivers.

RAY: Not in this room, Mrs Marlow. In there.

Kieran has been standing at the end of the counter with his exercise book clutched to his chest. He opens it, peels a ticket off the page, and holds it out. Not to his mother. To the policeman.

KIERAN: It printed this. Friday. There was nobody on it.

Ray takes it. 02:04 · LITTLE BRIDGE · −59.0 kg.

RAY: You keep these?

KIERAN: Every night. For school.

Ray looks down at the steel plate in the lino, and at the green safe standing at the end of it.

RAY: A plate reads a man in the black as a pull upward. Minus fifty-nine's a man, standing over your scale.

Something passes across his face at the number, and is gone. He takes out his notebook and writes. For a second, over his arm, we see the page above the line he's writing: MAYFAIR · 03:12 · 2ND BODY · 59.0. He shuts the book.

TESS: You've seen that number before.

RAY: I've seen a lot of numbers, Miss Marlow.

He hands the ticket back to Kieran.

RAY: Keep that somewhere safe, son. *(a glance at the safe)* Somewhere safer.

He takes out a clean handkerchief, picks the bullet off the painted pan with it, and folds it into his pocket.

RAY: I'll keep this.

PEG: And the picture?

RAY: I've seen a picture in a safe. Couldn't tell you which way the light fell.

Terry puts a mug in front of him. Hot water, no tea bag. Ray drinks it standing, the way other men drink tea.

RAY: Whoever stood there can stand anywhere. By your bed. By his.

Peg looks at the plate, at the safe, and at the stairs up to where Kieran sleeps.

PEG: Fetch his bed down.

KIERAN: What?

PEG: You're sleeping in here. *(She taps the steel plate with her slipper.)* Nobody stands near that boy without the scale knowing.

RAY: It won't know till the morning.

PEG: Then I'll know in the morning.

Ray puts the mug down. At the public door he stops with his hand on the bolt.

RAY: You'll want to answer this. Don't.

He lets himself out. Peg shoots the bolt behind him, hard.

### Scene 3. The A13 at Grays, eastbound, Tuesday, 3 p.m.

Rain, spray, lorries. Rosie's white van runs in the slow lane, the yard's own gravel heaps coming up on the right. Her lips move: a sum. On the passenger seat her coat is bundled over something, and a collar strap with a brass disc on it pokes out from under the hem.

Blue lights flicker in the grille of the grey car behind her. She swears softly and pulls into a lay-by beside a shut burger van.

Ray comes to her window with rain on his shoulders. She winds it down.

RAY: Nearside brake light.

ROSIE: Is it.

RAY: It is now. *(He looks past her at the coat on the seat, and the strap.)* Licence for that?

ROSIE: It's for the pit. Training.

RAY: Then the pit'll have to manage.

He walks round, opens the passenger door, pulls her coat aside and takes the rig: belt, collar, wrist unit, the brass disc swinging on its strap. He tears a property receipt off a pad and hands it to her through the window.

ROSIE: That's worth more than the van.

RAY: Then you'll want it back. *(beat)* Hear somebody emptied a cold room over in Gravesend last week. Never opened the door. Cold work, that.

ROSIE: Cold-water swimming's more my pace.

RAY: So I hear.

He looks at her face a beat longer than a policeman needs to. There's colour in her cheeks now.

RAY: You look well.

ROSIE: I've eaten.

That seems to puzzle him more than anything she has said. He goes back to his car with her rig under his arm, like a man carrying a cat he doesn't trust.

### Scene 4. The weighbridge office, Tuesday, 9 p.m.

Supper is cleared. Terry is at the sink. DANNY (41), still grey with concrete dust from the pour, holds the property receipt at arm's length as if it smells.

DANNY: A rig's more than a car.

TESS: More than that van. And Terry's tubs. And the gas.

PEG: We're always short.

TESS: We're shorter.

DANNY: *(the receipt down on the table)* Someone stood in my mum's kitchen and left a bullet.

PEG: Not tonight.

DANNY: When?

PEG: When I know who.

DANNY: Aherns.

PEG: When I *know*.

DANNY: *(to Tess)* Their tow. The times.

TESS: Out at half one. Past our wharf just gone two. Back for four. Every night but Sunday.

DANNY: *(to Kieran)* Your ticket. The time on it.

KIERAN: Four past two. Little bridge. Minus fifty-nine.

DANNY: Just gone two. He came off that boat, stood in my mother's kitchen, and rode away down the river for his breakfast.

TESS: If he came off the boat.

DANNY: He came off the boat.

Nobody argues with the face he's making. Behind them LIAM (19) and SHAUN (26) come down the stairs with Kieran's camp bed between them, bumping it round the turn, and set it up in the corner at the end of the little bridge. Its head is a foot from the green safe. Peg brings down the good blankets, the ones from the airing cupboard that nobody's allowed, and makes it up with hospital corners. Kieran watches her do it like a boy watching his first tent go up. He puts his exercise book under the pillow.

Liam straightens up from the bed and pulls his belt a notch tighter. The notch is new: a hole punched through the leather with a nail, rough at the edges. Rosie sees it. Liam sees her see it, and grins, and she looks away first.

On the radio on the counter, the calm BBC voice: "...and the gravity teams report another next-door event in the Thames estuary overnight. Too small to feel. In sport tonight..." Nobody listens.

**Later.** The office in darkness. The display on the little bridge is dark; only the printer's small green light shows. The hatch is down. Upstairs a floorboard settles.

Kieran lies on the camp bed in the good blankets, wide awake, looking at the green safe beside his head. Then he turns over on his side so that he faces it.

---

## Act two: Con

### Scene 5. The Ahern kitchen, Gravesend, Thursday, 3 a.m.

A baby crying overhead: CIAN, thin and tired. MAGGIE AHERN (44) comes down the stairs in a dressing gown over a fleece, pushing her hair out of her face. The stove is banked low. The marine radio on the sill murmurs river traffic.

CON AHERN (70s) sits in his chair by the stove, as he sits every night, in his cardigan over his pyjamas. He is thinner than he was a fortnight ago; his wrists are sticks. A mug of hot water with gin in it has gone cold on the chair arm. The yard cordless, taken off its hook for the night the way he always takes it, so the house can't hear him, lies on his knee, its little screen still lit: TEL. The call was never made.

On the table beside him stands a small brown pill bottle with its lid off. It is empty.

MAGGIE: You'll catch your death sat down here.

He doesn't answer. She knows before she touches him. She stands still in the middle of the kitchen for a long moment.

Upstairs, Cian cries.

Her hands do the only thing they know. She fills the pan. She makes up the bottle — water just off the boil, a scoop levelled with a knife, another, another — warms it, and tests it on the inside of her wrist. All the while she doesn't look at the chair.

SIOBHAN *(from the stairs)*: Mum?

SIOBHAN (13) stands halfway down in her parka and pyjamas.

MAGGIE: Take him his bottle, love.

Siobhan comes down for it. She sees Con. She takes the bottle without a word and goes back up. The crying stops.

Maggie crosses to Con and lifts the cordless off his knee, gently, as if it might wake him. The screen goes dark in her hand. She sets it back on its hook by the door.

She sits down at the table opposite him. On the fridge, under the tug magnet, are Aoife's photo and the long curl of printout. She turns the empty bottle round and round on the oilcloth.

MAGGIE: *(quietly)* Made to your hand.

### Scene 6. The yard at Grays, and the Ahern kitchen, Thursday, 7 p.m.

**Grays.** Terry sits on the upturned crate by the bins with one glove off and the cordless phone in his bare hand. On its screen: CON. Beyond the fence the gravel heaps stand pale in the dusk. Out on the river a tug goes by with a lighter in tow.

This time he presses the green button.

**Gravesend.** On its hook by the door, the yard cordless rings. Its little screen says: TEL. Siobhan, doing homework at the kitchen table, looks at it for three rings. Then she picks it up.

SIOBHAN: Hello.

TERRY: Is Con about, love? It's Tel. About the gravy.

Silence on the line.

SIOBHAN: He's dead.

Terry shuts his eyes.

SIOBHAN: Wednesday night, in his chair. He had you up on his phone. Mum found it on his knee.

TERRY: Was it his heart?

SIOBHAN: His tablets were in the tubs. *(beat)* Grandad says the Marlows took the tubs. *(beat)* Are you a Marlow?

Terry looks across the yard at the office window, at the blind with the shapes of his family moving behind it, and the hatch light.

TERRY: Yes, love.

She rings off.

### Scene 7. The weighbridge office, Thursday, 8 p.m.

Supper. The whole firm is at the table: Peg, Danny, Tess, Liam, Shaun, and Kieran at the end with his homework. Rosie has a blue-lidded tub in front of her. Liam has a full plate he's moving round. Sixpence lies under the table where Liam's feet are, hopeful.

Terry comes in from the yard and puts the phone down on the counter. He doesn't go to the stove. He pulls out a chair and sits down at the table, which he never does at supper, and the table notices.

TERRY: Con's dead.

PEG: Con?

TERRY: Con. Gravy Con. *(beat)* Con used to ring me about gravy.

DANNY: Con who?

TERRY: Con Ahern.

Forks stop.

TERRY: Since I turned. He was turned himself, years back. Neither of us could taste a thing. We'd ring up and tell each other what we remembered. He remembered a sausage he had on Margate front when he was a boy better than I remember my wedding.

PEG: You've been on the phone to them.

TERRY: To him.

DANNY: What was it? His heart?

TERRY: His tablets were in the tubs.

Silence. Then Tess puts down her fork.

TESS: The little tub. The one that rattled. Pills in foil, made to his hand. *(beat)* I put them in the stove.

Every head turns to her.

TESS: Their weigher rang me Saturday. I've known since Saturday.

DANNY: And said nothing.

TESS: I didn't have a number for it.

Rosie looks down at the blue-lidded tub in front of her. At her spoon standing in it. It's Con's dinner, and she has eaten it every day for a fortnight. She puts the spoon down on the table. Across from her Terry has a white tub from the Laundry, as he has had every day since the raid, and she understands at last why he never touched the blue ones.

At the end of the table Kieran looks at the stove, at the little glass window with the fire behind it.

PEG: We took their dinner. We never killed anybody.

TESS: And his tablets.

PEG: *(hard)* They put a bullet in my safe. Where that boy sleeps.

DANNY: And now they've a funeral. They'll be coming.

Shaun has sat quiet all through. He taps the table with one finger.

SHAUN: Then we go first. Not a person. Boats. Their three tugs lie up on the pontoon off their quay, back off the tow by four. Ebb starts after. I undo every knot on them in the one go, and the river does the rest. They're a tug firm with no tugs for a week. *(beat)* Nobody dies.

DANNY: Nobody dies.

PEG: *(after a moment, a nod)* Boats.

TESS: Every line, one trip.

SHAUN: Never go back twice.

Terry gets up. He takes his coat off the back of the door.

TERRY: I'll drive.

PEG: You don't go on jobs.

TERRY: I've never seen where he lived.

Peg opens her mouth, and closes it.

The table breaks up. Chairs scrape. Rosie stays sitting with the tub in front of her. After a moment she pulls it back towards her, picks up the spoon, and eats, because she has to, without looking up. Terry, passing behind her with his coat on, rests his gloved hand on her shoulder for a second, and goes out.

---

## Act three: The knots

### Scene 8. The Ahern moorings, Gravesend, Friday, 4:25 a.m.

The river black and wide, turning on the top of the tide. Off the Ahern quay a long floating pontoon, and rafted along it three tugs, back off the night tow, their engines ticking as they cool. Mooring lines run from bow and stern to the pontoon's cleats and bollards. Up the quay, in the Ahern house, a light goes off. Then another.

Next to the yard, a set of public river stairs runs down into the water: worn stone, green with weed. At the top of them the Marlow van waits with its lights off, Terry at the wheel. Down at the waterline stand Shaun, rigged, and Danny, Tess and Liam. Liam holds a torch he has been told not to switch on.

SHAUN: *(low, looking at the tugs)* Round turn and two half hitches on the bow lines. Bowlines on the sterns. Declan's knot. He ties it like he's cross with it.

TESS: Every line.

SHAUN: One trip.

He flexes his fingers once, the way every Marlow does, and steps off the world.

Tess's lips start moving.

**The black.** Sound drops to Shaun's heartbeat and breath. His glow reaches an arm's length and no further. It shows him the inside of the stone step he was standing on, grit and shell in it, and then the river under the pontoon, clear as air in the glow: a flatfish lying on the mud, an old anchor furred with weed, a bicycle wheel. He pulls himself out along the pontoon, small pulls at nothing, and its floats slide past him from the inside, rust and dark water and bolts.

The first cleat. A rope wound round it. In the glow the rope is clear as jelly, every strand, and the knot sits inside itself, loop through loop. Shaun puts his fingers into the knot, not round it but into it, the way Rosie's hand went into the crate at Mayfair, and draws the end back out through the loop as though the rope were smoke. The knot isn't there any more. Neither end of the rope has moved.

The second. The third. He goes along the raft, line to line. A tug's hull slides by from the inside: the engine room, a kettle on a swinging ring, a calendar still open at Christmas, and taped inside a locker door a photograph of a crew on a quay in the sun — six of them squinting, and at the back, laughing, a young woman with a baby on her hip. He doesn't look at them.

The sixth. The seventh. The eighth is the stern line of the last tug, a bowline gone hard as wood with salt and years. It takes him longer to see the way out of it. His fingers hunt. Then they find it, and the loop opens like a hand.

Close on his fingertips: the fizz under the nails.

The ninth line runs to the far bollard, an arm's length beyond where he is. He could turn now. He looks at it. Never go back twice. He reaches, and takes the knot apart, and turns, and finds home, the one pull in the black that means home, and faces it, and steps, a hair slow.

### Scene 9. The river stairs, continuous.

TESS: ...eight...

Shaun lands on the stairs with a gasp and falls sideways into Liam. He's lying wrong.

His right leg ends above the knee.

The end of it is a clean flat plane, as if the leg had been cut on a butcher's slicer: the white ring of bone, the dark meat, the yellow of the fat, all perfectly flat and perfectly dry. For one second nobody moves and nothing bleeds.

Then it does.

Terry is out of the van and down the stairs faster than anyone has ever seen him move. His belt is off before he reaches the bottom. He cinches it high round Shaun's thigh, jams the handle of a spanner through it and twists.

DANNY: Get them gloves off, you can't feel.

TERRY: They stay on.

He twists again. The bleeding slows. Shaun is white and shaking and grinning at the sky.

LIAM: *(from habit)* Straight?

SHAUN: Straight. *(beat)* Did they go?

Behind them, on the black river, in silence, the tugs are leaving. Their lines lie round the cleats and bollards with no knot in them, and one after another the ropes slide off into the water as the ebb takes the hulls. The three tugs swing out, turn their sterns to the town, and go off down the river in the dark together, slowly, like cows walking out of a gate somebody's left open.

DANNY: They went.

Liam gathers Shaun up off the stairs the way he carried the painting in that first morning, like a tray of glass, and carries him up to the van. Terry climbs after them with his gloves red to the wrist.

Tess stays a moment on the bottom step and looks at the place where Shaun came back. There's nothing there. Only water slapping at the stone.

### Scene 10. The Ahern quay, Gravesend, Friday, 6:30 a.m.

Grey light. Across the river a harbour launch is nudging one tug off the mud below Tilbury. A second lies hard against the ferry pier. The third is out in the fairway, turning slowly, with a pilot boat circling it and hooting.

On the pontoon DECLAN AHERN (70, cap) holds up a mooring line he has pulled from the water. Its end is whole. No cut, no fray, no knot.

DECLAN: Not a knot on the river.

JOE AHERN (17, skinny, hoodie) coils another line that came to nothing. Siobhan stands on the quay in her parka.

Maggie walks down the public stairs next to the yard. Halfway down she stops. On the stone at the waterline, where the tide hasn't reached, is a dark smear, and a long drag of it up the steps, and a spanner lying in it.

MAGGIE: Somebody paid for that.

DECLAN: *(from the pontoon)* Not enough.

Maggie looks across the river at Grays, where the gravel heaps are coming pale out of the dawn.

---

## Act four: No margin

### Scene 11. The flooded gravel pit, the following Tuesday, 4 p.m.

The water is cold pewter under a white sky. Rosie kneels at the edge of the pontoon with her phone's stopwatch running. There's no rig on her belt.

Liam stands at the edge in a wetsuit that fitted him at Christmas. Now it hangs in folds at his belly, and his collarbones stand out above the zip like coat-hangers. His goggles are painted black with enamel. He drops in.

**Under the water** it's black, and the bubbles roar. He tumbles. Then he stops fighting. He lets a little breath go, feels which way it runs, and follows it.

He breaks the surface.

ROSIE: Four.

LIAM: Four!

ROSIE: Terry took a month.

LIAM: Again.

He hangs in the water, grinning, his teeth chattering.

LIAM: What's it like? The tingle.

ROSIE: Pins and needles. Only in your nails. It comes about two seconds before you're done.

LIAM: And then you step out.

ROSIE: Then you step out if there's air. *(beat)* If you're in the concrete, you don't. Never. You keep going till there's air.

LIAM: And if there's no air?

ROSIE: There's always air. Tess makes sure.

He grins and goes under. Rosie's lips move as she counts, just like Tess's. At three she opens her mouth to call him out. She doesn't. He breaks the surface.

LIAM: How long?

ROSIE: Four.

Danny comes down the haul road from the plant with concrete to his elbows and stands at the end of the pontoon. He watches his son haul himself out onto the planks, and sees the wetsuit hanging off him.

LIAM: Four seconds, Dad. Find up blind. First time, every time.

DANNY: *(to Rosie)* Is he?

A beat.

ROSIE: Every time.

LIAM: The lock-up. Their money's in it, Shaun says, in a box sunk in the floor. That's Rosie's cure, near as. *(beat)* I'm not the big lad any more, Dad.

Danny looks at him for a long time: the shoulders that used to fill a door, the ribs under the rubber.

DANNY: Yes.

Liam whoops and falls backwards into the pit.

Rosie looks down at her phone. She stops the stopwatch.

### Scene 12. The weighbridge office, Tuesday, 9 p.m.

Shaun lies on the settle by the stove with the stump bandaged and propped on a cushion, feverish and bright-eyed. Terry sits beside him with a bowl of water and a cloth, in his gloves. He dips the cloth and wrings it out.

TERRY: Locum's been. Danny's cash, no names. Three weeks and a stick, he says.

SHAUN: Cheaper than a funeral.

The rest are at the table.

DANNY: Liam's going. The lock-up.

The table goes quiet.

SHAUN: *(from the settle)* Make it worth my leg.

TERRY: He's nineteen.

PEG: Tess was fourteen.

Terry says nothing more. He wrings out the cloth.

TESS: Can he find up?

ROSIE: *(not looking up)* Four seconds. Every time.

Tess nods. Her eyes go to nothing, and she plans aloud, the way she always does, with no paper on the table.

TESS: Lock-up stands on the river wall. Floor's beads, a metre and more. Box is sunk in it by the river side, and that wall's a weigh-wall. Floods at three. You go in from the mud at low water: through the river wall, through the floor to the box. Bags. Back the way you came. Seven dead.

LIAM: River wall's brick. Brick's quick.

TESS: Brick's quick. Beads aren't.

LIAM: Two goes, then? Under three each. Wall'd never know.

TESS: A metre and more of beads, boy. Three seconds gets you halfway to the box and leaves you there. There's no short way to this one.

PEG: Never go back twice anyway.

TESS: Tingle comes, you're back in the brick.

LIAM: Back in the brick.

PEG: Weigh him in the morning.

Peg looks up at the chalk board. LIAM 72.0 is still there, from Kieran's birthday, never wiped. She rubs it out with her thumb.

On the camp bed at the end of the little bridge, Kieran lies with his exercise book on his chest and watches the grown-ups decide.

### Scene 13. The weighbridge office, Wednesday, 4:30 a.m.

Dark at the windows. The printer's small green light. Kieran asleep on the camp bed by the safe. Sixpence in his basket.

The family comes down the stairs quietly: Tess, Danny, Rosie with no rig, Peg in her apron over her nightie. Terry stays upstairs with Shaun. Liam comes last, in Shaun's rig. It fits him. That says everything, and nobody says it.

He steps onto the little bridge in his socks. The printer chatters. On the camp bed Kieran raises his head. Tess tears off the ticket.

TESS: Sixty-two four.

For a second nobody cheers, because of the hour. Then Danny bangs the table with the flat of his hand, and it isn't quiet any more. Peg picks up the chalk and writes LIAM 62.4 on the board, and beside it, slowly, a star. His first. Liam looks at it like a man looking at a medal.

On the camp bed, up on one elbow, Kieran looks at the star too. Then he lies back down.

TESS: Sixty-two four. One and a half of notes coming out. Sixty-three nine. *(beat)* Seven two.

DANNY: Job's seven.

TESS: Two tenths.

ROSIE: It's not much, Tess.

TESS: It's twice what Nan ever gave anyone.

PEG: *(at the stove, lighting the gas)* A tenth was always plenty.

DANNY: Near enough.

TESS: Near enough isn't. Seven two. *(to Rosie)* Margins are for people who can't count.

Tess takes hold of the brass disc on Liam's collar and gives its tab a hard tug, the way a diver checks a partner's belt before the drop.

LIAM: Can I bite it if I'm short?

TESS: Not in the black. Only once you've stood a minute on the wrong side. Then it fires once, for half your seconds. *(beat)* Tab's field's skin-tight. You and what's on you, nothing else. It'll fire in water. Your belt won't. *(beat)* And step out on purpose, it's twenty years.

Liam nods, as if he'd always known.

TESS: Van.

Tess goes out into the dark yard to load the van, and Danny after her. Rosie follows them to the door and stops there. She looks back at her brother at the table. She almost says something. Then she goes out.

Liam is alone at the table with Peg and the dog.

The bacon spits. Peg turns from the stove with a plate piled high: two eggs, three rashers, black pudding, fried bread. She puts it down in front of him.

PEG: Lucky breakfast.

LIAM: Nan...

PEG: You're not going over that water on nothing.

He looks at the plate. He looks up at the star beside his name. Under the table Sixpence's tail thumps the lino, waiting, the way it has waited every morning for three weeks.

Liam picks up his fork.

The camera stays at the table. He eats every bite. Sixpence's tail slows, and stops.

PEG: That's my boy.

A horn in the yard. Liam wipes his mouth, kisses his nan on the cheek, and goes. Peg scrapes the plate clean into the bin, though there's nothing on it to scrape.

On the camp bed, Kieran is asleep again, with his face to the safe.

---

## Act five: The hundredths

### Scene 14. The foreshore below the Ahern salvage lock-up, Gravesend, Wednesday, 5:40 a.m.

First light. Low water. The river has pulled back off a hundred metres of mud that shines like pewter. Above the mud stands a Victorian river wall of soft red brick, green with weed to the tideline, and on top of it the Ahern salvage lock-up: a squat shed of engineering brick with no windows on the river side.

At the foot of the river wall, on the mud: Tess, Danny, Rosie, and Liam in Shaun's rig. Liam has his palm flat on the weed.

DANNY: Straight back.

LIAM: Straight back.

ROSIE: Tingle, you keep going till there's air.

LIAM: I know.

He grins at her, the big lad's grin, in a face that has got too small for it. He flexes his fingers once, the way every Marlow does, and steps off the world.

Tess's lips start.

**The black.** Sound drops to Liam's heartbeat and breath. His glow reaches an arm's length. It shows him the weed from the inside, then the brick: old soft red brick and lime mortar, a clay pipe somebody lost when the wall was new, a dead crab's shell in a gap. He goes through it like a dog through a hedge. Brick's quick.

0.9. 1.3.

Then the floor. Inside it are the beads, packed grey through the grey like frogspawn in jelly, and the going turns thick and slow. He pulls. It's like wading. He knows this stuff; he has barrowed tons of it. His lips move: *not ours*.

1.8. 2.2. A mat of steel mesh slides through his glow, rust-red rods in a grid, and behind him.

2.6. The box. A steel box sunk in the concrete, and inside it, lit through its walls by his own light, bundles of fifties in clear bags. He reaches through the steel and draws the bags out, gently, the way Rosie drew the canvas out of its crate, and holds them flat against his chest.

**The mud.**

TESS: ...two... three. There's the wall.

Through the brick above them comes a roar like a tipper shedding its load. Danny's hands close into fists.

**The black.** Out of the top of Liam's glow comes a silent black hail. Beads pour down into the box's well where his hands were a moment ago and fill it. The room above him is filling. He turns for home with the bags against his chest and starts back through the floor.

3.4. 3.9. The mesh again. 4.4.

4.9.

Close on his fingertips: the fizz under the nails.

His glow is full of beads. Beads ahead of him, beads on every side. At the very edge of the light, ahead, the first course of the brick is only just showing.

Tingle comes, you're back in the brick. He isn't back in the brick.

His face. He knows what Tess said. He knows what Rosie said. Never in the concrete. He pushes.

5.3. The brick. Quick again. He goes through it as fast as he has ever moved in his life.

6.1. 6.5.

The outer face of the wall comes into his glow: the weed on it seen from the inside, pale green, a hand's breadth ahead. Beyond the weed there is only black: the air over the mud, where his dad is standing.

6.9.

He reaches, the bags flat against his chest, both arms round them.

The glow goes out. The heartbeat stops between one beat and the next.

Black. Nothing on the screen at all.

He was two hundredths of a second from home.

### Scene 15. The foreshore, continuous.

TESS: ...six... seven.

Nothing. The river, a long way off, talking to itself.

TESS: ...seven two.

Nothing.

Danny steps up to the wall and puts his palm flat on the weed, where his son went in.

TESS: ...eight.

DANNY: Tess.

TESS: ...nine.

Rosie sits down in the mud, the way you sit when your legs decide for you.

TESS: ...ten. Eleven. Twelve...

She keeps counting. At twenty her voice stops. Her lips go on.

DANNY: *(not turning round, his palm still on the weed)* The bags.

TESS: He had them.

Her lips go on.

Up on the lane a door bangs.

### Scene 16. The Ahern salvage lock-up, Wednesday, 5:50 a.m.

Maggie comes down the river stairs in her coat over her nightclothes. In her hand is a long curl of thermal paper, torn off the printer in her kitchen. Joe comes behind her. At the top of the stairs stands Declan, with a boathook.

Maggie stops on the last step above the mud. Danny turns from the wall and comes at her.

DANNY: Where is he?

Tess steps between them without a word and stands there.

MAGGIE: *(holding up the paper)* It printed all night.

For a moment none of them understands. Then Tess does, and her face goes.

MAGGIE: He's on my wall.

She looks at them: three Marlows on her mud at dawn, and the girl sitting in it. Then she turns and goes back up the stairs, and after a moment they follow her.

She unlocks the lock-up's steel door and swings it open. Inside is a salvage store: chains, a ship's bell, coils of rope, a brass porthole on a nail. Half the floor is buried under black tungsten beads, heaped like spilled coal over the place where the box is sunk. In the river-side wall is a plain steel panel the size of a door: the weigh-wall. There is nothing on it to read.

Declan fills the doorway behind them.

DECLAN: Not in my lock-up.

MAGGIE: Dad.

Just that. He looks at the panel. He looks at the Marlows. Then he goes.

Joe stays at the door, holding it open, not looking at anyone. He doesn't look at Danny at all.

MAGGIE: It's a minute. That's all you get. Then you go home.

She looks at her watch.

They stand. Danny in front of the panel, his hands hanging. Tess beside him with her eyes shut, counting it, because that's the only way she can stand there. Rosie next to her father, and she can't look at him. She looks at the beads. Out on the river a gull cries, and stops.

A minute.

MAGGIE: *(tearing the last length off the curl in her hand)* It'll read him a month. Softer every day. Then it won't.

As she folds it we see the last line printed on it: 65.1 KG.

MAGGIE: You'll want his reading. Your line of work.

TESS: I know what he weighed.

Maggie looks at her, one weigher to another. Then she holds the fold out to Danny.

He takes it. He doesn't look at it. He folds it again, smaller, and puts it in his wallet, behind his driving licence.

Rosie goes out past Joe into the light.

### Scene 17. The weighbridge office, Wednesday, 7:10 a.m.

The hatch is down. Kieran is pulling his school jumper over his head by the camp bed. Peg is at the stove with the kettle, and the fry-up pan is still on the back ring.

Outside, the van rolls onto the big bridge. The printer chatters. Peg tears the ticket off out of habit and pushes it onto the spike, on top of the one that printed at five, when the van went out.

She looks at the two tickets. Then she takes the top one off the spike again and holds it beside the other, one in each hand, and reads them both.

She sits down on the bottom stair.

KIERAN: Nan?

The door opens. Danny. Tess. Rosie, with mud to her hips. Nobody else.

Danny stands in the doorway. His eyes go up the wall, past Frank's photo, to a smaller frame beside it: a young woman laughing on a beach, years ago. He looks at it a long moment. Kieran follows his gaze, and looks at his uncle.

PEG: *(from the bottom stair, quietly)* Their mother's been in Grays churchyard since Liam was four. This house doesn't say her name.

On the board, in fresh chalk: LIAM 62.4, and the star.

Nobody wipes it.

### Scene 18. The weighbridge office, Wednesday night.

Dark. The whole house is in bed, or pretending. The display on the little bridge is dark; the printer's small green light glows. The kettle ticks as it cools.

Kieran is awake on the camp bed. He gets up in his socks and stands on the little bridge. The printer chatters. He tears off the ticket and reads it by the green light: 39.2.

He sits on the edge of the camp bed and opens his exercise book. Down the page runs the column of his test weight, night after night: 19.78, 19.78, 19.78, 19.78. With his school ruler he draws a second column beside it, heads it *me*, and writes in it: 39.2.

He puts the book under the pillow and lies down facing the safe. After a while his breathing finds the slow of sleep.

**Later.** Footsteps on the stairs.

Danny comes down in the dark in his vest. He doesn't put the light on. He steps onto the little bridge. The printer chatters. He tears off the ticket and reads it by the green light.

DANNY: Eighty-eight.

He looks at the green safe, and at the wall clock above it, and down at the steel plate under his feet.

DANNY: *(very quietly)* Two seconds is enough to grab someone.

His right hand opens and closes at his side, opens and closes, in the green light.

**END OF EPISODE 5**

---

## Plants and payoffs

**Continuity.** Episode 4 ends on Saturday 11 April (its quake morning is canonically Saturday 4 April, and its scenes run Saturday to Saturday). In the ten days between, the family lives on the stolen blue tubs — "the best part of a fortnight" — and the stones go to the fence. School goes back; this one runs from Tuesday 21 April (the bullet, Ray, the A13) through Thursday 23 (Con found at 3 a.m., Terry's call, the table) and Friday 24 (the knots, before dawn) to Tuesday 28 (the pit, Danny's yes) and Wednesday 29 (the weigh-in, the lock-up, 39.2). Episode 6's "a week later" falls about Wednesday 6 May.

**Paid off from earlier**
- **The 02:04 ticket (4):** a man in the black over the plate, pulling it up. The bullet is what he delivered. Kieran's bed moves to the office.
- **The painting in the safe, reached only by a sent hand (1, 2):** Peg forces the old key, "Not since Blair." Tess's "Pans are level" (1): a bullet now stands on one pan, and nobody says so.
- **Ray's "I'll keep that" (3):** his notebook shows MAYFAIR · 03:12 · 2ND BODY · 59.0 as he writes down −59.0. Only the audience sees the match. It feeds the spinner theory.
- **Maggie's call and the burned pills (4):** Con dies with an empty bottle; Tess confesses she has known since Saturday. **Terry's secret (4):** "Con used to ring me about gravy... Con Ahern." His unpressed call is pressed; Con died with TEL on his screen, never rung. Terry's refusal of the blue lids: Rosie sees why.
- **Danny's near miss, "Nearly's nothing" (4):** Shaun reaches for one more knot and leaves a leg.
- **"Blood pays blood" (3, 4):** each answer is meant to be kinder than the last (boats, not people; money, not people) and each costs more.
- **Aoife's month on the lock-up wall, and the family's minute (4):** Liam runs out on the same wall. "Not in my lock-up" and "Dad" play for an audience that knows why. The Marlows don't. Her photograph in the tug locker pays the fridge photo: the crew on a sunlit quay, Aoife laughing at the back, the baby on her hip.
- **Tess's tow times (4: "out at half one, comes past ours just after two"):** Danny puts them beside Kieran's ticket and does his sum. Episode 6's stakeout is deduced on screen, not asserted.
- **"Everything in you counts, breakfast too" (1):** the lucky breakfast after the weigh-in. Nobody says it.
- **Rosie's coaching, the plate to the dog, "That's my boy" (4):** the nail-hole in the belt, the wetsuit, Shaun's rig that fits, Sixpence's tail that stops, "That's my boy" over a clean plate.
- **Terry's "They let the family stand a minute by the reading" (1):** the minute, given by the enemy.
- **Sixpence's growl (1–3)** keeps its run, at Terry, first thing. Nobody explains it any more, which is worse now the family knows what it means.
- **"Near enough isn't" (planted in 3); "frogspawn in jelly" and Liam's handprint in a wall (3); "like a tray of glass" (1, 3); "cold-water swimming" (3) and Ray's "So I hear" (2).** World rule paid: police don't cuff a crosser, they take the rig.

**Planted for later**
- **Liam's sums (9).** 62.4, 1.5 of notes, 63.9, 7.2 and "Job's seven" said aloud; "Tingle comes, you're back in the brick." The tingle comes at 4.9, in the beads. The fry-up is seen only by Peg and the audience. The bags are held flat against his chest at his last reach, and registered once on the mud ("The bags." / "He had them."), so the loss of the cure money is on screen and the wall's 65.1 can be laid out clean in 9. Danny folds the printout unread, and Tess declines to hear it ("I know what he weighed").
- **The bite's half-seconds (9).** Tess's tab lesson now says it plain: "Then it fires once, for half your seconds." Rosie's bite in the Deep brings her home on 5.4, half of 10.8, and the audience has already been told the tariff.
- **Danny's tow arithmetic (6).** The Ahern night tow past the wharf at just gone two, the 02:04 ticket, and "He came off the boat": the stakeout's premise, made on screen. Tess's "If" is left standing, which is what episode 6's blind step into the black costs.
- **Peg's "A tenth was always plenty" (10):** "7.1" on Frank's ticket.
- **Kieran on the camp bed by the safe (6, 9);** his second column, headed *me*, 39.2 (6–9). He watched Liam win a star for 62.4. When Danny comes down to weigh himself and say what two seconds is enough for, the boy is asleep. His test-weight column is still flat at 19.78; the wobble starts in 6.
- **Danny: 88, "Two seconds is enough to grab someone" (6),** said alone in the dark under the wall clock above the safe.
- **Rosie's rig is with Ray (6, 9):** Tess must cross herself; the one rig left on the rack in 9 is Tess's old one. The property receipt is paper with Rosie's name on it.
- **Ray keeps the bullet, returns the ticket, warns "Don't" (7, 9), and is puzzled that Rosie looks well (8:** he knows the Laundry sends only enough for one).
- **The drawer (9, 10):** the Trading Standards envelope on top; a pale green corner with a printed hand, the 2019 Register letter. **The padlock key on orange baler twine** on Peg's safe-key ring (10: the Mucking gate).
- **"Another next-door event in the Thames estuary" (8).**
- **Shaun's leg, above the knee, left in the black;** "Make it worth my leg" (9: "Eleven tenths"); the locum's "three weeks and a stick", paid in cash, which times his return in 7 and keeps the world's medicine around the family's flesh, never around its crossings.
- **Terry's "They stay on" (10):** gloves even for a wound, unexplained.
- **Two van tickets on the spike (10):** the rolls hold the van going out, and coming back lighter by a boy.
- **Maggie (6, 7):** the minute, the printout, "Softer every day", and one weigher offering another the number ("You'll want his reading") — the seed of the ferry. Her decency is the seed of the envelope Joe carries in 6. **Joe at the lock-up door, not looking at Danny (6).** **Declan, "Not enough" (epilogue):** he never changes.
- **Con's phone is the yard cordless (4's continuity note honoured):** off its hook at night so the house can't hear him, back on its hook when Terry rings, so Siobhan answers the same phone her uncle died holding.
- **The second frame (texture only):** a young woman beside Frank's photo, Danny's glance, Peg's one line. Pays nothing this season.

**Gaps.** *Opened:* why Liam ran out when every number said he'd make it. *Kept open:* which Ahern stood by the safe (the family has the boat and the hour; the audience has the Mayfair match). *Closed:* how the bullet was delivered (off the night tow, at two past two); who Con was, for the family.

**Knowledge at the end.** The family knows Con was an Ahern, that Tess burned his pills, and that Danny believes a man came off the Ahern tow at 02:04. Only the audience knows about the fry-up, Ray's Mayfair match and Kieran's second column. Only Danny and the audience know what he weighs and what he means to do with two seconds.

**Numbers.** Canon only: 02:04, −59.0; the tow out at half one, past the wharf just after two, back for four; 62.4, 1.5, 63.9, 7.2, 7.0, two tenths, 65.1, two hundredths; 72.0 on the board; Danny's 88; Kieran's 39.2 and 19.78; Mayfair 03:12 and 59.0; the wall at three; a tenth; twenty years. Derived, with nothing resting on them: Liam's wrist-unit tenths, the tingle at 4.9 (about two seconds before his true end at 6.98, as in episodes 1 and 3). Talk only: the locum's three weeks, three tugs.


# Episode 6: Ten Seconds

*Danny gambles his hand to turn an enemy, and Tess breaks into a clinic for a cure and finds a clock.*

---

## Cold open

### Scene 1. The weighbridge office, Grays, Wednesday, ten past one in the morning.

A week since the river wall. The office is dark except for the small green light of the ticket printer and the grey square of the blind where the yard lights lie. The house above is asleep. On the camp bed at the end of the little bridge, a foot from the green safe, KIERAN (12) sleeps the sleep of a boy who has been allowed to stay up too many nights, his face turned to the safe, the way he has slept since Peg moved him down here.

At the table, in the dark, sits DANNY MARLOW (41), and he is not asleep. His coat is folded over the chair back, his boots under it, because everything counts. He is in his vest and trousers and socks, and buckled over all of it is his rig: the padded belt, the collar with its brass disc at the throat, the wrist unit reading zero. His right sleeve is rolled to the elbow.

He has done the sum, and the sum is all in his head, because that is how this family keeps things. The Ahern tow goes out at half one. It passes the Marlow wharf just gone two. The night the bullet came, a man came off that tow, stood in the black beside this safe, and left a bullet standing on a painted balance pan, a foot from where a boy sleeps now. Seventh night. If the tow runs, the man comes again. Six nights he has stepped in at four past by the clock and found the black empty. Six crossings, two seconds each, off the plate, and nobody told.

The scale's display is dark. There is nothing in this room to watch but the clock.

DANNY: *(under his breath, to the dark)* Wait for the printer and the lad's gone. The plate prints after a load settles. I have to be in there first.

At half one, far off on the river, a tow's horn, twice. Danny's eyes stay on the clock. The fridge hums. The clock ticks.

02:03. He stands up without a sound. He crosses the dark lino round the edge of the plate — the steel stays empty — and stands in the narrow gap between the safe and the dresser, just off the plate's end, an arm's length from where a man would come. He flexes the fingers of his right hand once, the way every Marlow does.

02:04. He steps off the world.

**The black.** Sound drops to his heartbeat and his breath. His glow reaches an arm's length round him, grey-gold, and past it there is nothing at all. His ears scream that he's falling. He knows that voice. There is no down to fall to.

The safe's wall goes up past him like a cliff seen through water. The plate's steel lies a stride from his toes, and he is not on it. At the edge of the glow, on the camp bed, the boy sleeps on, his face turned to the safe.

And then, into the glow, out of the black — a body at the safe.

Slight. Young ribs, narrow as staves. A heart going fast, a bird's heart. And through the safe's steel, lit the way everything inside the glow is lit, a white envelope lies on the flat of the painting, and the fingers that let it go are still sliding back out of the steel.

Danny's hand goes up.

Close on his fingertips as the fizz starts under the nails.

The heart is two fingers from his fingertips, beating like a sparrow's, and for one beat, two beats, his hand hangs there with the count dying in his nails.

Then he closes it on the bare wrist above the sleeve, and wrenches.

Home. The one pull in the black. He faces it, and steps.

**The office.** A gasp. The smell of the room: bacon fat gone cold, printer ink, dog. On the camp bed the boy sleeps on, face to the safe.

DANNY: Got him.

He raises his right hand into the green light.

The four fingers are gone. Off clean across the knuckles: a flat, dry plane, white bone and dark meat and yellow fat, all perfectly smooth, bloodless.

For a second.

Then not.

He closes his left hand round his own wrist above the plane and holds it hard against his chest. His eyes go to the camp bed, a foot and a half of dark away. The boy's breathing doesn't change. He will not have the boy see it first.

He fights the buckle of his rig belt one-handed, gets it loose, and cinches the belt round his forearm above the wound, twisting it tight and trapping the twist under his knee. Terry's trick, from the river stairs, done alone and badly.

Then the tea towel off the rail, wrapped over all of it. He sits down at the long table under the chalk board, where LIAM 62.4 and its star are still chalked and nobody has wiped them, and he waits for morning without waking a soul.

The clock ticks over to 02:05.

**MAIN TITLE: SECONDS**

---

## Act one: breakfast

### Scene 2. The weighbridge office, Wednesday, half past six.

TERRY (66) comes down first, in his dressing gown and his yellow gloves, and puts the kettle on out of habit before he sees his nephew at the table with the towel gone rust-brown around the knuckles. The kettle goes on anyway.

PEG (72) follows, in her dressing gown. Then ROSIE (22) on the stairs, and TESS (38) behind her, and Kieran sitting up on the camp bed with his hair on end.

Danny crosses the room to the table, and SIXPENCE gets up out of his basket and growls at him. The low, steady note. The one he has given Terry for five years.

The kitchen goes very still, because they all know that note now.

PEG: Danny.

DANNY: Put the kettle on, Mum.

Peg goes to the dresser and takes her glucose meter out of the cutlery drawer, from among the spoons. Danny sits and holds out his hand, the good one, and she pricks his finger, and squeezes the bead of blood onto the strip, and waits for the beep the way she has waited for it every morning of her own life.

The screen reads **LO**.

Nobody speaks. Peg looks at the meter, and at her son, and her hand goes out, not to him but to the fridge — to the shelf of white tubs that came in the Laundry van, the only food in this house her son can now eat — and stops on the door handle.

PEG: *(to Terry, quietly)* How much of yours is there?

TERRY: It's ours, Peg.

She opens the fridge anyway, takes out a tub and a spoon, and puts them down in front of Danny. Then she stands there with her hand flat on the table, because there is nothing else her hands know how to do.

TESS: You went alone.

DANNY: You'd have had sums against it.

TESS: I told you. Skin, or it's nothing. I told you what it costs.

DANNY: He stood over Kieran's bed.

Tess has no number for that. Her lips move, and nothing comes off them.

TESS: How long had you?

DANNY: Two point four.

TESS: It comes two seconds before you're done.

DANNY: I know when it comes.

Her lips go on moving a moment longer, the way they do when a number won't settle, and then they stop, because there is no number here this morning.

TESS: Who was it?

DANNY: A lad. Light build. Wrist like a birch twig.

On the camp bed, Kieran has the night's tickets in his hand — he tore them off the printer the moment he saw the grown-ups' faces. He holds one out. 02:04 · LITTLE BRIDGE · −59.0 KG.

KIERAN: It printed him again.

TESS: Fifty-nine. Just the one on it. *(beat)* Maggie's boy. The one at the lock-up door.

DANNY: *(quiet)* Younger than my Liam.

Nobody says anything to that either.

DANNY: He'll be home for his breakfast.

Nobody laughs.

ROSIE: Coin says which.

TERRY: Yeah.

Terry fetches the bowl, the warm water, the clean towels. He dresses the hand on the table, working in his gloves, and nobody asks why the gloves. The plane across the knuckles is smooth as a chisel cut. The thumb he keeps. Four fingers, gone, and no wound anywhere else on him, and no fingers anywhere in the room.

TERRY: Locum at nine. Cash.

Peg nods. Terry ties off the dressing, tucks the ends in neat as a butcher's parcel — and then, without a word, he reaches over and slides his blue enamel pot across the table to Danny.

Danny looks at it a long moment. Then he takes the lid off, and eats, a spoonful at a time, the way Rosie eats, the way Terry eats.

ROSIE: *(quietly, across the table)* Little and often, Dad.

DANNY: I know how to eat.

ROSIE: You know how to make weight. This is different.

He looks at her. She holds his eyes, because she is the only person at this table who knows what she is talking about. He takes another spoonful.

Sixpence growls again, low, from the basket.

DANNY: Jealous old bugger.

TERRY: *(gently)* No, love.

Danny stops chewing for one beat. Then he goes on.

Tess, at the counter, does the only thing she can do with any of it. She counts.

TESS: One mouth was three hundred a day. Then two. *(beat)* Three's nine hundred. Six thousand three hundred a week, and he'll want it front.

DANNY: How much is in the house?

TESS: Thirty-eight days.

She doesn't say the money. She has never not said a number before.

DANNY: The lock-up job was the cure. That box under the beads.

Nobody answers that either. Peg looks round the kitchen — the fridge, the pot, her son eating with one hand — and says the family's own words back to it, flat as a ticket.

PEG: We feed our own.

Out in the yard the first lorry sounds at the gate. Kieran is dressed already, school bag on, and he stands at the door a second too long, looking at his uncle.

DANNY: Bus.

KIERAN: Dad —

DANNY: *Bus.*

He goes. Through the window we see him stop in the yard and look back once.

### Scene 3. The weighbridge office, Wednesday, nine.

The locum, a tired man with a bag, meets Danny at the yard door and examines the dressing under the awning.

LOCUM: Guillotine. Who did the first dressing?

TERRY: Me.

LOCUM: It's good work. *(He repacks his bag.)* You people are going to run out of parts.

Peg pays him in notes from the float tin in the dresser, and he goes. Then she takes her key ring to the safe — the long brass key, and the small padlock key on its loop of orange baler twine gone pale — and kneels down on the steel plate to open it. Under the counter the printer chatters out her weight. Nobody tears it off.

The lock gives with its clack like a knuckle.

The cash box. The brown envelope. And lying flat on *The Assayer's Wife*, face up on the painted woman's balance, on the very pan where the bullet stood, a white envelope.

In square capitals: **TESS MARLOW**.

Peg lifts it off with two fingers and holds it out, unopened.

PEG: From them.

DANNY: Burn it.

PEG: It's got her name on.

Tess takes it. She turns it over once. The back is blank. She puts it in the inside pocket of her coat, which hangs on the chair, and looks at nobody while she does it.

### Scene 4. The Ahern kitchen, Gravesend, Wednesday, six in the morning.

The tide book lies open on the oilcloth at the Nobody columns. Two mugs of tea, one gone cold. CIAN grizzles overhead, thin, and stops. MAGGIE AHERN (44) sits with her coat on over her nightclothes, as she has sat since half one.

The door opens. JOE (17), white to the lips, the river cold still on him.

She is up before the door shuts. She takes his wrist — the wrist — and turns it to the lamp. Whole. Unmarked. She turns it over anyway.

JOE: He was waiting in there. In the black. At the safe. *(beat)* He had hold of me, Mum, and then he was holding nothing.

MAGGIE: And the letter?

JOE: Went in. I let it go in the box. *(beat)* Then he took hold.

Maggie lets his wrist go. She looks down at the tide book, and her finger finds the Nobody column and runs down it, the way other people say a prayer.

MAGGIE: I rang that house once and got a dial tone. Post a letter there and it goes in a drawer. A thing in that safe gets read. *(beat)* Thursday fortnight, then.

JOE: They know the tow now. They know it's me.

MAGGIE: I know. *(She closes the book.)* Half one. You're on her.

JOE: Grandad says —

MAGGIE: Your grandad says a lot. *(not unkindly)* Bed.

He goes up. Maggie stands at the window, across the black water, at the orange glow over Grays, and does not move for a long time.

---

## Act two: the records room

### Scene 5. The weighbridge office, Wednesday evening.

The lorries are parked up and the hatch is down. On the long table: the last of the money in thin piles, and the Obverse leaflet, soft at its creases, with £180,000 printed on it and LISBON. HALF in biro underneath.

DANNY: Aherns keep their money in that lock-up. What's left of it. I'll —

TESS: You're not crossing.

DANNY: I've still got a hand.

TESS: You've got two point four seconds and one hand. You're not crossing.

DANNY: Then what do we do? Starve politely?

TERRY: *(quietly, from the stove)* Two, then. Rosie and him. *(beat)* I've had my eleven goes.

TESS: *(not arguing with him, because there is nothing to argue)* Three hundred and sixty thousand. There's nothing in this country pays that but years. *(beat)* So we don't pay it.

PEG: We don't go to them.

TESS: We're not going to them for help.

Peg looks at her daughter across the kitchen.

TESS: The method's written down somewhere. Price lists, deliveries, how it's done. Whatever a hundred and eighty thousand buys, there's a paper for it under the price.

PEG: *(a long beat; then, because it is her own line, from the wall, in a frame)* Paper talks.

ROSIE: *(quietly, from the stairs)* There's a room marked records.

Every head comes round.

ROSIE: At the clinic. I asked for the toilet.

TESS: You asked for the toilet.

ROSIE: Course I did. *(counting it off on her fingers)* Black door, three steps. Reception desk. Corridor past her room — Osei's. Stairs down, lower ground. Toilet's under the stairs. And past it a steel door. RECORDS. STAFF ONLY. There's a keypad on it. And on the wall by the toilet, one of them steel panels. Like the one in their lock-up. Area steps. Their door's below the pavement.

TERRY: That's their wall. It floods at three.

TESS: I'm two and a half. Not a tenth more.

DANNY: Keypad doesn't matter.

ROSIE: I know it doesn't matter. I'm telling you where it is.

TESS: The cabinet?

ROSIE: Didn't see in. Door was shut.

DANNY: Strongroom furniture. It'll be steel, and it'll be bolted through the slab. Tall as Terry.

TERRY: Flattered.

Tess is already walking it, lips moving: area steps, one wall, half left, first door.

TESS: Cabinet on the left going in.

ROSIE: The wall you face coming out.

TESS: Say it back.

Rosie says it back, word perfect.

Peg goes to the steel rack by the back door, where the rigs hang on hooks like coats. Rosie's is gone into a police evidence store in Chelmsford. Shaun's went under the river wall with Liam. Danny's belt is on Danny. Two left: Terry's, and an older one, its strap gone cracked.

Tess takes down her own rig.

PEG: Not you.

TESS: Rosie's got no rig. Danny's got one hand. Shaun's got one leg. *(buckling it on)* Terry's turned, and I'm not sending anything else in the black for this family. Not this month.

TERRY: *(going over the buckles with his gloved hands, thorough)* Charge is sound. Collar's sound. You want the strap seen to after.

TESS: After.

She steps onto the little bridge in her jersey and socks. The display is dark after hours; the printer chatters. Terry tears off the ticket. 61.0.

TESS: Seven point eight. *(beat)* I'll take two and a half.

TERRY: That's five seconds of change.

TESS: First go in twelve years. I'll have the change.

PEG: Face home.

TESS: Face home.

From the camp bed, where he has been pretending to read, Kieran puts his book down. He doesn't say anything. On his way to the stairs he steps onto the little bridge — one beat, in his socks — and off, and up. The printer chatters behind him. Nobody tears the ticket off; every eye in the room is on the rig and the woman buckling it. And he goes without arguing, which is not like him, and nobody notices that either.

### Scene 6. Harley Street, Thursday, ten to two in the morning.

Night. The black door with its brass plate, OBVERSE, and the fanlight dark above it. A minicab rank, one car idling. A man walks a terrier along the area railings.

Round the corner, in a side street, the white van sits with its lights off. Terry at the wheel. The window down an inch.

TERRY: Ten minutes and I'm coming through the shopfront.

TESS: You'll count to a hundred and fifty. I'll be out at ninety.

She gets out, crosses to the railings, and goes down the area steps to the lower-ground wall, where she stands like a woman waiting to be late for an appointment. She flexes her fingers once, and steps off the world.

**The black.** It comes back to her body like an old song. Her ears scream falling; she greets the scream like a bus she's caught a thousand times. The glow opens round her, an arm's length of grey-gold, and the wrist unit counts tenths, and her lips move, and she goes straight in, level, one wall, the way you'd walk from a passage into a kitchen.

0.4. The area wall from the inside: brick, then a coal chute, a mouse run in old lath. 0.9. The lower corridor, warm, and the shapes in it: a desk from the inside, its lock, a lamp's wiring lying in the wood like veins. On the wall a plain steel panel the size of a door, with nothing on it to read. It doesn't care what her budget is. It counts to three.

1.2. The cabinet. Steel, floor-bolted, tall as Terry, exactly as Danny said, and through its door the spines stand clear as through water: LEDGER A–F. LEDGER G–M. SUPPLIERS. KITCHENS. PROTOCOL.

1.6. The wall counts to three, and the wall doesn't count her change.

1.7. She draws the PROTOCOL binder out through the steel, the way Rosie drew the canvas out of its crate, and holds it flat against her chest.

No fizz in her nails. Home is one pull, the strongest thing in the world. She faces it, goes back the way she came, and steps out into the area.

A gasp. Exhaust, cold, the hiss of a night bus overhead. She comes up the area steps, round the corner to the van, and Terry's window is down.

TERRY: Straight?

TESS: Straight.

She can't know that.

---

## Act three: the protocol

### Scene 7. The van, a side street off Harley Street, continuous.

A streetlamp over the bonnet. Terry drives them round the corner and parks under it, and kills the engine.

TERRY: I'll be at the corner. Watching the street.

He gets out and walks to the corner, and stands there, a big shape under the next lamp, and the terrier man passes him going the other way.

Tess opens the binder on her knees. Cloth-bound, OBVERSE stamped in the corner.

The first sheet is in a plastic sleeve, typed like a recipe. She reads it aloud, low, to nobody, the way she counts, so someone's counting.

TESS: Transit ten point nought seconds. Expected restoration nought point seven nought. *(beat)* Clinician in transit: less than two seconds. No contact.

She turns pages, and stops. An annex: two columns, hand-ruled, filled in ink. Seconds down one side. Odds down the other. She reads it out loud, flat, the way she reads a shopping list.

TESS: Two seconds: none ever recorded. *(beat)* Three: one in five thousand. Four: one in twelve. Five: one in six. Six: one in four. Seven: one in three. Eight: one in two. Nine: three in five. Ten: seven in ten.

Silence in the van. A taxi goes by outside, lit up yellow. At the corner, Terry doesn't turn round.

She sits with it a moment. Then she squares the pages, and shuts the binder, and holds it flat on her knees with both hands.

Terry comes back. He opens the door and gets in, and looks at her face, and then straight ahead, and doesn't ask.

TERRY: Well?

TESS: It's a crossing. Ten seconds in the black, under their roof. That's the whole of it. That's what the money buys.

A long quiet. Terry looks down the street at the dark clinic, and something goes out of his face that has been in it for five years.

TERRY: Eleven times I went in. On two seconds.

TESS: I know, Tel.

TERRY: You can't buy nothing with two seconds.

He starts the engine. As they pull out he starts, very quietly, half-singing the old washing-day song, the one he sings when there is nothing to say, and Tess lets him sing it. She holds the binder flat on her knees with both hands, all the way over the river.

---

## Act four: the sums

### Scene 8. Dr Osei's consulting room, Harley Street, Thursday, nine in the morning.

The waiting room, the orchids, the hush. The RECEPTIONIST looks up, her right hand moving on the keyboard, her left hand very still beside it.

TESS: No appointment. Tell her it's about her records room.
