Danny's good hand goes up first.

DANNY: I said I'd sort it.

Shaun's hand. Terry's gloved hand.

TERRY: I'll drive. (beat) Nobody's sending me in on my seconds, mind. Twos I'll do. And I'll drive.

From the camp bed, Kieran's hand comes half up off the comic. Peg looks at it. It comes down again. Nobody asks him. Rosie's hand never moves. Tess isn't asked at all.

PEG: Carried.

She tucks the bootlace with the safe key back under the neck of her apron, and Tess, watching her mother's face do exactly what it did when the Twitch was on the radio, picks up her coat.

At the door Tess stops once.

TESS: The Register feeds anybody. He said the words I'd say, so I'll say them: walk her in.

PEG: Out, Tess.

Rosie, alone at the table with the detective's dinner, pulls the tub towards her, peels the lid, and eats.

### Scene 8. A car park by the river, Grays, Tuesday, eleven at night.

Tess's car, engine off, lights off, the river talking to itself beyond the wall. The orange glow of the town on the clouds. She dials.

TESS: (into the phone) Is that directory? I want a number. Handedness Register. It'll be a night line. (beat; then, to a new voice) Is that the night line? (beat) Yes. I want to report a turned woman. Unclaimed. She's starving. (beat) No, she'll come. She's past saying no. (beat) Marlow Aggregates. The public weighbridge, Grays. (beat) No. No name of mine to give.

She rings off. She sits with both hands on the wheel, and her lips move, and no number comes off them, the way no number came at the pit when her son asked how long.

TESS: (to the windscreen) You're not going in the black for a box of porridge.

### Scene 9. The yard, Marlow Aggregates, Wednesday, ten past six in the morning.

A green minibus comes up the lane and turns in the gate, HANDEDNESS REGISTER and a small printed hand on its side. Three crew in plain green smocks get out: two women, one man, a cool bag, a clipboard. No police. The CHIEF (50s, kind, no nonsense) knocks at the office door, and when Peg opens it, doesn't come in.

CHIEF: Somebody rang the night line. We don't take nobody that won't come, Mrs Marlow. There's a bed, there's a kitchen, and no lock on the door either way.

Behind Peg, at the table, the family has assembled itself out of the morning: Danny, Terry, Shaun, Kieran in his school shirt. Rosie is already on the stairs in her coat. She heard the diesel.

She looks at the chalk board. PURFLEET. Then at Peg.

PEG: You'll be fed.

ROSIE: Yeah.

Rosie kisses Danny's cheek. She touches the top of Kieran's head, the way Tess does, which makes nobody look at anybody. At the gate, past the green minibus, stands Tess's car, and Tess beside it, come to see it done.

Rosie stops level with her.

ROSIE: Nine point four, Tess.

Tess takes it the way you take a slap you've been owed for years, standing still, hands at her sides. The minibus rolls out of the gate, over the big bridge; the printer chatters and prints its weight; and at the office window a boy with an exercise book watches it go.

### Scene 10. The Register ward, Whitechapel, Wednesday afternoon.

A converted warehouse by the railway: high windows, beds with lockers, a kitchen you can smell from the door — gravy, and something bread-like, and hot fat. Doors stand open or swing on springs.

At the end of the corridor, one plain steel door with a keypad, and a small green light above it. The pantry.

A NURSE (30s, tired, cheerful) walks Rosie in with a folder, talking without slowing. Halfway along she keys the pantry door open, goes in, comes out with a tray, and lets the door click shut behind her without once thinking about it — the only lock in the building, and it is hers to work.

NURSE: Bed's that one. Kitchen's through there — eat at four in the morning if you like, nobody'll stop you. You can walk out that door the day you can feed yourself out there, and not a day sooner. (She hands over a small device, a meter with a lancet, in a box.) Yours to keep. Finger, mornings. It tells you if you're eating. First number'll frighten you. Don't let it.

Rosie turns the T-meter over in her hands, and sets it on the locker shelf, in the middle, where she can see it.

ROSIE: Who sees all this? The list. Who gets it?

NURSE: Police don't get the list, love. Not since 2019. (beat) A court can, if there's ever a case. That's the whole of it.

Rosie stands very still. Seven years of a family's one reason, gone in a sentence, and she doesn't say a word about it either.

In the next bed, a FIREMAN, DEN (50s), sits with both hands bandaged to the fingertips, holding a teaspoon in the join of his thumbs like a man in cuffs.

DEN: Leeds. Went in for a lad off a tower-block stair. Seven seconds, both of us under a slab. (He raises the bandaged hands.) They tried donor mesh on me three goes. Somebody else's skin won't take on me — wrong-way flesh, they said — and I'd nowt of my own to spare. So it's dressings, and dinner, and patience.

He is going home on Friday. His wife has had the pantry lesson; his first ration box is on the chair, packed and labelled. He talks about his allotment the whole way through the meal: the leeks standing, the greenhouse glass he reglazed one-handed, first asparagus next spring, if he's still fed come winter.

Later: the ward is short-staffed, a man fitting in bay four, the nurse running. Rosie watches Den try and fail to peel the tape off his own knuckles with two spoon-hands. She gets up. She gowns, and gloves, from the box on the wall.

NURSE: (passing, not stopping) For him, love. Not for you.

Rosie unpicks the dressings on Den's burned hands, cleans, dresses, and tapes them down, quick and light and exactly right, the way the surgeons do it in the videos she has watched four thousand times.

DEN: Steady hands.

ROSIE: Show-off, me.

### Scene 11. The weighbridge office, Grays, Wednesday night.

Dark, the printer's green light. Kieran comes down the stairs in his socks and stops, and looks up at the chalk board: LIAM 62.4, and the star beside it, nobody having wiped it.

Then he steps onto the little bridge in his pyjamas, bare feet on the steel, tears off the ticket, and reads it by the glow. 38.3.

He sits on the edge of the camp bed and opens the exercise book. In the second column, the one headed *me*, under 38.6, he writes it. Then he looks from the star on the board to the number in the book and back, a boy doing a sum nobody set him. Book under the pillow. Face to the safe.

---

## Act five: Two weeks on

### Scene 12. The Register ward, Whitechapel, Wednesday 10 June, mid-morning.

Sunlight down the high windows. Rosie, in a t-shirt with her sleeves pushed up, stands on the ward's platform scale by the door while the nurse moves the weights.

NURSE: Forty-six two.

Rosie's lips move.

ROSIE: Ten point eight.

NURSE: That's kilos, girl.

ROSIE: I know what I said.

There is colour in her, and weight on her, and she carries the trays for two men who can't use their hands. Den's bed is stripped and made square. On her locker, propped against the water jug, a postcard: a field of leeks. On the back, in a bandaged printing: *FIRST BOX OPENED. LEEKS STAND WELL. DEN.*

A RECRUITER (50s, good suit, lanyard, kind and brisk as a travel agent) sits on the edge of Rosie's bed with a folder open on his knee.

RECRUITER: Fever Company. You'll know the poster. (beat) You can't catch what they've got, and they can't catch you. There are wards on three continents nobody has walked down since the turn. We pay proper. We feed you for life. There's a pension.

ROSIE: Where?

RECRUITER: Where we send you. It's a company, love, not a club.

ROSIE: Do your people get their ten seconds?

RECRUITER: In turn. The Army buys them, same as the rations.

ROSIE: (beat) Leave the card.

He leaves the card on the bed. She looks at it a moment. Then she opens her locker, stands the card inside on the shelf, and shuts the door on it.

### Scene 13. The ward common room, that night.

The ward telly, sound up for once, because half the ward has gathered. Beds wheeled in, the nurse with her tray stopped mid-round.

On the screen: a press conference. Behind the scientists, a chart of jagged lines, regular as fence posts.

TV NEWSREADER: ...the gravity group at Reading, the same team that heard March's silent merger, now says the tremors in the Thames estuary since April were not events at all, but strokes. Read together on the estuary's instruments, they spell a molecule. A sugar — of the other hand.

Rosie's hand goes out to the locker shelf, to the T-meter. She turns it over in her fingers. *A sugar, of the other hand.* She looks at the little machine that reads her blood, and she puts it back on the shelf.

TV SCIENTIST: (mild, astonished, on a riverbank) It writes, and then it stops, and then it writes again. And the first stroke — the first stroke was on the fourth of April.

TV NEWSREADER: Tomorrow's papers have already settled on a name for it.

A mock-up front page fills the screen: **COME ROUND FOR TEA.**

Down the ward a patient says it out loud — "Come round for tea" — and somebody laughs, and somebody else tells him to shush, and the nurse laughs into her hand. Rosie doesn't.

**Intercut: the weighbridge office, Grays.** The radio on the counter, the same story in radio words, and at the end of it: "...a name the morning papers have already settled on." Danny, at the table with tea in his good hand.

DANNY: April the fourth. That's the morning the cups walked off Mum's dresser.

PEG: (counting the float) Then somebody next door's got a lot to say for themselves.

Kieran, at his end of the counter, writes on a fresh page of the exercise book: *4 APRIL — FIRST STROKE.* He looks up at his graph on the wall: the flat line, the step, the circled wobble. He looks at it a long time, the way you look at a face you half know, and then the news moves on to football.

**The ward, lights out.** The beds, the breathing, the pantry's green light down the corridor. Rosie's phone lights up on the pillow, and she reads it under the blanket.

**KIERAN:** *Nan's doing the depot tonight. They won't let me go.*

Rosie sits up. In the dark she sits a long moment, still as a sum being done. Then she is out of bed. The T-meter comes off the locker shelf and goes into her coat pocket. The coat goes on over her pyjamas. She takes the recruiter's card off the shelf and slips it into her coat pocket, beside the T-meter. She looks back once, at the stripped bed, and at the ward door — which has no lock, and never has had.

She walks out through it.

**Last shot:** her bed with the blanket thrown back. On the locker shelf, the field of leeks standing alone. Down the corridor, dark, the small green light over the pantry goes on blinking, the only lock in the building, keeping the food.

**Cut to black.**

**END OF EPISODE 8**

---

## Plants and payoffs

**Paid off here, from episodes 1 to 7**
- **The Laundry.** Dilys's Tuesday van, "Your whites, Terry" / "Bless you, Dilys" (1, 2, 5); the batch codes on the lids (1); "He'll want it, Peg. He always does" / "He'll get it" (2); the clean spoon and tub set out for a delivery (7); the month-up-front envelope still squared on the counter (7). The Laundry is Ray: the van, the thinness, the Register card and "what was left of him" (1), the lard and vodka (1, 2) all planted. What he keeps on the family is what a man could keep from a ledger and a gatehouse: dinners out, money in, and the dates the mouths changed — the seventh of April (after the meter), a fortnight Tuesday (after the grab) — so he knew the week they did, and said nothing, which is his flaw and theirs in one.
- **"We feed our own" (1, 3, 4, 6)** pays off as Tess's "We never fed our own", said in front of a full fridge. Peg's cover action — putting the waiting spoon and tub away in the drawer — is the pride breaking.
- **Ray's puzzlement that Rosie looked well (5):** the doubled line in his ledger. **His 2002 first case (1, 3)** becomes the shut back of the folder and the month he watched; the figure stays in his head, where episode 9's phone call is waiting for it.
- **The spinner (3, 4, 7):** cut here, as agreed — "There's no such person. There never was."
- **Terry's eleven hops (1, 2, 6, 7):** fourteen more, run lovingly by Peg at the pit, the meter answering LO. The family's cure was always the same, and it never was one.
- **"I gave you nine" (6)** returns as Rosie's parting shot at the gate: "Nine point four, Tess."
- **Peg's "No Marlow crosses till I say" (7):** she says it — for hops only — and wears the safe key on a bootlace from this episode on.
- **The grip (2, 4, 6):** "Me and you could hold on, in there", and Danny's ruined hand refusing: "Coin says which of us comes back with one hand. Not you."
- **The estuary events: the quake (4), "another next-door event" (5), the metronome spacing (7)** pay off as strokes that began on 4 April, read by the team that heard March's silent merger (1), and named by the tabloids: COME ROUND FOR TEA.
- **The Fever Company poster and "Not had a cold since '21" (2)** become the recruiter; **Obverse's Army rations (2)** stand behind "the Army buys them, same as the rations."
- **Rosie's surgeon videos (1, 2)** become the dressings on Den's hands — the thing she wants, done for nothing, on a ward.
- **Osei's "The Register won't ask him anything" (2)** is what Rosie throws at Peg, and what the ward makes true.
- **The 2019 letter in the drawer (1, 5)** is answered by a nurse in one line: "Police don't get the list, love. Not since 2019." The family doesn't hear it; Rosie does, and says nothing.

**Planted here, for later**
- **The T-meter, hers to keep (9):** on "a sugar — of the other hand" her hand finds it and turns it over; it leaves with her in her coat pocket. The instrument that will settle what the crab is, and what two meters say about one drop of blood.
- **The ward's one locked door (9, epilogue):** custody by pantry, built into the building before anyone says it; the nurse keys it without thinking. Rosie's unlocked walk-out is caused by an idea, not a door.
- **The depot recon (9):** gatehouse, two cameras, a lorry every ten minutes, cases on pallets — and the day itself off Mick at the hatch (Scene 5A), which is why Peg's chalk says Wednesday fortnight, when the Army's consignment fills the shed. Shaun, Terry driving, Danny's "I said I'd sort it."
- **Ray suspended, the passbook, the shut 2002 papers (9, 10, epilogue):** his number will go on the roll; the money in Terry's name stays untouched; whatever the back of that folder holds, Tess will have to ring him to get it — "What did my dad weigh?"
- **The message: a sugar of the other hand, first stroke 4 April, COME ROUND FOR TEA (9, epilogue):** Rosie pockets the T-meter while the ward sleeps; nobody says the connection, and the family files the date under "next-door event" while the answer to Liam's two hundredths sits in the same sentence.
- **The recruiter's card (epilogue):** slipped into her pocket beside the T-meter when she goes — the road not taken, kept.
- **Rosie's 46.2, and "Ten point eight":** the lightest courier's arithmetic lives on, and her seconds have grown.
- **Kieran's 38.3**, taken with his eyes on LIAM 62.4 and its star, and his page *4 APRIL — FIRST STROKE*, the first thing he has ever written that isn't homework (9: the log proves the step; 10: he writes what the adults remember).
- **Terry's rewrapped lunch:** he knows a fourth person in the house is refusing food, and says nothing, as he never said anything about the pot.
- **Tess alone with her sums (9, 10):** the call she makes without asking Rosie; her lips moving with no number on them, twice.

**Knowledge at the end.** The family knows the Laundry is Ray, that he is turned, that the money sits in Terry's name, that the Register feeds anybody. Rosie alone knows the police can't read the list. Everyone knows the message began on 4 April and what the papers call it. Only the audience knows about Kieran's column, the rewrapped lunch, the shut back of Ray's folder, and where Rosie has gone.

**Numbers used.** Canon only: £300 a day, five years; seven claimants; 2019, 2015, 2002; fourteen hops of two seconds; 38.6 to 38.3; 9.4; 46.2 on the ward scale ("ten point eight"); LO. New, and logged: the ledger's dates — one mouth from 2021, two from Tuesday 7 April, three from Tuesday a fortnight before the visit (12 May); the return — two crates Tuesday, the rest Thursday; Ray walked out Tuesday week (19 May); the vote's "Wednesday fortnight" = Wednesday 10 June, which dates scenes 12 and 13 and the depot raid; twelve spoonfuls each. Talk only: a month up front.

**Gaps.** *Closes:* who fed them; why the van stopped; what the estuary events were writing, when it began, and what the country calls it. *Opens:* will the depot raid work, and what it will cost; where Rosie is going on the last train; what next door is, and what it wants (a wonder).


# Episode 9: The Deep

*The family raids the state's pantry while Rosie breaks the family's oldest rule and finds what it hid.*

---

## Cold open

### Scene 1. The weighbridge office, Grays, Wednesday, ten o'clock at night.

Job night, and the ritual holds the room together the way it always has. The lorries are parked in a row outside, the blinds are down, the yard lights lay their orange bars on the lino. Terry wraps bacon butties in foil in his yellow gloves. Peg, apron over her nightie, chalk in her hand. On the board, in Peg's handwriting, not Tess's: **PURFLEET — DEPOT.** Under it her sums. Kieran has been allowed up, in his pyjamas under his school coat, and sits on the camp bed by the safe with his exercise book on his knees, because no power on earth would have kept him upstairs.

At the table's end, Danny sits with a nail from the toolbox, working a new hole through his belt — rough at the edges, the way his son's was — and pulls the strap in a notch tighter. Nobody says anything.

SHAUN (26, on a stick since the leg) steps onto the little bridge in his socks. The printer chatters. Peg tears off the ticket and reads it under the lamp.

PEG: Fifty-one nine.

DANNY: Nine and a bit, less the case.

SHAUN: Eight for the far stack, Nan. *(He taps the stick on the steel.)* My leg weighed five and a half when it went. Eleven tenths. I'm worth more without it.

The table laughs, because you do. Peg chalks: SHAUN 51.9 + CASE 3.0 = 9.0.

Then Kieran's turn, uninvited. He is already on the plate. The printer chatters.

PEG: Thirty-eight dead.

KIERAN: Twelve point four. I'm the lightest Marlow there's ever been.

The room goes up. Shaun whistles through his fingers. Terry sings a line of the washing-day song. Even Danny bangs the table once.

PEG: Lightest there's ever been.

The cheer is still going, and her chalk goes with it: KIERAN 38.0, and a star beside it. And Danny has taken the ticket out of her other hand. He reads it, and then he looks at the boy on the plate, and something in his face closes like a shop shutter.

DANNY: Forty. At your birthday.

KIERAN: I'm the lightest—

DANNY: You've been making weight.

The room goes quiet in the middle of its cheer.

KIERAN: Everyone makes weight.

DANNY: Liam made weight.

Nobody says anything. Kieran looks round the table for one adult to say it isn't the same thing, and finds none, because it isn't a different thing, and they all know it.

DANNY: *(to Peg, not loud)* Not him. Not after Liam.

Peg's chalk hangs over the board. Then she turns, and with her thumb she wipes the 38.0 and its star away, three strokes, the way she wiped Rosie's column in April.

PEG: Bed.

KIERAN: Nan—

PEG: *Bed.*

He goes, slowly, hugging the exercise book. On the stairs he stops.

KIERAN: Mind the beads, Shaun.

SHAUN: Yeah. Night, shrimp.

Peg lifts the bootlace over her head and kneels at the safe — the printer prints her knees, and nobody tears it off — and the lock gives its clack. She lifts the three rigs out, laying them on top of *The Assayer's Wife*, and hangs them on the steel rack by the back door. Terry takes his own belt. Shaun takes Danny's and knots the strap short for a thinner man, and Danny, who cannot do a knot one-handed, watches him do it.

One rig stays on the rack: the oldest, its shoulder strap cracked along its length.

TERRY: That strap wants seeing to.

PEG: Everything wants seeing to.

They load the van. At the door Peg stops them, one hand each, the way you'd hold two dogs you loved.

PEG: Face home, the both of you.

TERRY and SHAUN: Face home.

The van rolls over the big bridge and the printer chatters it out of the yard. On the landing, at the dark window, Kieran watches its lights go down the lane.

### Scene 2. The last train out of Fenchurch Street, eleven thirty-five.

A warm June night, the carriage half asleep. ROSIE (22) sits awake in her coat with the estuary sliding past, refineries and cranes hanging their light on black water. In her fist, in her coat pocket, the T-meter from the ward.

The train slows. A recorded voice says *Grays*. She is the only one who gets off. The platform, the fog off the river, the one lit way out. She goes through it at a crosser's walk, counting nothing, knowing the way.

**MAIN TITLE: SECONDS**

---

## Act one: Two crossings, one clock

### Scene 3. The marsh behind the Register depot, Purfleet, five past midnight.

Reeds, a rutty track, the palisade fence, and beyond it the long blind back of the depot shed, corrugated steel on a brick slab. Far off at the front, the gatehouse light, and a floodlight on a pole that swings its glare across the yard every six minutes. Peg sat in the reeds on Monday night and counted it, because that is Peg's job now.

The van sits in the reeds with its lights off. Danny stacks crates at the tailgate, one-handed, his bandaged hand held against his chest. Peg kneels in the load bay with her lips already going. Terry, belted, walks the last few yards to the shed wall — and away off in the yard a dog sets up, plain and level, the moment his boots come off the track.

DANNY: Dog.

PEG: Foxes.

TERRY: *(laying his palm flat on the corrugated steel)* He can smell me. Sixpence's done the same five year.

A beat. Nobody does anything with that. Peg's lips have already started.

PEG: Two seconds. Out at two, whether you're holding a case or holding nothing.

TERRY: Two seconds I can do, love. It was the long ones I said no to.

He flexes his fingers once and steps off the world.

**The black.** His glow opens, an arm's length of grey-gold. The sheeting's inside rolls past him, then the slab, and then the floor of the shed: concrete with the beads hanging in it grey and far between, like currants in a bad cake. A pallet edge. Shrink-wrap, seen through like water. A sealed case, stencilled in green.

He's back, gasping, the case in both arms, and sets it in Danny's.

TERRY: Don't ask, lad. Same way as I went in.

Danny takes the case and stacks it. Out in the yard the dog goes on, level as a drip. Terry squares his shoulders to the wall and goes again. Peg counts each one, low and even, and the crates fill, one case at a time, two seconds a go.

### Scene 4. The weighbridge office, twenty to one.

Rosie lets herself in at the back. The office is dark but for the printer's small green light. On the camp bed, face turned to the safe, Kieran sleeps like a boy who lost an argument.

She hangs her coat on the stairs and takes the T-meter out of its pocket, and sets it on the dresser, square by the safe, where it will stay dry. At the rack there is one rig, and she knows whose it is: Auntie Tess's, the strap cracked along its length. She buckles it on, takes the gaffer tape out of the toolbox, and binds the crack tight, the way you'd bind a split spade.

Then she stands a moment in the dark, talking the sleeping room through it in a whisper, the way you'd talk a job to yourself.

ROSIE: If it's rock, I'm dead where I stand. If it's nothing, I fall for ever. If it's water — *(she touches the brass disc at her throat)* — then it's the minute. Stand a minute over there, any world, and the tab brings me home. Half my seconds. Five point four.

She steps onto the little bridge, in her socks. The printer chatters, and she lets it.

ROSIE: *(to the plate, almost a joke)* Print me proper.

She flexes her fingers once, the way every Marlow does. And then she does the thing no Marlow has ever done. She does not look for home.

**The black.** Sound drops to her heartbeat and her breath. The glow, the arm's length of everything. Her ears scream that she is falling, and there is nowhere to fall to, and she knows the voice.

And the pulls come, the way Terry taught a boy at a stove: home, strongest, like someone calling you in for your tea. Off east, hard, like leaning on a lorry — the Hill. Below her, soft and wide, a feather bed you'd sink in — the Deep. And behind it all, faint, like a kettle two doors down, one more: the Far.

All her life she has turned to the caller. She turns her back on it, and lets herself sink into the feather bed.

One. Two.

She steps out.

### Scene 5. The far stack at Purfleet, and the sea one world over, intercut. One clock.

**The black at Purfleet.** Shaun goes through the wall's beads and into the floor, and here the beads are packed grey through grey, frogspawn in jelly, the thickest pour he has ever pulled through. The wrist unit counts tenths. Pallets slide past in the glow. At the far end, stacked three high, cases stencilled ARMY. Nobody in his family knows that but him.

**The marsh.** Peg, eyes shut:

PEG: …two… three…

Through the shed wall above him comes the roar like a tipper shedding its load.

**The water.** Rosie steps out and there is nothing to stand on.

The screen goes black.

Cold closes on her like a hand. Salt. No floor, no up, no down, and her ears telling her she is falling in every direction at once. In her chest: the office air, bacon fat and ink, going stale. Her thumb goes to the wrist unit — and stops, an inch off the button.

So that is why the Deep keeps you.

She takes her thumb away, lets a little breath go, the way the pit taught her, and the bubbles tickle past her face — up — and she follows them, kicking in her clothes, in the dark, eight metres by her own count.

**The black at Purfleet.** The wrist unit reads 6.9. Close on Shaun's fingertips: the fizz starting under the nails, like rain on a shed roof. He takes a case off the Army stack, and the first beads fall through the top of his glow, silent, black hail. He turns for home, small pulls at nothing, patient through the thickness of it.

**The marsh.**

PEG: …seven… eight.

He lands with a gasp, the case in his arms, rain of the reeds on his face.

DANNY: Straight?

SHAUN: Straight.

PEG: Once more, son. Then we're away.

DANNY: Never go back twice.

PEG: It's a pantry, not a bank.

He squares up and goes again.

**The sea.** Rosie's head breaks surface into air she cannot see. A wave slaps her face. She takes one breath of it — one — and holds it, and treads water, and counts.

Something long and soft slides across her cheek. Weed. She doesn't cry out, because her mouth is shut, and her breath is the only sample of that world anyone has ever held.

Forty. Forty-one. Fifty.

Something small walks into her hood and settles there, against her skull, cold and quick. She lets it stay.

Fifty-nine. Sixty.

Her teeth find the tab, and she bites.

**The bite.** No light. No glow, no gauge, nothing to see at all — only her own body, the cold going out of her clothes, and the pull of home through it all, steady as the tide. She lets herself sink, and counts the count she counted up, the way you drop a floor into a vault.

Eight.

The pull of home stands square on, like a door into it. She steps.

**The marsh.** Shaun lands with the last case, gasping. Danny takes the weight of it, and looks back at the blind shed, and the beads he can hear still ticking down inside it.

DANNY: That wall'll have read the lot of us. Every body. Every go.

PEG: Then they can read it on a full stomach.

**The weighbridge office.** Rosie lands on the little bridge with a gasp, drenched to the skin, weed in her hair, and the smell of the room comes back to her: bacon fat, printer ink, dog.

The printer chatters.

On the camp bed, Kieran sits up like a cork.

For a moment nobody moves: a boy in his pyjamas, a girl dripping on a steel plate in the dark.

She reaches into her hood and pulls out a crab, a green-shelled crab the size of her fist, legs going, and holds it up in the green light, water running off her wrist onto the plate.

ROSIE: It's sea. Under us. All of it.

---

## Act two: The cases

### Scene 6. The weighbridge office, ten past one.

Rosie, wrapped in the good blankets, sits at the stove end with her teeth chattering, a mug of tea she can't drink going cold in her hands. The crab is in the washing-up bowl on the drainer, walking the rim. Sixpence has come out of his basket and stands in the middle of the floor, growling his low steady note, at her, and at the bowl, and at the puddle spreading off the plate.

ROSIE: You as well.

Kieran tears the night's tickets off the printer and squares them: his own at nine, hers from the plate, hers from the plate again — and between hers, one at a quarter to one. LITTLE BRIDGE. **−46.2 KG.**

KIERAN: Minus forty-six two. Who weighed forty-six two?

ROSIE: Me. To the gram. Ward scale had me at the same on Wednesday. *(beat)* Ten point eight seconds, that. Not that it's any use in water.

KIERAN: You crossed on the plate.

ROSIE: Wanted it written. *(beat)* If I'd drowned over there, somebody could read where I went.

He looks at her a moment. Then he does the only thing this family has ever done with a stranger: he lifts the crab out of the bowl, sets it in the middle of the little bridge, and waits. The printer chatters. He tears off the ticket — 0.38 — and writes the number in his exercise book, and under it, in pencil: *CRAB. FROM NEXT DOOR.*

KIERAN: Nobody's ever brought anything back from there.

ROSIE: I know.

KIERAN: What's it like?

ROSIE: Cold. *(beat)* Dark. Hungry work.

KIERAN: *(looking at the minus ticket in his hand)* If you'd turned on the way back, you'd have come home straight. You'd be at Nan's fry-up.

ROSIE: Five in six I stay as I am. I know. *(beat)* I didn't go to be turned back, Kiz. I went shopping.

He looks at her. The word lands somewhere behind his eyes and stays there.

KIERAN: The message. *Come round for tea.* That's what the papers—

ROSIE: Yeah.

She shivers, and holds the mug anyway for the warmth of it. He goes to the airing cupboard and brings the second blanket, and puts it round her shoulders without a word, the way she used to put things round Liam. Before he shuts his book he lays the new ticket against the old one, the −59.0 from April, the two of them square, and looks at them a while, the way he looked at the Twitch ticket and the minus fifty-nine once before. Then he tapes his own in beside it and closes the book on them both.

### Scene 7. The weighbridge office, dawn.

The van rolls onto the big bridge and the printer prints it coming home. The family comes in with the cold on them: Peg, Danny, Terry, Shaun on his stick — and stops, all together, at the sight of the salt.

The puddle by the plate has dried to a white crust. Peg crouches and rubs it between finger and thumb, and looks at the powder on her skin.

PEG: There's salt drying on my bridge. You went in the Deep.

ROSIE: *(from the stove end, in the big jumper, dry now)* I went in a sea, Nan.

PEG: Same thing.

ROSIE: It wasn't. *(quiet, so only the room hears)* Eight metres up. I counted it. Their sea's sitting over this lino.

Danny crouches at the plate and touches the crust, and looks up under the board at the chalk, at the painted rule that has ruled them all their lives.

Then the morning's business, because lorries don't know. Terry carries the cases in and stacks them on the table. Eleven cases, sealed, stencilled, batch-coded. Peg wipes her hands.

PEG: Everybody that crossed, over here. One at a time.

Terry first, out of habit. She pricks his finger with her meter, and waits for the beep. **LO.**

TERRY: I could've told you that for nothing.

Rosie is already holding her hand out. Peg pricks it, and waits for the beep. **LO.**

ROSIE: Still me.

Shaun sets his stick against the table and holds out his hand.

PEG: Straight?

SHAUN: Straight.

She pricks him. The beep. **LO.**

The kitchen goes very still. Shaun looks at the little screen a long moment, then out of the window at the yard.

SHAUN: I never felt a thing. You never do. That's the trouble with the black.

PEG: You said straight.

SHAUN: I knew before we were out the marsh. *(beat)* I said it anyhow.

Nobody says anything. Danny closes his eyes.

PEG: *(finally, and it is awful)* Well. You're fed now, anyhow.

DANNY: We went out to feed three.

He says it to the table. Terry, counting the cases with his eyes, the way everyone in this family counts everything now, finishes it.

TERRY: Two week, at three. *(beat)* Four now.

PEG: Then we go again. Fortnight Thursday.

Nobody argues. Shaun, white around the mouth, nods once, like a man taking a wage cut.

Then Sixpence works along the stack, case by case, growling his low note at each one — at the green-stencilled Army cases, at the Register's plain white ones — and stops at the last case of all, the second of Shaun's two, and puts his nose down and his tail loose, and lies flat, incurious, the way he lies at Peg's feet.

Kieran fetches the knife and cuts the case open. Inside: sealed bricks of porridge in sachets, printed, labelled, ordinary as a supermarket. He pinches one open, mixes a little with water in an eggcup, and dabs Peg's meter strip with it. The beep. A number. An ordinary number.

KIERAN: It's ours now. This lot. This case is food for us.

He looks at the meter, and the dog, and his uncle, and something enormous arrives in a twelve-year-old's face. He takes his pencil, leans over the printer, and writes on the audit roll itself, in the margin, in a schoolboy's hand: **FOOD TURNS TOO.**

Peg looks at the case, and at the dog, and at her chalk sums on the board, 51.9 + 3.0 = 9.0, the numbers she trusted.

PEG: Bad luck.

That is all she has. She says it, and goes to put the kettle on.

---

## Act three: The other hand

### Scene 8. The weighbridge office, twenty past eight in the morning.

The hatch is up; the day runs on; Mick has been and gone with his ticket and his bacon roll. In the scullery bucket by the door, Rosie's sea-soaked jumper and a fistful of weed sit in their own water. Sixpence lies in front of the bucket, growling at it, steady as a fridge.

ROSIE: Sit down, Uncle Terry.

TERRY: It's my kitchen, love.

ROSIE: Sit down.

He sits. She takes the T-meter off the dresser, where it has waited by the safe since one in the morning, and pricks his finger, and squeezes the bead onto the strip, and waits.

The screen: **2.8.**

ROSIE: Two point eight. That's a starving man. *(She puts the meter down in the middle of the table where everyone can see it.)* Nothing else for an hour. Not a tub. Not a spoon of dripping. Not a sweet.

TERRY: I couldn't eat another thing anyhow.

ROSIE: You're the worst liar in this family, and that's a cup with entries.

Terry gets up — nobody stops him — and does the thing he is: he fills the big pot, he salts the water with a teaspoon levelled off with a knife, and he cooks the crab, in his gloves, at his own stove. Then he lays a place. Plate, fork, napkin folded. Bread and butter for the look of it, that he cannot eat. The family stands round the kitchen on a Thursday morning, between lorries, and watches a turned man eat a crab from another world.

He eats slowly. He is starving, and he eats slowly, because he is a cook.

Nobody says what it tasted of.

Kieran sits at the counter with his exercise book and writes: *9:22. Crab.* Under it he rules a space.

### Scene 9. The weighbridge office, twenty past ten.

Rosie pricks Terry's finger again. The bead comes up. She holds her hand out to her grandmother.

ROSIE: Meter, Nan.

Peg brings hers out of the cutlery drawer, from among the spoons. Two strips, one bead of blood, one each.

The T-meter beeps: **5.6.**

Peg's meter beeps: **LO.**

Rosie lays them side by side on the table, both face up, the way Ray laid two photographs on the counter in March. Five point six. LO. Kieran writes both numbers in the ruled space.

ROSIE: Two meters. Same blood. Both of them right.

Terry looks at his own finger for a long moment.

TERRY: Five point six. *(beat)* That's a dinner, that.

He pushes his empty plate out into the middle of the table, towards the middle of the family, the way a man asks for more.

There is no more crab. The pot is empty. The bowl it lived in is empty. The sea is eight metres up.

DANNY: What's it taste—

He stops. He looks round the table, at his daughter, at his mother, at his uncle's face, and nobody in the room wants the answer, and the question dies where it stands.

ROSIE: Two point eight to five point six, off one crab. *(quiet)* There's a sea of it, Kiz. Under us.

Terry takes his plate back, and wipes it, and puts it away, and stands at the sink with his gloves dripping, looking out at the pit.

### Scene 10. The yard and the office, that night.

The office is lamplit. The stove ticks. Terry and Peg, alone, the way they have been alone in kitchens for sixty years. He is drying the last pot; she is at the dresser with the day's takings.

TERRY: Five year of them tubs, Peg. They kept me breathing. *(beat)* That crab's the first thing that's ever moved my number.

PEG: You had one crab.

TERRY: There's a sea of them.

PEG: Terry.

TERRY: I'm going, Peg.

The clock ticks. Peg does not turn round. When she speaks, her voice doesn't rise at all.

PEG: Get rid of that jumper before they come.

TERRY: It's twenty year, stepping out.

PEG: *(to the dresser, to the drawer, to thirty years of envelopes)* So's starving.

Out in the yard, Danny works the iron bar under the drain grate and lifts it, one-handed, his bandaged hand braced on the kerb. Kieran kneels with the bucket between his knees and wrings the sea out of Rosie's jumper, twist on twist, and the water runs dark down the hole, and goes on running when the jumper is dry, because the weed holds it.

The grate goes back. The yard is the yard again.

At the counter, later, alone, Kieran opens his exercise book to the page with the 04:17 ticket taped in, and beside it tapes one green frond of the weed, flattened flat as a flower.

---

## Act four: The step

### Scene 11. The weighbridge office, Friday, nine in the morning.

Tess's car comes over the big bridge and the printer prints her in. Kieran unbolts the door before anyone has decided anything. Tess stands on the lino with two days of sleeping in the car on her, and looks at the salt crust, which nobody has scrubbed, and the cases, and her family.

TESS: The ward rang me Wednesday night, the minute you walked. I'm down as next of kin. *(beat)* I gave you your raid. Then I came. *(to Rosie)* You walked out.

ROSIE: Door had no lock.

Peg opens her mouth. Tess doesn't wait for it.

TESS: I'm not here to plan.

KIERAN: *(before anyone else can speak)* Auntie. Before anything.

He takes her by the sleeve — the first time in his life he has led an adult anywhere — to the graph on the wall. The flat line. The step. The circled wobble. And beside it, in his book, open on the counter: the second column, the one headed *me*. 39.2. 38.9. 38.6. 38.3. 38.0.

Tess reads down it twice. Her lips move. For once there is a number and she doesn't say it.

TESS: Since Easter?

KIERAN: Since Easter.

TESS: *(to the room, quietly)* He's been making weight under your roof.

PEG: He eats his tea.

KIERAN: I eat some of it.

TERRY: *(at the stove, not turning round)* I found two rounds of butties in the bin last week. Dried them off and put them back in his bag. *(beat)* Thought he was fussy.

Nobody has anything. Danny looks at the boy the way he looked at the ticket on Wednesday night. Rosie looks away, at the fridge, at the two birthday cards, and keeps looking at them.

Tess turns back to the graph, and is Tess again.

TESS: The step. April the fourth. *(to Kieran, testing him, the way she tests sums)* How do you know it's not the weight?

KIERAN: Nan says it's chipped. Dropped it that morning. *(beat)* Chip's the size of a fingernail.

TESS: Right.

She goes to the shelf and takes down Peg's five-kilo bag of flour, and sets the brass balance on the counter, and lays the kilo and the two twos in one pan, and spoons nothing, because the bag is a bag of five kilos by law. She sets the bag in the other pan.

The pointer settles dead centre. Five kilos. Exactly.

She lifts the bag onto the little bridge. The printer chatters. The ticket: **4.95.**

The kitchen is silent. The fridge hums.

TESS: It was never the chip.

TESS: Their wall had me at sixty-one seven, at the clinic. My bridge said sixty-one nought. *(beat)* I told myself their wall was wrong.

She stands looking at the plate, and everyone in the room does the same sum at the same speed.

DANNY: Every job since April. *(He looks at his bandaged hand.)* The bridge has had me at two point four since April.

TESS: Two point two.

Tess takes the last things out of her inside pocket and lays them side by side on the counter. The ticket Kieran lent her on the ferry: 02:04 · LITTLE BRIDGE · −59.0. And, next to it, Wednesday's: −46.2.

TESS: Maggie's steelyard had her boy at fifty-nine, and a steelyard don't lie. My mother's bridge can't read a bag of flour. *(beat)* Those two are the only true numbers it's printed since April, and they're both of people nobody could see.

Then she turns to the dresser, and hauls the drawer. It sticks, as it always sticks, and comes with a tearing noise, envelopes sagging out of it, years of them, brown and buff.

PEG: That's my drawer.

TESS: Yeah.

Tess takes the topmost brown window envelope — WEIGHTS AND MEASURES, April — and opens it with one slit of her thumbnail, and reads it aloud, flat, like a ticket.

TESS: "Following a local gravity change of minus 1.1 per cent beneath the estuary, all weighing equipment in the affected area must be re-verified within 28 days." *(beat)* Dated the ninth of April. It's the twelfth of June.

She digs. Low down, the corner of pale green with the printed hand on it. She reads that too.

TESS: "Since the fourth of June 2019, patient lists held by the Handedness Register are no longer available to the police, save on the order of a court." *(beat)* Twenty nineteen.

DANNY: Seven year.

ROSIE: *(from the stair, not loud)* Nurse told me that my first day in.

Peg sits down slowly on the bottom stair, next to her granddaughter, under the framed cuttings that say CLEAN TRANSLATION, NO ROTATION, and says nothing at all, and nobody in the room can look at her, and nobody can look away either.

---

## Act five: The hundredths

### Scene 12. The weighbridge office, that afternoon.

The hatch down. The family at the long table: Peg, Danny, Tess, Terry, Rosie, Shaun on his stick, Kieran at the end with his book. The box of audit rolls from the loft sits on the dresser where it has sat since the night of the ferry.

TESS: Danny. His number. Bring me his number.

Danny takes his wallet out. From behind the driving licence he draws the folded thermal printout, soft at its folds, carried two months, never once unfolded. He opens it flat on the table and holds the corners down with both hands, one bandaged, as if it might blow away.

The wall's last line: **65.1 KG.**

Tess has the loft roll unrolled across the table, pinned at its corners with Peg's brass weights. Her finger finds the ticket. 4:30 a.m., the twenty-ninth of April.

TESS: Sixty-two four. Plus one and a half of notes. Sixty-three nine.

DANNY: *(to the printout, to the room)* Why's it a kilo out?

Tess turns the April letter over and takes the pencil Kieran holds out to her. She writes on the back of it, talking the numbers as she goes, because someone's counting.

TESS: Sixty-three nine, chalk. The letter says one point one per cent light. *(She writes.)* Sixty-four six, true.

DANNY: Sixty-five one.

TESS: Half a kilo short still.

The kitchen waits. Nobody has it. And then Peg, at the head of her own table, in her own kitchen, says it the way she says everything, plainly, to the table.

PEG: I made him breakfast.

Nobody breathes.

PEG: After he was weighed. Lucky breakfast. Three rashers. Fried bread. He ate every bite, and I scraped his plate into the bin because there was nothing on it to scrape.

Tess writes the last line of the sum: 64.6 + 0.5 = 65.1. Then the seconds, underneath, small.

TESS: Six point nine eight. Job was seven. *(beat)* The lie on its own, he makes it. The breakfast on its own, he makes it. *(She puts the pencil down.)* Either one, he makes it. Both, he doesn't. By two hundredths.

The clock over the counter ticks. The printer's green light blinks. Rosie is looking at the chalk board, at LIAM 62.4 and the star beside it that nobody has wiped.

ROSIE: *(very quietly)* He was so pleased about the star.

Peg gets up. She takes the duster, and reaches up, and rubs out the 62.4 under the star, and then, slowly, in her sign-writer's hand, writes in its place: **65.1.**

PEG: There's your number, love.

She stands back and looks at it, the first true number the board has ever held, and puts the duster down, and squares her shoulders on thirty years of running this family off that bridge.

PEG: Your dad never had a second to spare in his life. Fifteen stone. It's why we make weight. It's the one thing in this family that's never been wrong.

Tess looks at her mother. Then up at Frank's photo, the big man in the donkey jacket, laughing at whoever held the camera. She looks at him a long time.

Then she takes her coat off the chair and goes out.

### Scene 13. The yard, dusk.

Tess sits in her car with the phone at her ear and the river running grey behind the fence. The office window glows yellow behind her; through it, the family sits on at the long table under the chalk star.

**Intercut: the flat above the bookmaker's, Tilbury.** Ray, in his shirtsleeves, awake at his table among the squared files, the window box behind him. The phone is at his ear. He has been awake since two.

TESS: Ambrose. It's Tess Marlow.

A silence on the line, the length of a held breath.

RAY: Yes.

TESS: My dad. What did he weigh?

The line hums. Out on the river a tug sounds its horn, twice, far off.

RAY: Sixty-four kilos.

Tess closes her eyes. In the office window, small behind glass, the big man goes on laughing at whoever held the camera.

RAY: You'll be wanting the rest of it.

TESS: Yes.

RAY: Tomorrow, then.

He rings off. Tess sits with the dead phone in her hand, and doesn't go in.

**Cut to black.**

**END OF EPISODE 9**

---

## Plants and payoffs

**Paid off here, from earlier episodes**
- **"Face home" (1, said at the door tonight):** Rosie turns her back on the caller, and the rule made before bites breaks on screen. "The Deep keeps you" (1) breaks with her: it kept her sixty seconds, then the tab brought her home — the Marlows' own pit training, the one family that has it, is what kept her alive. Her thumb stopping an inch off the wrist unit is the moment she learns *why* the Deep keeps you; the pit exchange from episode 1 — "Then you'd be carrying the pit with you. Water counts. Everything counts." — is the plant her stopped thumb pays, with Terry's "The Deep keeps you" beside it.
- **The bite (taught in 5: "Only once you've stood a minute on the wrong side", and the tab's skin-tight field that fires in water when the belt won't):** used at last, in their sea, half her seconds, 5.4, blind, no light and no gauge. The minute and the tariff were planted; nothing else is said.
- **Liam's nail-hole in his belt (4, 5):** Danny punches a new hole in his own belt at the table on raid night, rough at the edges, the way his son's was, and pulls the strap in a notch tighter; nobody says anything. The fortnight of hunger, and what it teaches, in one wordless beat.
- **The plate prints a body in the black as a minus (2, 6):** Rosie crosses standing on the plate on purpose — "Wanted it written" — and Kieran finds the ticket: −46.2 at a quarter to one, to the gram against the ward's 46.2. The girl who hid everything now leaves her number where it can't lie, and the roll holds her crossing.
- **The T-meter from the ward (8):** it settles what next door is, with Terry's blood — 2.8 to 5.6 on one crab — while Peg's meter reads LO on the same drop. The proof is an instrument and a man's blood, never a taste: Danny's question dies half-asked, and nobody says what it tasted of.
- **The message, "COME ROUND FOR TEA", first stroke 4 April (8):** "I went." Rosie's crossing is the family's answer to the invitation, made alone, at night, by its best and hungriest crosser.
- **The drift table (6, 7):** Shaun's two eight-second legs come up one each way, and the family rolls him with the odds already known. Peg's answer is the doom itself: "Then we go again. Fortnight Thursday."
- **The grip and the hand (6, 8):** Rosie's ward offer to Danny is dead ("Coin says which of us comes back with one hand"), and the crab shows her the other way to feed them.
- **Liam's two hundredths (5):** 62.4 + 1.5 = 63.9 chalk; the April letter's −1.1 per cent gives 64.6 true; Peg confesses the lucky breakfast; 65.1; 6.98 against a 7.0 job. Either error alone, he lives. The audience's own theory from episode 5 was only half the answer, and the missing half is the neighbours'.
- **Kieran's log (2, 4, 6, 8):** the step on 4 April, the wobble, the chip, the *me* column — "Since Easter." The boy's homework is the proof, and the flour on the brass balance ("Never told me a lie", 1) kills the chip story: 5.000 against 4.95.
- **The clinic's sixty-one seven (6):** said aloud at the plate at last — her bridge had her at sixty-one nought — the reading Tess told herself was wrong, filed in May and never used until the flour made her say it.
- **Terry's rewrapped lunch (8):** he found the boy's unopened sandwiches and put them back, and thought he was fussy. Even the one who feeds him in secret didn't ask.
- **The drawer (1, 4, 5):** hauled open at last. The April letter read aloud; the 2019 Register letter read aloud, and the family learns what Rosie learned from a nurse in episode 8: the police haven't been able to read the list for seven years. "We don't go to them" ran out in 2019 and nobody looked.
- **Danny's two point four (6):** two point two is the truth.
- **Episode 8's 'Nobody's sending me in on my seconds, mind' (Terry at the vote):** owned at the wall — 'Two seconds I can do, love. It was the long ones I said no to.'
- **The −59.0 ticket (4, 5, 7) and Maggie's "fifty-nine" off the steelyard (2, 7):** laid beside Rosie's −46.2, with the steelyard named — two instruments agreeing while the little bridge can't read a bag of flour.
- **The dog law (1–8):** the depot dog sets up at Terry's approach, before anyone crosses — dogs smell the turned — and Peg answers "Foxes." Sixpence then picks Shaun's turned case out of the stack without being told.
- **Rosie's "cold-water swimming" (3):** true at last, dripping.
- **Ray's 2002 file (8, shut):** the hook call. "Sixty-four kilos" against a legend of fifteen stone, and "Tomorrow, then" books his kitchen-door visit.

**Planted here, for later**
- **The sea's geometry:** eight metres up, counted twice, up and down. The door of episode 10 is a depth and a tide, and the baking tray waits.
- **Terry's decision:** made the night of the crab, against Peg's "Get rid of that jumper before they come." His five-in-six odds, said by Kieran tonight, are his die in episode 10.
- **The jumper and the weed down the drain (a wonder):** the neighbours' sea in a Grays drain, and our life in their sea on Rosie's jumper — the epilogue's charge and its residue. One frond stays taped in the book, beside the 04:17 ticket.
- **Kieran's crab weighed, the first thing ever brought back from there:** the only honest channel, used by a boy. His log now holds the step, the wobble, the *me* column, two minus tickets squared together, the crab, and FOOD TURNS TOO written on the roll itself — the first writing on the roll before the night of writing.
- **The roll keeps its own copy of Rosie's crossing**, and of every lie on the board since April: the raw material of episode 10's vote — and of whatever the depot's wall has logged, which Danny has now said aloud and Peg has answered with "a full stomach."
- **"I went shopping":** the pantry next door, priced in one crab and two meter readings.
- **Peg's re-chalk, 65.1:** the board's first true number, and her hand that writes it.
- **Fortnight Thursday:** another depot run promised, unfilmed, the ratchet still turning until the rolls stop it.

**Knowledge at the end.** The family knows: next door is a sea and its food fits the turned; Rosie crossed on purpose, on the plate, and it is on the roll; Shaun is turned; the little bridge has lied since 4 April; Liam died by two hundredths, of the scale and the breakfast together; the police cannot read the Register; Kieran is starving himself. Tess alone knows what Frank weighed, and that Ray has "the rest of it". Nobody but Rosie knows what the sea was like beyond cold and dark, and nobody asks twice.

**Numbers used.** Canon only: 38.0 (12.4) and 40.0 at his birthday; 51.9 + 3.0 = 9.0 chalk, legs of 8.0; Terry's 2-second legs; the bite at 5.4; eight metres; 2.8 and 5.6 and LO; 5.000 against 4.95; sixty-one nought against sixty-one seven; −59.0; −1.1 per cent, 28 days; 62.4, 1.5, 63.9, 64.6, 65.1, 6.98 and 7.0, two hundredths; 2.4 and 2.2; sixty-four kilos; "eleven tenths"; "twenty year"; 46.2 come home from the ward. New, and logged: the minus ticket, −46.2 at 00:45 (her two tickets on the plate read 45.7; nothing rests on them); the crab, 0.38 on the little bridge; eaten 9:22, metered 10:22; Shaun's wrist unit at 6.9. Derived, nothing resting on them: eleven cases; four turned mouths.

**Gaps.** *Closes:* what the Deep is; whether next door is food; why Liam ran out; what made the scales lie. *Kept open, as wonders:* what went down the drain; what the neighbours are; what Terry will find; what Frank's sixty-four kilos means, which opens episode 10.


# Episode 10: Paper

*The family reads its own record for the first time, and must decide who goes where, and whether to burn the record or give it away.*

---

## Cold open

### Scene 1. The weighbridge office, dawn, spring 2002 — as Peg remembers it.

Golden light through blinds that haven't been cleaned in a decade. The smell of a fry-up. The printer, newer then, chatters like a till.

FRANK MARLOW takes up the whole of the little bridge. A big man, fifteen stone if he's an ounce, donkey jacket still on, laughing at something somebody said before we arrive. PEG, forty-eight, stands at the printer with the chalk. On the stair, in her socks, TESS, fourteen. In the doorway, DANNY, seventeen, already too wide for the frame.

FRANK: Mind, our Peg. Weigh me after me dinner and you can bill me as a lorry.

PEG: You'd eat the profit.

FRANK: I'd eat the plate an' all.

The ticket prints. Peg tears it off, holds it up to the lamp the way she does, and chalks it on the board without reading it out. Numbers like that don't get read out.

FRANK: *(swinging Tess down off the stair by one arm)* And you keep off that plate, our Tess. You're not for the black.

She shrieks with laughter, fifteen stone of father swinging her — and he laughs, laughs at whoever is holding the camera —

— and the laugh hangs on the office wall, framed, twenty-four years later. The same big man. The same laugh.

**MAIN TITLE: SECONDS**

---

## Act one: Frank

### Scene 2. The loft over the office, Saturday, eight in the morning.

Dust and paper. The boxes in their rows, 01 to 26, in Peg's handwriting. The one marked 02 stands where Tess left it in May, lid squared back on.

Peg climbs the ladder first, refusing the hand offered below. Tess comes behind, having slept on the settle. Terry last, slow, one rung at a time, and sits on the old trunk to get his breath. Kieran brings the torch. Rosie stays in the hatch, sitting on the top rung, in Danny's old jumper.

Tess unrolls the last of the 2001 roll and the 2002 roll after it, end to end, across the boards, and pins them down with Peg's brass weights, one at each corner.

The job-day lines run down them in purple ink gone lilac: a date, a time, a weight. No names. A roll never knew a name.

Under the rolls, flat in the bottom of the box, lies an envelope gone the colour of tea. Nobody asks why these were never burned. Inside: Frank's tickets, torn off one by one, every one he ever printed, Peg's pencil still on them. Tess lays them along the boards in order, each against its date on the roll.

The first: September, 78.0. On it, in pencil, in Peg's own hand from twenty-four years ago: *12 st 4*.

Then week on week. 76.9. 74.4. 71.8. 69.0. 66.9. Kieran reads them out steady, like a lesson, because somebody has to. Nobody tells him to stop.

The new year. 66.0. 65.9. 65.0. 64.8. Then spring, and the last of them: 64.4 — and on that one, in pencil, *10 st 2*, and under it, alone, *7.1*.

The torch hums. Terry looks down the line of them the way only a man who has lived one can.

TERRY: That's not a man going careful. That's a man that can't eat.

TESS: September. What was the job?

TERRY: Farringdon. Four seconds. *(beat)* Only one he did all year.

Tess's lips move. One in twelve. She looks down the whole year of it, week on week, and nobody in the loft says the word yet.

TESS: *(to nobody, flat as a ticket)* Forty-four. Forty-three six. Forty-three one. Forty-one eight.

It is Rosie's year, said out loud in a loft, and everyone in the loft knows it. Rosie says nothing.

### Scene 3. The weighbridge office, 2002 — as it was.

The same kitchen, the same light, wrong about one thing only: the man at the table is thin. Sleeves too big, the donkey jacket hung on him like a curtain. His hands are steady around a jam jar, turning the lid the wrong way, and the lid won't give, and he turns it the wrong way again, carefully, as if patience were the trick.

Peg takes it off him, laughing, turns it once the other way — pop — and laughs as she spreads the jam, and talks over her shoulder about the lorries.

Frank watches her laugh.

He says nothing at all.

Cut to Rosie in the loft hatch, sitting on the top rung, wearing exactly that look.

### Scene 4. The weighbridge office, Saturday, mid-morning.

The family is round the table — Peg, Tess, Terry, Danny with his bandaged hand flat in front of him, Rosie, Shaun on his stick, Kieran at the counter end — and at the open back door, in a coat too big for him, stands DS RAY AMBROSE, suspended, unshaved, holding a buff file.

He comes in when Peg steps aside to let him, which is its own event. He lays a yard of thermal paper on the table: line under line of print, a date, a time, a weight, and the weight a little smaller every line, down the whole strip, for a month.

RAY: Marrable Street. Spring of two thousand and two. The wall had him at sixty-four point three. *(beat)* Seven point one seconds in the black. At that weight, seven point one is every tenth he had.

Nobody in the kitchen moves.

PEG: Seven point one. *(beat)* That's my hand. I pencil'd it on his ticket. A tenth was always plenty.

RAY: At seven he was still at the tray. Starving men are slow.

PEG: Did he go quick?

RAY: Between one second and the next, Mrs Marlow. That's the one mercy in it.

He squares the strip on the table, an evidence man to the end, and looks at it a while instead of at them.

RAY: He'd have known, mind. The minute he looked at a ticket. They always know. *(He doesn't look at Rosie either.)* And I wrote it all down in a book and showed nobody. Twenty-four year. That's my bit of it.

### Scene 5. The dresser drawer, Saturday, eleven o'clock.

Peg hauls it open. It comes with its tearing noise, envelopes sagging. She digs down past the crowns and the window envelopes to the corner gone the colour of tea, typed MR F. MARLOW.

She opens it herself. One slit of the thumbnail.

She reads it aloud, standing, the way she reads tickets.

PEG: "Dear Mr Marlow. The Ministry invites anyone who has crossed to attend free handedness screening at Grays Town Hall. Places on the ration scheme are available to turned crossers." *(beat)* "Applicants will be interviewed by police."

She reads the last line again. Then she folds the letter along its old crease and puts it on the table where everyone can see it, and says the only arithmetic that's left.

PEG: He never saw this. I never let him.

At the table, under the brass weight, lies Frank's last ticket. 64.4. *10 st 2.* *7.1.* Peg picks up the hatch pencil — the one she marked his tickets with — and beside her own old marks she writes, in the sign-writer's hand:

**FRANK. 10 ST 2. TURNED.**

PEG: There's your number, Frank.

Then she takes the duster and wipes the chalk board bare. 65.1 and its star. The PURFLEET sums. Every name and every number. The board hangs empty, and the chalk dust comes off on her apron.

### Scene 6. The weighbridge office, noon.

Two REGISTER INVESTIGATORS at the counter — a woman and a man, green lanyards, windcheaters, a clipboard between them. Not police. Something politer and worse.

INVESTIGATOR: Wednesday night, between midnight and one, the Purfleet depot's walls logged two bodies in the black. One of them nine times, three kilos each go. The other twice, the same. Every reading to the hundred grams, cargo and all.

She looks at the bare board, and at her clipboard, and back.

INVESTIGATOR: Your granddaughter walked off our Whitechapel ward at eleven that night, Mrs Marlow, and her next of kin is this address. *(beat)* We'd like the family's weights, Mrs Marlow. To match.

PEG: Board's being re-chalked.

INVESTIGATOR: *(mild, immovable)* The audit rolls, then. We'll be back Monday. With a warrant.

They go. The lorries come and go over the big bridge, and the printer prints their weights, and the family sits inside its own record with a day and a half of it left.

---

## Act two: The night of writing

### Scene 7. The weighbridge office, Saturday night.

The boxes are down. The rolls run the length of the long table and over the ends, pinned with brass weights, tea mugs, the sugar bowl. One lamp. The stove going. Kieran sits where the till usually is, with his own sharp school pencil, and his exercise book open to a fresh ruled page.

The rhythm finds itself, and it is Terry's, and Tess's, and it goes like this:

TERRY: Fenchurch Street. March, '09.

TESS: Six and a half.

KIERAN writes: name, seconds.

TERRY: Turned. Maureen.

And on. Romford. The grain job. The Old Street mess. Vic, who died of "his heart" in the March of '09. Lee, off his food till Canary Wharf and right as rain after.

TERRY: The Ahern lock-up. April.

TESS: Seven nought. *(beat)* Liam.

ROSIE: *(from the stove end, before anyone can move on)* Nan put the plate in front of him. I'm the one taught him to be hungry for it.

ROSIE *(cont'd)*: And write my lie down. Something took hold of me. That's Con.

DANNY: And I said yes. Write that first.

No reply. Kieran writes it, and the night goes on.

KIERAN: *(not looking up from the roll)* Mum. Your first one. How old were you?

TESS: Fourteen.

KIERAN: You said it was a different week. *(beat)* Which week?

TESS: The week your grandad went. Nan stood me on that plate and everybody clapped, and I was hungry.

Kieran writes that too.

Names Kieran has never heard said at this table go into the book, and every line of it is a line the Register will read on Monday with a warrant, and they keep writing it anyway.

Terry names, Tess dates and seconds, Kieran writes, and Shaun, when they come to the river jobs, corrects Terry from the chair. By midnight Kieran has ruled a new graph and pinned it beside his old one on the wall: seconds along the bottom, ticks and crosses going up. The crosses crowd above the eight, exactly where the ferry timetable said they would, except now they have names under them, in pencil, on the certified roll.

### Scene 8. The weighbridge office, two in the morning.

Headlights swing across the blind. This time nobody tenses. The back door opens on MAGGIE AHERN with her tide books under her arm, and JOE behind her carrying the salvage steelyard wrapped in sacking, the long iron arm over his shoulder like a spade.

Joe loops its chains over the stove-flue bracket. The wooden board swings level. One at a time, in their socks, holding the chains, they stand on it, and Maggie slides the brass weight out the arm until it floats.

DANNY: Eighty-nine dead.

TERRY: Seventy-one four.

SHAUN: Fifty-two five.

RAY: Fifty-eight six.

ROSIE: Forty-six two. Same as Whitechapel had me.

Kieran steps up last. The board creaks.

KIERAN: Thirty-eight four.

He gets down, steps onto the little bridge, and tears his own ticket off the printer. 38.0. He writes them both, side by side — the first Marlow in twenty-five years with two numbers to his name, and he's twelve.

Then Terry. Nobody asks him to, and nobody stops him. He steps onto the little bridge for the first time all season and stands still on the steel. The printer chatters. Kieran tears off the ticket and hands it to him: 70.6. Terry reads it once, folds it small, and puts it in his breast pocket.

Then Tess does the thing she has never done. Down the roll, beside each turned name, in pencil, where the person can stand and read it, she writes the odds.

Terry finds his and reads it out loud, slow, a man reading his own menu.

TERRY: Here, a five-second hop: one in six, each go. There: five in six, better if I'm quick. A handshake: one in two, and a hand.

He holds up his yellow gloves.

TERRY: I cook with these.

He leans over and takes a die out of Kieran's board game box on the shelf. He holds it up where the table can see it.

TERRY: Six, I stay hungry. Anything else, I get my tea.

He rolls it once against the table, covers it with his palm, looks, and sweeps it into his breast pocket without showing a soul. Nobody asks.

TESS: Five seconds, six goes. Two in three you're eating here by Monday.

Peg takes the pencil off Kieran. Beside Terry's name, in the sign-writer's hand, she writes — and says it as she writes, at the table, out loud, the first time in five years:

PEG: Terry. Turned. Twenty twenty-one.

Terry goes very still. Peg doesn't look round at him.

TERRY: Ta, Peg. *(beat)* Six goes to the end of my count, at my age, and a warrant Monday. Or one go, and I land the right way round. Five year I've been the wrong hand at this table. Over there I'm not.

TESS: Rosie'll find the pull in three. You'll take longer.

A beat. Then, quiet, half under his breath, because it's what he does when there's nothing to say: "'Twas on a Tuesday morning..." — and he stops there, and fills the kettle.

Tess writes Ray's number on the roll. It sits there in pencil, and nobody in the room reads it out.

SHAUN: *(reading his own)* Nine and a half's my number. *(beat)* I've given it a leg. I'll wait till someone counts it for me.

Rosie reads hers — seven in ten, at her weight, at her seconds — and doesn't answer anybody. She goes and stands at the stove end, where Terry stands.

Tess's pencil hovers over Danny's name. There's no row of the table that reaches him. Two point two seconds, and nothing at two.

DANNY: Neither.

One word, and he puts his one good hand flat on the table, and that's the whole of Danny's arithmetic.

### Scene 9. The weighbridge office, four in the morning.

The lamp. The dregs of tea. On the wall, Kieran's two graphs. At the far end, the framed receipt Peg took down herself, lying flat on the table under her hands.

PEG: It came up wet, sand on it, and the creek dry when it should have been full. Your grandad said the tide had gone out and forgotten to tell the moon.

Maggie has her oldest tide book open. She has turned the pages back thirty-one years, to the week Peg names, and her finger sits on the Nobody column.

MAGGIE: Nobody low, that morning. To the minute. And your creek stood dry.

ROSIE: There's a sea under Grays. I swam up it.

Peg gets her baking tray out of the scullery. She heaps flour at one end of it — a white hill. Kieran props his school ruler level across the middle of the tray, a brass weight under each end, its near edge just biting the flank of the heap.

KIERAN: That's us. Our ground, dead level.

Peg runs water from the tap into the tray, slow, until the water stands well above the ruler's edge — and Rosie's hand stops her.

ROSIE: There. That deep over our lino. Eight metres. I counted it.

Then Maggie takes the tray, and tips it, slow, watching the water, the way she watches a river, letting it run off at the corner — and brings it level again, and the water lies dead along the ruler's top edge. Nobody low.

ROSIE: Anywhere else, you step off into their sea, or into their rock. There, it's a beach at our own height.

And on the flank of the flour hill, the wet edge comes down through the white and crosses the ruler's edge in exactly one place.

Peg sets the salt pot on it.

PEG: Our field. It never fell. It walked out of their sea onto our field.

Kieran writes it down. Tess reaches up, takes the pencil out from behind her ear — the one that has lived there for twenty years and written once, for Rosie — and hands it to him. He pockets it.

Maggie turns her tide book forward, page by page, to the coming Sunday, and turns it to face him.

KIERAN: *(reading it off the page)* Maggie's book says five fifty-two. Two hours.

Quiet, around the table. Then Ray, into it:

RAY: I'd take mine counted. Before Monday.

TESS: Grays, then. Me, Kieran, Maggie.

PEG: Mucking's mine.

---

## Act three: 05:52

### Scene 10. The weighbridge office, Sunday, 05:40.

Dark except the printer's green light and the stove's. Tess, Kieran, Ray, Maggie. Joe asleep in the van outside.

Ray buckles on Danny's belt, the strap knotted short for a thinner man — Shaun's knot. He steps onto the little bridge. The printer chatters: 58.0.

TESS: Eight point four, off this plate.

He steps onto Maggie's board under the flue. The brass weight slides; the arm floats.

MAGGIE: Fifty-eight six. Eight three, true.

TESS: A month back we'd have set you off at eight four and called it margin. *(beat)* She still lies. We know it now, that's all. So it's Maggie's arm we go by, and we send you for seven five.

RAY: The odd tenths?

TESS: They're yours. Nobody in this family crosses to the last of their count again.

RAY: And the odds.

TESS: About four in ten you come back straight. Six in ten you stay as you are.

RAY: Better than the Met ever gave me.

TESS: And if it don't, we go again before the warrant.

RAY: *(to Kieran)* Say the count for me, son.

KIERAN: Every tenth.

Ray flexes his fingers once, the way every Marlow does, and steps off the world.

He's gone. Hands still. Eyes on the empty plate.

### Scene 11. Mucking Marsh, Sunday, 05:20.

Fog off the estuary, thick as wool. A galvanized fence out of the fog, waist high, rolls of it, and a sign gone grey: MINISTRY OF DEFENCE — and a hut beyond with one dead sensor light on it. The marsh grass lies flat. Somewhere behind the fence the creek talks to itself, low, at the bottom of its bed.

Set into the mesh, incongruous, an old five-bar gate. Oak, silvered, a different century's work from the fence around it.

Peg takes her key ring out of her apron pocket. The long brass safe key, and the small padlock key on its loop of orange baler twine gone pale.

PEG: That's our gate. My dad hung it.

The small key turns in the orange padlock as if it were oiled yesterday. Thirty-one years, and nobody has changed the lock.

Peg steps through, onto the field. She stands still in the soaking grass with her hand on the gatepost, a woman on her own ground, on the wrong side of the law.

PEG: They never even put a sheep on it.

There's a standpipe on the hut wall, dripping. Rosie hangs her coat on the gate rail, and takes her clothes off in the grey light, folding them, quick and unembarrassed, a crosser packing a job down to weight. Peg works the soap over her back, her shoulders, her hair — backs to camera, and not one word between them. The water steams off her skin.

Then the breathing: the big slow divers' breaths, and one long out, and Rosie stands empty, holding nothing, her chest still.

Terry is next. He takes his clothes off like a man getting into a cold sea he has decided about, and Peg washes his back, and his hair, and neither of them speaks. A big soft man in the fog, shivering once, and done. Then, still in silence, she buckles his belt round him and clips the collar at his throat. His clothes go folded onto the rail. The yellow gloves go on top, folded together.

05:50.

### Scene 12. The black — Ray; the black — Rosie; the office; the field. Sunday, 05:52. The same minute.

**The black — Ray.** Sound drops to his heartbeat and his breath. The glow opens an arm's length round him, grey-gold, and in it the office stands open like a cutaway: at the end of the plate the green safe — its steel gone clear as water, and through it, lying flat on its tea towel, a woman at a window with a little brass balance, the light falling from the wrong side.

He doesn't move. He counts. Somewhere above the world Kieran's voice isn't, and Tess's isn't, and he counts anyway, the way he has counted everything for twenty-four years, in a book nobody read.

**The field.** Rosie, wet-haired, empty-lunged, rigged in her aunt's old belt with the gaffer-taped strap, stands in the grass inside the field. She flexes her fingers once, the way every Marlow does — and does not look for home.

**The black — Rosie.** The tugs come, the way Terry taught a boy at a stove. Home, strongest, someone calling you in for your tea. Below her, soft and wide, the feather bed of the Deep. Far off behind everything, faint, a kettle two doors down. And east, hard, like leaning on a lorry: the Hill.

She has followed the Deep once. She has never followed this one. She hunts for it, patient, small pulls at nothing — two seconds, three — and finds it, and goes along it, and steps out.

**Black.** Nothing on the screen at all. She takes one breath of their air and holds it, as she did in their sea. Her heart. Cold air moving on wet skin, once, across her shoulders. No other sound. Nothing else, for a minute.

**The black — Ray.** 6.3. Close on his fingertips: the fizz starting under the nails.

He waits it out. Hands still. Feet still. Home is one pull, and he never left it.

7.5. He steps.

**The office.** A gasp. The smell of the room: cold bacon, printer ink, dog.

Nobody asks him anything.

Kieran tears the ticket off the printer — the one the plate printed while he was gone — and pins it under the brass weight by Ray's odds.

KIERAN: Minus fifty-eight six. That's you true.

Kieran holds out Peg's meter, unopened, on the flat of his hand.

**The field.** Rosie lands on her hands and knees in the grass, gasping ours. Sand on her feet, on her shins, under her nails. Wet grass, fog, the gate, her family.

ROSIE: Still me.

DANNY: What did you see?

She doesn't answer. She gets up, walks off along the fence line into the fog, twenty yards, alone, and stops, and lets her last breath out — hers, only hers, into our air — and stands a moment breathing like a woman coming up from under a wave, and comes back.

Terry watches her feet the whole way. The sand. He nods once. That's the report.

ROSIE: Five and a bit, Uncle. Find it in four if you can.

TERRY: Yes. *(beat)* And I'll not bite back. Whatever I land as over there, I stay.

Danny holds the gate open. Peg stands on her field with the soap still in her hand. Nobody says goodbye. Nobody reaches for anything, and nobody refuses anything.

Terry flexes his fingers once, the way every Marlow does.

TERRY: One.

**The office.** Ray doesn't take the meter yet. He takes the pencil instead, and writes his crossing onto the roll — the hour, the seconds — and under it, his name.

**RAY AMBROSE**, flowing, easy as breathing, every letter running right to left.

**The field.**

TERRY: Two.

And steps off the world.

There was a man, and then there was the field.

**The office.** Faces fall. Maggie's too. Kieran's hand with the meter in it drops an inch.

Ray laughs, once, and it's the driest sound in the world.

RAY: Eleven year I've wrote back to front so you lot could read me. Give us a fortnight.

Then he takes the meter off the boy, pricks his own finger, matter-of-fact, and squeezes the bead onto the strip, and they wait, all four of them, in the green light.

The beep. **5.1.**

MAGGIE: *(steady, first to recover)* Write it under your name, then. Both numbers.

He does. Kieran is already at the stove, in Terry's spot, turning bacon with the tongs. Ray sits down at the long table under the chalk board that hangs bare as a washed window, and eats the sandwich slowly, making it last, and nobody hurries him, because it is Sunday morning and the world has changed shape.

**The field.** The fog comes through the open gate and past them, and the grass stands empty to the creek, and nobody looks at any instrument, because there isn't one, and nobody reaches for one, and that was the choice they made in the kitchen at four in the morning.

Rosie watches the empty grass a long time.

ROSIE: Seven in ten, it's Nan's fry-up for me. Three in ten, I'm still this. *(beat)* It's wrote down now. It'll keep. *(beat)* I'm staying as I am. Can't catch anything. There's wards that need that.

DANNY: I'm done making weight. I'll go to them.

Peg gathers his clothes off the rail. She folds the yellow gloves in on top, against his shirt, and carries the bundle out last, and the padlock clicks behind them, and the field is left to the Ministry and the fog and whatever is standing on the shore of it, one world over.

---

## Act four: What the rolls are

### Scene 13. The weighbridge office, Sunday afternoon.

Grey light. The lorries have been and gone. Ray went over the river at dinnertime to be in Victoria by nine, and the flat above the bookmaker's is his till Monday. Everyone else is here: Peg, Tess, Danny, Rosie, Shaun, Kieran, Maggie, Joe. The rolls lie the length of the table, written now, in pencil, in three hands — Terry's memory, Tess's seconds, Kieran's writing — and a fourth hand in square capitals down the far end, Maggie's tide books open beside it.

Tess stands at the head of the table, and she lays it out the only way she knows, which is objects.

TESS: Twenty-five year of tickets. Printed by the machine, on certified roll, dated to the day — crown stamp, Pinnock's certificate on the wall. *(beat)* Their walls have the same dates, and the seconds, to the hundred grams. The Register holds the day every turned soul in this country claimed. *(beat)* Lay the three together, and it's the ferry sheet with names on — and it's not our word for it. It's the banks' paper and the state's paper saying what Terry's memory says. Nobody can call it made up.

She picks up the 2002 roll, the one that came down from the loft, and lets it run through her hands.

TESS: And here's what only our paper's got. Every crosser that turned, and kept it hid, and kept on working — our rolls hold their year. Falling, week on week, like his. *(beat)* The Register can put a name to every one of them.

She turns over the one page that isn't theirs: Ray's strip, the month of Marrable Street going down.
